# Kitab al-Riyad: A Judgment Between the Two Reformers

## Introduction to the Book

This is a book of judgment. Its author, the Ismaili missionary Hamid al-Din Ahmad ibn 'Abd Allah al-Kirmani (اَلْكِرْمَانِيّ), known by the title Hujjat al-Iraqayn (حُجَّةُ الْعِرَاقَيْن), did not compose it to lay out a doctrine of his own. He composed it to weigh, within a garden already planted, the work of two who came before him — each a reformer in his turn. The first is al-Nasafi (اَلنَّسَفِيّ), author of a book called al-Islah; the second is Abu Yaqub al-Sijistani (أَبُو يَعْقُوبِ السِّجِسْتَانِي), author of al-Nusra. Between these two the author takes his stand, measuring what each advanced and rendering a verdict.

The setting is the world of Islam as the third century of the Hijra drew toward its close, a world stamped with philosophy and letters and unsettled by fresh calls to doctrine and disputes that branched in every direction. Against that background the book turns to the questions its two authors argued: the rank of the soul beside the intellect, the parts that compose the human being, the human person as a small image of the whole, decree and destiny, the origin of the sacred law in Adam and Noah, and the oneness of God together with the act of creation.

It is, then, a work of measured argument — a garden opened not to be planted anew, but to be judged.

## 1. The Age of Open Argument

In the name of what I here set down, I have named this book:

> كِتَابُ الرِّيَاضِ

the Book of the Garden. I have composed it, and I am the most eminent Ismaili دَاعِي, حَمِيدُ الدِّينِ أَحْمَدُ بْنُ عَبْدِ اللَّهِ الْكِرْمَانِيُّ (Hamid al-Din Ahmad ibn Abdallah al-Kirmani), called حُجَّةُ الْعِرَاقَيْنِ (Hujjat al-Iraqayn).

I do not open this garden in order to plant it anew. I open it in order to judge within it. My purpose is a ruling, a weighing between two who came before me, each a reformer in his turn:

> فِي الْحُكْمِ بَيْنَ صَاحِبَي الْإِصْلَاحِ وَالنُّصْرَةِ

a judgment between the two authors, the author of الإصلاح and the author of النصرة. Between these two, صَاحِبَي الْإِصْلَاحِ وَالنُّصْرَةِ, the companions of al-Islah and al-Nusra, I take my stand, and it is between them that I measure the matter and decide.

This, then, is the garden and its purpose: not a fresh field of my own making, but a place of judgment set between the two reformers, that what each of them advanced may be weighed and a verdict rendered.

As the third century of the Muhammadan Hijra drew toward its close, the world of Islam bore the stamp of learning, philosophy, and letters. Fresh calls to doctrine, disagreements branching in every direction, and a debate in theology that never settled at any boundary or stayed fixed in any one place — all of this moved through the cultural sphere and the community of scholars. To understand the origins of these disagreements in depth, one must weigh the opinions of the writers and scholars who lived in that distant age: men who left behind scholarly traces and new modes of expression suited to their time and to their grasp of philosophical questions, men who labored at interpreting texts and expounding verses. Only then can a reader arrive at a sound judgment that carries him toward the truth of the matter.

More often than not, these disagreements did not stay within the bounds of spoken discussion once raised in the salons and the centers of learning. They grew into long treatises that appeared on the pages of books and circulated as writings. Among the groups that contributed most to this activity were the Ismailis. And yet I find a strange thing: this group did not finish its argument in theology with the other groups before it entered upon a debate of an altogether new kind — a debate among its own callers.

In this lies clear proof of the freedom of mind that governed their lives — a release from the constraints that hold sway over conservative societies. Such societies draw a fixed boundary about themselves, bind themselves to a settled balance, and forbid their members to break the pattern or depart from an inherited tradition.

The first debate — or, to put it more exactly, the first scholarly discussion — to open new horizons for the Ismailis, carry them into an air of wide scholarly liberty, and make them a subject of conjecture throughout the world of Islam was the appearance of the book المحصول, The Harvest.

This book entered circulation at the beginning of the fourth century of the Hijra, the tenth of the Christian era. It is ascribed to the great caller of Khurasan, Muhammad ibn Ahmad al-Nasafi (محمد بن احمد النسفي), who was credited with turning the Samanid state in Transoxiana to Ismailism, and who was executed in the year 331 of the Hijra, 942 of the Christian era, as recorded in الفرق بين الفرق by Abd al-Qahir al-Baghdadi (عبدالقادر الْبَغْدَادِيُّ). It is, then, a very strange thing that I — حُجَّةُ الْعِرَاقَيْنِ (Hujjat al-Iraqayn), حميد الدين احمد الكرماني (Hamid al-Din Ahmad al-Kirmani (حَمِيد الدِّين أَحْمَد الْكِرْمَانِي)) — who took up this very subject and went deep into its examination, made no mention of the author of al-Mahsul. My discussion rested instead upon Abu Hatim al-Razi (أَبُو حَاتِم الرَّازِي) and Abu Yaqub al-Sijistani. There must be fundamental reasons that justify this silence; and it may be that the book al-Mahsul is ascribed to al-Nasafi in error.

Whatever the truth of the matter, the acclaim that al-Mahsul won among the thinkers of the Ismailis and the ranks of their scholars spurred Abu Hatim al-Razi, the chief caller in the north-west of Persia, to compose a book in which he examined what al-Mahsul had said. He named it الإصلاح. The opinions al-Razi (اَلرَّازِي) advanced plainly stirred a reaction in another great caller, Abu Yaqub al-Sijistani, who held them to run counter to the truth and to first principles. This is what moved him to write a book contesting al-Razi's views, which he called النصرة, in which he defended al-Mahsul and assailed Abu Hatim al-Razi without mercy. Al-Nusra appears to be among the earliest of al-Sijistani's works, as I myself note in this book.

Not long afterward, I surveyed the whole subject in full and composed this treatise, الرياض, a judgment set between the two reformers, صَاحِبَي الْإِصْلَاحِ وَالنُّصْرَةِ, the authors of al-Islah and al-Nusra, as I called them. I gave the opinions that appeared in all three books a thorough scholarly appraisal and set them beside قَانُونُ الدَّعْوَةِ الْهَادِيَةِ (the Law of the Guiding Mission) — that is, beside the philosophical and doctrinal positions officially held by the Fatimid court in my day. On the whole, I find myself preferring the theories of al-Razi over those of al-Sijistani (اَلسِّجِسْتَانِيُّ), and I take exception to the latter for failing to conform to the truth and for their leaning toward deviation. Beyond this, I observe that al-Razi passed over some of the statements set down in the book al-Mahsul, and so I bring them forward myself, as I note in the tenth chapter of this book.

It is worth remarking, too, upon the scholarly ideas these works contain. A body of philosophy such as this deserves to be studied, examined, and scrutinized with care; but, as ill fortune would have it, the two books al-Mahsul and al-Nusra have been lost, while al-Islah of al-Razi and this book, al-Riyad, are still preserved.

## 2. The Two Reformers

Al-Kirmani's *al-Riyad* stands among the important works that give voice to the philosophical theories of Ismailism in the fourth century of the Hijra. Before I take up the opinions, arguments, and statements it contains, I must set down a brief account of the three philosophers who took part in the discussion: al-Razi, al-Sijistani, and al-Kirmani.

**Al-Razi**

He is Abu Hatim Ahmad ibn Hamdan al-Laythi al-Warsanani al-Razi (ابو حاتم احمد بن حمدان الليفي الورسناني الرازي), reverently called in Ismaili circles our master Abu Hatim (أَبُو حَاتِم) the Exalted. He preached Ismailism in Rayy, then moved to Baghdad and settled there. His exact birth date is unknown. His work in the Ismaili دَعْوَة coincided with the imamate of Muhammad al-Mahdi Billah (مُحَمَّدٌ الْمَهْدِيُّ بِاللَّهِ) (Ubayd Allah (عُبَيْدُ اللّٰه)), founder of the Fatimid state in the Maghrib. He was known for the sweetness of his speech and his celebrated eloquence. He won al-Marwazi (الْمَرْوَزِيّ), the prince of Rayy — a committed Sunni — to Ismailism, and drew the princes of those regions into its fold. In time he became one of the landmarks of Islamic politics and religion, and one of the leading voices of the Islamic scholarly renaissance in the late third and early fourth centuries of the Hijra. He found his refuge in theology and philosophy. The orientalist Paul Kraus said of him: "He was a leading preacher of the Ismailis and played an important role in political affairs in Tabaristan (طَبَرِسْتَان) and Daylam (اَلدَّيْلَم), especially in Rayy and Isfahan, until the chief statesmen responded to him, such as Asfar ibn Shirawayh (أَسْفَرُ بْنُ شِيرَوَيْهِ), Mardawij (مَرْدَاوِيجْ) the commander, and others."

In truth, Abu Hatim al-Razi was a landmark of the intellectual renaissance not only within Ismailism but across the Islamic world. He helped carry philosophical teachings to every corner of the eastern lands and more broadly through Islamic civilization. Yet his enemies in Daylam did not spare him: he spent his final years in hiding. He died in 322 AH — by some historians' account at the age of sixty-two — after the Fatimid al-Qaim (الْقَائِم) had assumed charge of the Ismaili imamate in the Maghrib.

He was a contemporary of Abu Bakr Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (أَبُو بَكْرٍ مُحَمَّدُ بْنُ زَكَرِيَّا الرَّازِيُّ), the celebrated physician, whose philosophical views were well known: he departed from many of Aristotle's theories in natural philosophy and metaphysics alike, rejected any reconciliation of philosophy with religion, and held that philosophy alone was the path to reforming the individual and society. Between the two men there were fierce and repeated debates, attended by scholars and political figures. Abu Hatim recorded these exchanges in his book *Alam al-Nubuwwa*. He does not name his opponent there, calling him only the atheist, but the evidence is conclusive: that atheist was Abu Bakr al-Razi (أَبُو بَكْرٍ الرَّازِي).

The great philosopher al-Kirmani, Hujjat al-Iraqayn, records in his *al-Aqwal al-Dhahabiyya* that Abu Bakr al-Razi and Abu Hatim debated prophethood and legal ritual on the island of Rayy in the days of Mardawij, and in his presence. Abu Hatim, al-Kirmani says, succeeded in humbling Abu Bakr al-Razi and refuting his views in *Alam al-Nubuwwa* — in pages that overflow with force and with wonder, closing off every avenue of evasion and flight for the arrogant and the stubborn.

His most important work is *al-Zina*, a study of the Arabic letters — their derivatives, the rationale behind their forms, and the meanings of words. It is a major work on authentic Arabic vocabulary, native and borrowed alike, examining the linguistic framework within which the Quran was expressed, which the Muslims established as authoritative usage, and from which Islamic terminology drew its shape. The book runs to twelve hundred pages. He dedicated it to the Fatimid imam who succeeded Muhammad al-Mahdi Billah (Ubayd Allah). Among his other works are *Alam al-Nubuwwa*, *al-Islah*, *al-Jami*, and more.

**Al-Sijistani**

Abu Yaqub Ishaq al-Sijistani (أَبُو يَعْقُوب اِسْحَاق اَلسِّجِسْتَانِي) — also known as al-Sijzi (اَلسِّجْزِي) — stands at the forefront of those scholars who dedicated themselves to grounding cosmic philosophical principles in doctrinal and intellectual foundations: solid, scientific, and unshaken by whatever crises might come. He gave everything he had to spread those teachings to other lands. Late in his life, the conservative public accused him of blasphemy and atheism; he was eventually killed for his faith.

Al-Sijistani played a major role in philosophy and rendered the finest intellectual service in the third century of the Hijra. His mark is visible in his disciple Hamid al-Din al-Kirmani (حَمِيد الدِّين أَحْمَد الْكِرْمَانِي), Hujjat al-Iraqayn, who followed his method, championed his teachings, and drew from the current of his thought. Knowing that al-Kirmani formed his philosophy in al-Sijistani's school, we may place al-Sijistani in the first rank of Muslim scholars and among the world's celebrated philosophers.

He lived during the Age of Manifestation — the period of the Fatimid state's flourishing and its establishment as an Islamic power of civilizational, scientific, social, and political distinction. He lived among people who held a doctrine different from his own, and was compelled to take التَّقِيَّة as a cover, moving and preaching with great caution. This is why his life comes down to us in some obscurity, and no one can be faulted for our incomplete knowledge of it.

The same obscurity surrounds others. Of the great preacher al-Nasafi, al-Nakhshabi — author of *al-Mahsul* — only a little has reached us: what authors record of his efforts and his dealings with the Samanid ruler Nasr ibn Ahmad (نَصْرُ بْنُ أَحْمَدَ) in Transoxiana, until that Samanid embraced the Ismaili دَعْوَة. We know little of Abu Hatim al-Razi the linguist — author of *al-Zina*, the oldest work in the science of the Arabic language. Little, too, has come down about Hujjat al-Iraqayn Hamid al-Din al-Kirmani, despite the survival of most of his writings; had not al-Muayyad fil-Din Hibatallah al-Shirazi — the friend and disputant of Abu al-Ala al-Maarri (أَبُو الْعَلَاءِ الْمَعَرِّي) — written his biography with his own hand, we should have known nothing of him at all. Such is the obscurity that surrounds the great men of the Ismaili دَعْوَة — the حُجَّج and district preachers of Syria, Iran, Yemen, and beyond. Their lives lie in deep shadow; the books in which their biographies were preserved have been lost, and only fragments survive.

He is traced to Sijistan (سِجِسْتَان), a province in the south of Khurasan, from a Persian family said to descend from the hero Rustam (رُسْتَم). Some, however, hold that he was of Iraqi origin — that his grandfather came from Kufa and settled in Sijistan. Some researchers place his death in 331 AH, but this does not accord with the historical record. Al-Sijistani was al-Kirmani's teacher, and al-Kirmani lived until 411 AH. When, then, would al-Kirmani have received the philosophical sciences of the دَعْوَة from him? Al-Sijistani himself states in *al-Iftikhar* that he composed it in 360 AH; *al-Iftikhar* is in turn cited in al-Kirmani's *al-Riyad*. This places al-Sijistani as an active preacher in the province of Bukhara during the caliphate of the imam al-Muizz li-Din Allah al-Fatimi (الْمُعِزُّ لِدِينِ اللَّهِ الْفَاطِمِيُّ). He was thus a contemporary of the great preacher Jafar ibn Mansur al-Yamani (جَعْفَرُ بْنُ مَنْصُورٍ الْيَمَانِيّ) and of the learned jurist اَلْقَاضِي al-Numan ibn Muhammad ibn Hayyun al-Maghribi al-Tamimi (اَلنُّعْمَانُ بْنُ مُحَمَّدٍ بْنِ حَيُّونٍ اَلْمَغْرِبِيُّ اَلتَّمِيمِيُّ), chief judge of the Fatimid state — among the great authors and scholars of the دَعْوَة in that luminous golden age of learning. Nothing demonstrates al-Sijistani's scholarly stature more plainly than the writings he left behind — composed mostly in Arabic, with some in Persian. Al-Biruni cited them in his works; al-Baghdadi (الْبَغْدَادِيُّ) mentioned them in his *al-Farq bayn al-Firaq*.

As I have said, al-Sijistani left behind a rich body of scholarly work — reckoned among the most profound in philosophy — numbering more than thirty titles. His most celebrated works include *Ithbat al-Nubuwwat*, *Kashf al-Mahjub*, *Tuhfat al-Mustajibin*, and *al-Yanabi* — the last divided into forty springs (يَنَابِيع), a work rarely surpassed in depth. He appears to have written it for a particular audience: the preachers, those who held the highest ranks in the دَعْوَة, and those who had reached the summit of philosophical study. He divided it into forty springs so that each might carry the interpretation of one of the forty limits who constitute the dawa's highest council.

Nasir-i Khusraw (نَاصِرِي خُسْرَو), the great traveler and celebrated Persian poet, also mentions his works in *Zad al-Musafirin*: The Foundations of the دَعْوَة; The Interpretation of the Laws; The Iris of Blessings, or The Iris of Survival; The Brilliant Message; the *Kitab al-Iftikhar*; The Scales, divided into nineteen scales; The Ladder of Salvation; The Victory; The Hooks; Amusements of Sorrows; Secrets of the Return; Sermons on Ethics; The Stranger on the Meaning of the Elixir; Sociable of Hearts; Composition of Spirits; Security from Confusion; Closets of Evidence; The Proof; and others.

What follows is a summary of al-Sijistani's theological views as he expressed them across his writings. He holds that the Originator of all originated things is Ancient and Exalted — deeply rooted in the primacy of creation — and that the world of beings and originated things is contingent, brought into time. If it were otherwise, something prior to it would have had to bring it into being. And if the world were ancient — prior to the Creator — then His omnipotence could not be bound to something equally ancient, nor His existence made to rest upon what is nothing; such a world would itself require an originator.

He is exalted above the grasp of the attributes: He is not attained by sense, does not fall under sight, is not perceived by the eyes, is not characterized by any genus, does not occur to conjecture, is not seen by the eyes, is not described by the senses, is not grasped by analogy, and is not likened to men. He is free of any contrary that would oppose Him, any equal that would match Him, any likeness of anything; He is exalted above comparison with bounded things. Imagination is confounded when it tries to describe His omnipotence; understanding falls short before the nature of His sovereignty; eyes are blunted before the vastness of His greatness. He has no like and no likeness. He is without equal and without opposite, for an opposite is opposed only by a rival. He has pointed to His identity through His creation and works, and to His names through His prophets. The intellect has no ground on which to reach His height, and no room for comparison, since to liken the Originator to what He has originated is impossible.

He is the cause of every existent — the Originator of the originated, the Inventor of inventions, the cause of the being of all beings, the Lord of all things, their Creator, their Perfecter, and the One who brings each to its best state. He is too exalted for any thought to bound Him or any measure to encompass Him. He has no names, for names were coined for the things He has brought into being. He has no attributes, for attributes belong to essences. The letters of language cannot reach the pronouncing of His name; none of them may be applied to Him, for all are among the things He has invented. Every name He has originated He made a name for something He has originated. He is Ancient, before eternity, possessor of the source of firstness in due order — for from Him the first limit issued forth and the first existent overflowed. He is the Originator of the originated, the Cause of causes, the Maker of what is made, the Ever-Existing by His singleness and His self-sufficiency, possessor of the first act of bringing-forth. He made the first number the origin of numbers, just as He made the Intellect the origin of existents and the Articulator the origin of the world of religion. Beyond all this: He is attained by no attribute; He is not a body nor within a body; no reasoner can reason out His essence; no sense can sense Him; He is neither image nor matter; and no language holds words by which He may be expressed. He exists — yet it is not correct to class Him as an existent of the kind that came into being from Him. The inference to Him is drawn from the existence of other existents: there is no effect without a cause, and no existent except by that which necessitates its existence. Existents rest upon one another in their being, yet some of those upon which others depend are not themselves fixed in existence and are not existent.

Al-Sijistani then turns to the existents in their order and sequence. The Originator brought into being, at the first of creation, nothing but the Intellect, and within its essence He enclosed the forms of all originated things. The Intellect is called the Pen, because through the Pen the inscriptions of creation appear from the beginning. The Intellect is called the Throne — a seat for the one who rests upon it, by whose elevation his majesty is known over all beneath him. The Intellect is called the First — because the firstness from which created things appear, all that exists and all that is stamped upon it, is prior in receiving the effects of wisdom before any of the remaining limits, on account of its nearness to that source and its union with it, which is knowledge and command, both one in meaning. The act of the Intellect may precede its power — a distinction found in no other existent, for all the limits beneath it have their powers preceding their acts. This excellence belongs to the Intellect alone, so that by it the Intellect is complete and perfect. The Intellect is called the Decree (الْقَضَاء): through the Intellect the Soul grasps what is known and attains what is sought, and the Intellect may therefore be said to be the decree of God, mighty and majestic, among His creation. The Intellect is also called اَلْهَيُولَى — prime matter — meaning that the forms which pour forth receive their subsistence through the Intellect. And it is called the Sun, for by the Intellect the realities are seen.

The Soul is the second creation, flowing from the first. It is named Soul (نَفْس) because it breathes perpetually in pursuit of benefit; the continuity of that breathing is the subsistence of creation. The Soul is called the Tablet: what issues from the Intellect — the lights of wisdom — is inscribed upon the Soul, and from the Soul flows what proceeds from it, proportionate to its purity and refinement. The Soul is called the King — for the Soul is the possession of the Intellect and its handmaid. Through the Soul the excellence of the Intellect is made manifest, just as through the servant the excellence of the master appears. The Soul is called the Follower: it is the Soul that follows the Intellect in receiving the effects of the Word, and the Soul's power may follow the Intellect's act. The Soul is called Destiny (اَلْقَدَر): what reaches the Soul of the Intellect's benefits is such that measuring and delimiting can encompass it. The Soul is called the Image — for the Soul was formed from the essence of the Intellect and its radiance, and whenever it strives to reach the Intellect so as to take its rank, its light is effaced, just as the moon draws its light from the light of the sun and is effaced when they meet at one station. Together, the Intellect and the Soul are called, in a single term, the Two Principles.

These are some of al-Sijistani's philosophical views on theology, which I have sketched briefly alongside what I could recover of his life's story. They furnish, perhaps, compelling proof of his command of the sciences and his deep-rooted gift for setting down texts in an attractive order and an exquisite arrangement.

**Hamid al-Din Ahmad al-Kirmani**

Here is a remarkable scholarly figure whose life is shrouded in obscurity — a great philosopher who lived in a luminous age of learning, a noble preacher who set down upon the pages of thought the soundest researches and the deepest lines of inquiry, and left to the generations a body of works of which the least that can be said is that they are a precious treasure and an enduring heritage. The philosophers of the Islamic world regard him as the greatest scholar the Ismaili school produced.

## 3. The Arbiter

His book, *Rahat al-Aql* — the Rest of the Mind — stands among those rare works that scarcely find an equal among the writings of the philosophers, or among what remains of their legacy, in strength, durability, and depth. Demand for it was small, its circulation slow, and its readership confined to a special class of brilliant scholars and philosophers capable of mastering it.

The great preacher and Yemeni historian Idris Imad al-Din mentioned him in his book *Uyun al-Akhbar*, saying:

> ((هُوَ أَسَاسُ الدَّعْوَةِ الَّتِي عَلَيْهِ عِمَادُهَا وَبِهِ عَلَا وَاسْتَقَامَ مَنَارُهَا وَبِهِ اسْتَبَانَتِ الْمُشْكِلَاتُ وَانْفَرَجَتِ الْمُعْضِلَاتُ))

"He is the foundation of the دَعْوَة upon which it rests and by which it is raised up; through him its beacon was set straight, and by him problems were identified and dilemmas resolved." The Syrian Ismaili preacher Nur al-Din Ahmad (نُورُ الدِّينِ اَحْمَد) described him in his book *Fusul wa Akhbar*, Chapters and Reports, saying:

> ((لو ان الدعوة الاسماعيلية لم تنتج غير الكرماني لكفاها فخراً ومجداً ولكان ذلك كافياً))
>
> "If the Ismaili dawa had produced nothing but al-Kirmani, that alone would have been ample pride and glory, and that alone would have been enough."

His impact and stature came into their own during the reign of the Fatimid Caliph al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah (اَلْحَاكِمُ بِأَمْرِ اَللّٰه). His famous title was *Hujjat al-Iraqayn*, the حُجَّة of the Two Iraqs, signifying that he had charge of the dawa's cultural centers throughout Persia and Iraq. In Cairo his standing was that of a حُجَّة — one of the twelve hujjahs who managed the affairs of the دَعْوَة, the intellectual vanguard of the world. He was later appointed head of *Dar al-Hikma*, the House of Wisdom, in Cairo, the cultural institution that I may rightly call the first university established in the world.

He traveled to Cairo in a delegation in 408 AH, at the request of al-Sadiq al-Mamun (اَلصَّاْدِق اَلْمَأْمُوْن), then the foremost of the Fatimid state's preachers in the era of al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah. He arrived at a moment when religious battles were heating up, new movements were arising, and the market of innovation was flooded with calls aimed at exaggeration and deviation from the realities and foundations of the faith. He gave talks and lectures in *Dar al-Hikma* and produced a great many works of research and scholarship. The most famous of these is *Risalat al-Waiza*, the Preacher's Message, composed in reply to al-Hasan al-Farghani (الْحَسَنُ الْفَرْغَانِي), who held that al-Hakim was divine by the command of God. Among his other works are the epistles *al-Mabasim*, the Good Tidings, and *al-Masabih*, the Lamps, in which he proved the Imamate as a necessary cosmic reality through reasoned and compelling arguments — arguments richly furnished with Hebrew, Syriac, and Persian expressions drawn from the books of the prophets of the heavenly utterance. He then took up Platonic ideas as the basis for his research and deployed them with a skill no one before him had brought to bear. All of this served as a general call to consolidate the philosophical and intellectual system, to raise its level, and to erase every trace of doubt, controversy, suspicion, and dispute.

In these pages I will not attempt to introduce the philosopher al-Kirmani merely as one of the Ismaili preachers who played an important role in the field of thought during the reign of al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah, nor merely as one of the philosophers who rendered the Fatimid دَعْوَة clear intellectual service. I present him, rather, as a philosopher among the philosophers of the world — one who walked upon the stage of universal philosophy and brought all his powers to bear on founding a philosophical school upon a new intellectual foundation, drawn from the modern and innovative theories of his time and from the mental systems then current. This applies in particular to the theory of the unity of being, which the Second Teacher, al-Farabi (اَلْفَارَابِي), and the Chief Sheikh, Ibn Sina, hold. Here he expanded the theory in exposition and departed somewhat from the general curriculum laid down for the Ismaili preachers, whether those who came before him or those of his own age, who were obliged to follow a well-defined general framework whose limits they could not transgress or cross.

When I return to al-Kirmani's writings and read them with care, I find that he has resolved that contradiction and disharmony exist among the existents, that some attempt to erase the others, yet that these existents persist in spite of this dissonance and contradiction; for none of them is lost by the presence of an opposite, but all are preserved within existence. This accords closely with the theory of the Second Teacher on creation, in which he says:

> (( حفظ ادامة وجود الشيء الذي ليس وجوداً لذاته ادامة لا تتصل بشيء من العلل غير ذات المبدع ))

"The preservation of the continuance of the existence of a thing that does not possess, in itself, a permanence unrelated to any of the contingents, other than the Creator." And Ibn Sina, in the course of speaking of the possible and the necessary among the other existents, gives an account that clarifies the meaning of the continuity of existence among existents, saying:

> (( أَمَّا كَوْنُ الْعِلَلِ مُمْكِنَ الْوُجُودِ فِي نَفْسِهِ وَاجِبَ الْوُجُودِ لِغَيْرِهِ فَلَيْسَ يُنَاقِضُ كَوْنَهُ دَائِمَ الْوُجُودِ بِغَيْرِهِ ))

"As for the fact that the contingents can exist in themselves, while not being obligated by the existence of others, this does not contradict their being always present in others." What these three philosophers — the Second Teacher, the Chief Sheikh, and the حُجَّة of the Two Iraqs — arrive at in this regard comes close to what Descartes said in the modern era:

> (( اذ يوجد من الفعل الذي يحفظ الله به العالم وبين الفعل الذي خلقه به ))

"There is, between the act by which God preserves the world and the act by which He created it, no real difference." This is what is known in his philosophy as the theory of *continuous creation*.

Al-Kirmani shared common ground with the Muslim philosophers and was influenced by some of them, especially those who engaged with Greek philosophy. He is distinguished from them, however, by the fact that when he presented these philosophies he stamped them with the marks of novelty, wit, and innovation, and became the precursor of the modern European philosophers and of the ancient Oriental renewers. I may therefore say that al-Kirmani left behind books and produced ideas that deserve to be seen not only as Ismaili intellectual views, but as Islamic philosophical views of a high and universal order — examining the essence of things and their deeper intellectual dimensions in a steady and elaborated manner where genius and brilliance are evident.

Al-Kirmani spoke within the Neoplatonic systems and devoted all his efforts to applying some of their material in his attractive philosophical and rhetorical style. He also worked to establish both Prophethood and the Imamate from the philosophical and the religious sides alike. On this point I can say that he upheld the view that adherence to the outward Shariah leads to scriptural worship, while the further reach of philosophical interpretation and discussion of the boundaries (اَلْحُدُود) is reserved for those permitted ones who have attained a high level in philosophy and science. I find all these features when I look closely at *The Illuminating Message*, *The Lamps in Proving the Imamate*, *Alerting the Guide and the Guided*, and *Rahat al-Aql*, the Rest of the Mind. Beyond this, al-Kirmani is a major force in the development of the intellectual system and the expansion of theoretical discourse — adding new elements, broadening certain topics, laying down the basic rules of interpretation, and treating contradiction and similarity, especially on the subject of the Imamate, which had been the focus of unstable and shifting opinions and theories. For this reason his book defined the rules, origins, ranks, and status of the Imamate, and set new foundations, systems, and order for it.

Al-Kirmani made his mark across a range of fundamental questions: the doctrine of the call to existence; the negation of essence (اَلْاَيْسِيَّة) and substance (اَللَّيْسِيَّة); the absolute negation of the divine attributes; the doctrine of the call to التَّوْحِيد; the doctrine of the call concerning the two principles of origination; the elaboration of emanation; and the question of priority between the Imamate and the Prophethood — all treated in a purely rational and logical manner. He did not stop there. He gathered the two first principles, the Active Intellect and the Universal Soul, together with the system of the ten Platonic intellects upheld by the Second Teacher, al-Farabi. He placed the world of origination — the world of intellects and the spiritual world — alongside the corporeal celestial world (the world of stars and planets), the physical world (what lies below the lunar orbit), and the world of religion (the world of the boundaries of religion). He then drew geographical and astronomical diagrams for these, together with terrestrial and material plans that came out as works of remarkable artistry and creativity, revealing his ingenuity of expression and his eminence in philosophy.

On returning to the Rest of the Mind and reviewing what is stated in it, I see that it is full of formulations and arguments concerning the apostolic statements about God and the negation of the divine attributes. Among the positions he stated and supported are these: that no attribute attaches to Allah; that He is not a body nor in a body; that He neither rationalizes Himself nor senses Himself; that He is not in a form or in a substance; that He has no opposite and no likeness; that no language can express Him; and that He has no rank among existents. This indicates the breadth of his knowledge across the various sciences, especially the rational ones. On this the Mutazila and the theologians (الْمُتَكَلِّمَةُ) agree, and he agrees with Ibn Rushd. On the argument for the existence of God I see him agreeing with Descartes, even if they differ in the details and in the method of expression. Looking a little further, I see that he wove into his solutions and ideas some of the views of philosophers who preceded him and of his contemporaries: Plato, Aristotle, al-Kindi (اَلْكِنْدِي), al-Farabi, and Ibn Sina.

I note from al-Baqillani (اَلْبَاقِلَّانِي), al-Baghdadi, and al-Ghazali (اَلْغَزَالِي) that they ruled the Ismailis, by their denial of the attributes, to have fallen into invalidity (اَلتَّعْطِيل). But al-Kirmani answered this charge, saying:

> (( ان التعطيل الصريح انما يكون بأن يتوجه فعل حرف النفي ((لا)) نحو ((الْهُوِيَّة)) قَصْدًا، كَأَنَّ يُقَالَ مَثَلًا ((لَا هُو)) و ((لا اله)) وليس هذا مما تقول به الاسماعيلية ، اذ ان النفي عندهم هو نفي الصفات وحدها ، وتوجيه فعل حرف النفي ((لا)) انما ينصب عندهم على الصفات دون الهوية ))
>
> "Outright denial is to direct the action of the particle of negation, لا, no, against the identity itself, as in saying لا هو, He is not, or لا اله, there is no god. This is not what the Ismailis say. Their negation is the negation of the attributes alone; the direction of the negative particle لا for them is set upon the attributes, leaving the identity untouched."

However the matter stands, al-Kirmani gave special attention to this subject in his book, the Rest of the Mind. In this book I see him departing from all philosophers, writers, historians, and scholars: instead of dividing his book into the usual books, chapters, and sections that other men of thought and authors employ, he likens his book to a city encircled by seven walls. Whoever enters it must pass through the seven walls, and to pass through them he must face seven gates (الْمَشَارِع) branching off from each wall, except the seventh. As for the seventh and last wall encircling the city, it has fourteen gates. I will not enter here into an exposition of what al-Kirmani meant by his walls and his gates, for fear of going on at length, but this does not stop me from affirming his superiority in philosophy, the power of his thinking, and the depth of his grasp of the essence of things.

Al-Kirmani published a number of works, the most important of which include the following: *The Divine Treatise*, *The Treatise of the Systems*, *The Treatise of Light*, *The Illuminating Treatise*, *The Necessary Treatise*, *The Guiding Treatise*, *The Preacher's Treatise*, *The Sufficient Treatise*, *Alerting the Guide and the Guided*, *Wrists of the Guide*, *The Golden Sayings*, *Separating the Discourse and Distinguishing the Manifest Truth from Doubt*, *The Treatise of the Returner*, *The Treatise of the Index*, *Quantities and Facts*, *The Treatise on Tawhid in the Promised Land*, *The Crown of Intellects*, *The Balance of the Mind*, *The Book of Criticism and Obligation*, *Psychological Adequacy*, *The Book of Scales*, *The Baghdadi and Basran Sessions*, *al-Shaari's Treatise on the Elite*, *Rahat al-Aql*, and *The Treatise of the Week of the Role of the Cover*.

Finally, I must say that al-Kirmani is among the philosophers who carry great weight in our philosophical world, and that the study of his writings and his output is of great importance. It gives a clear picture of the impact of philosophy upon the history of thought for those interested in Oriental Studies and Islamic Philosophy.

## 4. A Map of the Garden

I turn now to the contents of the *Riyad* itself. Al-Kirmani opens the book by praising and glorifying God, as was the custom of scholars in those eras, before at last pointing to the Imam of his time, al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah of the Fatimids. He then says:

> لقد رأيت أبا حاتم الرازي قد قوَّم الاخطاء الموجودة في كتاب ((الْمَحْصُول)) فَجَاءَ أَبُو يَعْقُوبَ السِّجِسْتَانِيُّ فَدَافَعَ عَنْ مُؤَلِّفِ كِتَابٍ ((الْمَحْصُول)) مؤكداً صواب حجته وقوة نظريته وان الأمور التي ناقشها ليست من الجزئيات او الفروع الفلسفية الاسماعيلية حتى يمكننا التغاضي عنها وفي هذا كله ايذاء للعقيدة وتعطيلاً للمبادىء الاساسية وللأصول . ان أبا يعقوب كما يبدو قد دعم بعض النصوص مخالفاً أبا حاتم الرازي ومهاجماً اياه وفي بعض المواضع قد تكلم بما لا يمت إلى الموضوع بصلة مطلقاً، وَمَهْمَا يَكُنْ مِنْ أَمْرٍ فَقَدْ وَرَدَ فِي كِتَابٍ ((الْمَحْصُول)) بعض النصوص التي تعالج التوحيد فكان من الواجب على ابي حاتم ان يصلحها ويناقشها ولكنه لم يفعل ، بل فضل عليها تلك القصص الطويلة التي تعالج مواضيع اقل اهمية وهي البيانات التاريخية الواردة في التوراة ، وقد ملأ كتابه بها فتركها غير مصححة مهملاً اياها وبذلك أوجد نوعاً من الارتباك الفكري لاتباع الدعوة الهادية بتقديمه آراء مختلفة ومتباينة ، وَخَاصَّةً بَعْدَ اِنْكِبَابِهِمْ عَلَى قِرَاءَةِ كِتَابٍ ((الْمَحْصُول))، ولقد كان ذلك ذا تأثير على نقاوة فكرة مسالك التوحيد وأفساداً لأصول الشريعة ولترتيب الحدود .

I saw that Abu Hatim al-Razi had taken up the errors in the book *al-Mahsul*. Then Abu Yaqub al-Sijistani came and defended the author of *al-Mahsul*, insisting on the soundness of his argument and the strength of his theory. He held that the matters under discussion were not Ismaili philosophical side-issues or branches that could simply be set aside — to do so would be an affront to the creed and a disturbance of its basic principles and foundations. It seems that Abu Yaqub backed certain texts against Abu Hatim al-Razi, attacking him, and in some places speaking in ways that bore no relation to the topic at all. In any event, certain passages in *al-Mahsul* dealing with monotheism made it obligatory for Abu Hatim to correct and discuss them. He did not. Instead, he turned to long stories on subjects of lesser importance — the historical material from the Torah — and filled his book with them, leaving *al-Mahsul* uncorrected and neglected. By doing so he produced a kind of intellectual confusion among the followers of the quiet دَعْوَة, setting different and divergent opinions before them, especially once they had taken to reading *al-Mahsul*. This impaired the purity of the idea of the paths of monotheism and led to corruption of the principles of Sharia and of the arrangement of the boundaries (حُدُود).

And so I see him continue:

> ولهذا السبب قررت ان اورد هنا الاقوال الاصلية الواردة في كتاب ((الاصلاح)) مُصَحِّحًا بَعْضَ مَا جَاءَ فِي كِتَابٍ ((الْمَحْصُول)) وما ورد في اقوال ((النُّصْرَة)) الَّتِي تَدْعَمُ كِتَابَ ((الْمَحْصُول)) مقدماً رأيي وتعليقاتي عليها وغرضي من ذلك اظهار الاصول بالمعنى الصحيح والفصل بين الاثنين ، وعندما نصل الى هذا الحد يسهل اظهار الخطأ وتبقى التعاليم الصحيحة بمنجاة من الريب والشك وبعيدة عن كل تباين واضطراب .

For this reason I decided to quote here the original statements contained in the book *al-Islah* that correct some of what is mentioned in *al-Mahsul*, along with what has been mentioned in the statements of *al-Nusra* that support *al-Mahsul*, presenting my own opinion and comments. My aim is to display the principles in their correct sense and to separate the two positions. Once we have reached this point it becomes easy to expose the error, so that the correct teachings remain free of doubt and ambiguity, and far removed from every disparity and confusion.

Al-Kirmani divided his *Riyad* into ten chapters, each subdivided into several sections. With his literary and scholarly deftness, the strength of his exposition, and his learning, he selected what he saw as important and fundamental from the books *al-Islah* and *al-Nusra* and recast it in his own style — clear and concise — a kind of artistry wholly different from al-Razi's.

The first chapter is divided into thirty-eight sections and is devoted to the perfection of the soul. The author of *al-Islah* says:

> ان النفس تامة وهي مصدر تام كامل جاءت من الكمال لان العقل هو الكمال .

The soul is perfect, a perfect source issuing from perfection itself, because the intellect is perfection.

The author of *al-Nusra* contradicts this. He holds that the author of *al-Islah* fails to grasp that "the perfect" stands higher and is more complete than "perfection," because perfection is an attribute of the perfect and is therefore predicated of it. The thing that bears the attribute is accordingly higher, and the attribute takes its name from that thing. The perfect is what exists; when perfection exists, it becomes the follower, not the followed.

Here the debate between truth and fancy sharpens, and the author of the *Riyad* exposes the logical error. The author of *al-Islah* did not mean perfection in general but the highest perfection — namely the intellect, the *'aql* — which is the cause of the existence of the perfect soul that has come to be characterized by it. The effect cannot stand above the cause. This view leads us to hold that al-Sijistani's attack on al-Razi was out of place and incorrect.

I then take up al-Razi's opinion. He says that although the soul is perfect, its actions are not, since those actions depend on time. The soul is the emanation of the intellect, and the intellect is a being that in itself comprises origination, time, and perfection. The soul, unlike the intellect, is emanated within time and depends on the supply that comes from the intellect. This dependence does not impair the soul's substance or render it base, just as the intellect's dependence on the divine will does not make it inferior. The substance is not the inferior of matter on account of this perfection or imperfection, which are born of the soul and are not its substance. It may be said that everything in the first chapter of the *Riyad* — namely these two quotations drawn from *al-Islah* — al-Kirmani presents while strongly criticizing the opinions of *al-Nusra*, holding that al-Sijistani misunderstood these theories. He likens him to a newcomer who entered the world of the ideals with no goal but material vanity.

> قال صاحب ((النُّصْرَة)) مورداً قول امباذقليس على سبيل الاحتجاج به انما صارت اما لأنه لم يخضع لشيء إلا الباري عز وجل وصارت النفس ناقصة لانها خضعت للطبيعة نقول : ان الفلاسفة المتقدمين منهم وان كانوا فضلاء في زمانهم فقد استمر عليهم سلطان الخطأ في كثير مما تكلموا عليه من العقليات ، ولو كانوا في زماننا ورأوا ينابيع البركات كيف هي فائضة من بيت الوحي بحقائق الأمور على ما هي عليه من الدعوة العلوية لكانوا مع اجتهادهم لانفسهم ينصفون انفسهم ويعبرون عن كثير من اعتقاداتهم وفيما غاب عن الحواس يقفون . ولكانوا لآثار من جعل الله له نوراً في غوامض الامور يقنعون . فانهم في ازمانهم اتبعوا آراءهم وعقولهم فيما نحوه فصار كل منهم يذهب في اعتقاده الى ما تدل عليه مصنفاته على ما هو عليه أهل الظاهر في الملة الحنيفية حين اعتمدوا على عقولهم في سلوك مسالك الديانة فاختلفت آراؤهم وبين أولئك تفاضل فمنهم من صوابه اكثر ومنهم من خطأه اكثر والمعول على ما يشهد به ميزان الديانة الذي هو الآفاق والانفس . وفيما تقدم من الكلام على ذلك والبيان لأن اعتقاد الامر فيه على ما شرحه باطلاً غنياً عن التكلف والتطويل فما المعنى فيما أورده امباذقليس الا ما أورده صاحب النصرة في احتجاجه، وقول امباذقليس انصب للطعن ونسأل الله ان ينور عقولنا ويختم بالسعادة أمورنا والخيرات والاعمال الصالحات يوفقنا بمنه ولطفه وكرمه .

The author of *al-Nusra* cited the saying of Empedocles in protest: the intellect became what it is only because it was not subject to anything but the Almighty, and the soul became deficient because it was subject to nature. We say: the earlier philosophers, even if they were virtuous in their time, remained under the sway of error in many of the matters they addressed by their own reason. Had they lived in our time and seen the springs of grace overflowing from the House of Revelation with the true nature of things — the 'Alawi دَعْوَة — they would have done themselves justice in their diligence, would have expressed many of their beliefs more truly, and would have halted before what lies beyond the reach of the senses. They would have been content to follow the traces of those for whom Allah set a light in the mysteries of things. In their own time, however, they followed their own opinions and their own minds, each going in his belief in the direction his reason pointed him — resting on the same ground as the Zahiris of the Hanafi sect when they relied on their own minds in walking the paths of religion. Their opinions diverged, and among them there was a distinction: some closer to right, others closer to wrong. The reliance is on what the balance of religion testifies to, namely the horizons and the souls. Since what has been said makes it clear that this belief, as explained, is false and requires no further elaboration, what does Empedocles mean beyond what the author of *al-Nusra* put forward in his protest? The saying of Empedocles amounts to nothing but censure. We ask God to enlighten our minds, to seal our affairs with happiness, and to grant us success in good works through His kindness and generosity.

The second chapter is divided into nine sections and is devoted to the relationship between the intellect and the soul. Al-Sijistani objects to al-Razi for raising the topic of motion, repose, and stillness — the central topic that so often caused difficulty for the ancient philosophers. Here motion and stillness are taken as two effects united in "the First" and the Second. This supports what Abu Hatim al-Razi said: the First was made originative and has become attached to and responsible for origination, since the truth of His station is a duality — the originator and the originated — and these two effects are present in "the First." When the Second was brought forth from the First, the bringing-forth itself was dualized: the same emanation and the same emanated. The two effects are thus linked to the two basic principles. Motion is the benefaction (*fayd*) that the First releases upon the Second and which acts upon all assets and entities, while stillness is the acceptance of the First's command throughout all the basic entities.

Al-Razi says:

> أنا لا اقول ان الحركة والسكون هما متولدان من النفس بفعل القوة المستفادة من العقل ولكني اؤكد بان الاثنين هما أثران من النفس متولدان بفعل القوة المتولدة من العقل في الهيولى والصورة ولهذا السبب فان الهيولى والصورة اصبحتا اساس هذا العالم .

I do not say that motion and stillness are generated from the soul by a power received from the intellect. Rather, I maintain that both are two effects of the soul, generated by the power produced by the intellect within the اَلْهَيُولَى (matter) and the form. For this reason, matter and form have become the basis of this world.

Al-Sijistani, however, says in his book *al-Nusra*:

> ان هذا الكلام من نوع الهذيان لان الحركة تتولد من قصد خاص ((لِطَلَبِهَا)) كما تهدف حركة الاجسام المادية لتغطية فراغ حركة اللهب الصاعدة الى ما فوق أو حركة الارض لتحت وهكذا دواليك .

This argument is delirious, because motion is generated from a specific intention — to seek something — as the motion of physical bodies is aimed at filling a void, whether through the upward movement of flame or the downward movement of earth, and so on.

From this I conclude that al-Razi was correct while al-Sijistani was wrong.

The author of *al-Islah* says:

> ونقول : ان الحركة والسكون هما أثران متحدان بالأول والثاني لان الاول لما ابدع اتحد بالابداع فكان لذلك الاتحاد اثرين فيه بالقوة . وانما قلنا أثران لان الابداع والمبدع اثنان واحدهما الابداع والآخر ذات المبدع والابداع والمبدع فان كانا اثنين فهما ايس واحد وهو الكل وهذان الآثران هما في الاول بالقوة ثم انبعث الثاني عن الاول فكان من ذلك اَلْاِنْبِعَاثُ اثرين فيه بالفعل احدهما ذات اَلْاِنْبِعَاثُ والآخر ذات المنبعث فالأصلان متحدان بالأمر وأحد الاثرين هو الحركة وهو أعلى من الآخر الذي هو السكون كما ان احد الاصلين الذي هو الاول أعلى من الآخر .

We hold that motion and stillness are two effects united in the First and the Second. For when the First was creative it united with creativity, and by that union two effects arose in it in potency. We called them two effects because creativity and the creator are two — one being the creativity, the other the essence of the creator. Yet creativity and the creator, though two, are a single essence, which is the All. These two effects belong to the First in potency. Then the Second was brought forth from the First, and from that emanation two actual effects arose in it: one the essence of the emanation, the other the essence of the emanated. The two origins are united in the command. One of the two effects, motion, is higher than the other, stillness, just as one of the two origins — the First — is higher than the other.

The third chapter treats the question whether the soul (نَفْس) and the spirit (*al-ruh*) are one and the same. It is divided into six sections and opens with phrases drawn from *al-Nusra*, set among the texts cited from *al-Islah* and arranged in a way that clarifies their original meaning. I note here that al-Sijistani criticizes al-Razi for something al-Razi never said.

> وَقَالَ صَاحِبُ ((الاصلاح)) عَلَى مَا أَثْبَتَهُ صَاحِبُ ((النُّصْرَة)) : ولا نقول ان الطبيعة والهيولى الظلمانية متولدة من الثاني لتؤكد الثاني من الاول ، لِأَنَّ ذَٰلِكَ الْعَالَمَ عَالَمُ الشَّرَفِ وَالْفَضِيلَةِ وَهَٰذَا الْعَالَمُ هُوَ عَالَمُ الظُّلْمَةِ وَالْكُدُورَةِ ، والثاني وان كان متولداً من الاول فانه شبيه به ، وَلَا يُشْبِهُ حَالُهُ حَالَ الْهَيُولَى ، وَقَالَ صَاحِبُ ((النُّصْرَة)) : ان هذا غلط محض لانه لو كان ذلك العالم نورانياً لا ظلمة فيه وهذا العالم ظلمانياً لا نور فيه لم يقدر ان يقع بين هذا .

The author of *al-Islah*, in reply to what the author of *al-Nusra* had advanced, said: we do not say that nature and the dark matter are born from the Second, in order to confirm that the Second derives from the First, because that higher world is the world of honor and virtue, while this world is the world of darkness and gloom. The Second, though born of the First, resembles Him, and its case is unlike the case of the اَلْهَيُولَى. The author of *al-Nusra* replies: this is a pure mistake, for if that world were luminous with no darkness in it, and this world dark with no light in it, no resemblance could fall between them.

The fourth chapter, in eight sections, treats whether souls are parts of the First Truths. I note that al-Sijistani alters the meaning of what is stated in *al-Islah* by rearranging its phrases. On the subject of partial souls I agree with him, granting that the aggregate of these souls does not constitute the universal soul. Al-Sijistani then returns to speak of this world, alluding to the complete Truths, and says that the human soul belongs to the region between the world of nature and the world of rebirth. It is the compound, known by the natural force, and the soul may be raised to the highest world by practicing piety and then by uniting with the Second. He does not stop there. He says:

> ان العقل هو اَلْاِنْبِعَاثُ الاول بينما النفوس الناطقة هي اَلْاِنْبِعَاثُ الثاني وما جرى في اَلْاِنْبِعَاثُ الاول أيضاً انبعث في الثاني .

The intellect is the First Emanation, while the speaking souls are the Second Emanation, and whatever occurred in the first emanation is emanated in the second as well.

I take issue with this view and refer the reader to my own writings, especially the epistles *al-Waiza* and *al-Mudia*, in which I say:

> ان العقل ليس إلا ذات الكلمة والكلمة ليست إلا ذات العقل يصحح ذلك قول مولانا الامام المعز لدين الله صلوات الله عليه في كتاب ((تَأْوِيلُ الشَّرِيعَةِ)) حيث يتكلم عن العقليات ويشير بان الكلمة عبارة عن العقل وان هذه الاسماء يستحقها بالاضافة وسبق من شرحنا وكلامنا على المبدع والعقل والكلمة وما يقال على عالم الابداع واَلْاِنْبِعَاثُ الاول في الرسالة ((الْمُضِيئَة)) وَغَيْرُهَا مِنَ الرَّسَائِلِ مَا يَكْفِي الْبَاحِثَ .

The intellect is nothing but the essence of the Word, and the Word is nothing but the essence of the intellect. This is confirmed by the saying of our Master, Imam al-Muizz li-Din Allah (عليه السلام), in the book *Tawil al-Sharia*, where he speaks of the intellects and indicates that the Word denotes the intellect, and that these names are properly applied to it by attribution. What we have already set out — concerning the Originator, the intellect, the Word, and what is said of the world of origination and the First Emanation — in the epistle *al-Mudia* and other epistles is sufficient for the researcher.

Nor do I stop here. I bring forward the words of al-Razi recorded in *al-Islah*, where he says:

> ان الحركة والسكون هما كروح الهيولى والصورة وهذين الاخيرين كجسد الاثنين الاولين وذلك لان الروح ألطف من الجسد والجسد اكثف من الروح ، وَالْحَرَكَةُ وَالسُّكُونُ هُمَا أَلْطَفُ مِنَ الْهَيُولَى وَالصُّورَةِ .

Motion and stillness are to matter and form as the spirit is to the body, and these last two are as the body of the first two, because the spirit is subtler than the body and the body is denser than the spirit. Motion and stillness are subtler than matter and form.

Al-Sijistani appears to reject this analysis, favoring instead the theory of the author of *al-Mahsul*. I hold that both men erred — al-Razi especially so on the question of motion. Motion is the action of the mover, which we call the soul, and stillness is the cessation of the mover's action. If the mover is the soul, it cannot resemble motion, which is its own action; and the same applies to stillness. Furthermore, motion and stillness cannot be combined into a single thing, because they alternate as opposites, whereas matter and form are inseparable and together form one thing. I have treated this topic fully in the book *Rahat al-Aql*.

The fifth chapter, in seven sections, takes up the theme that the human being is the fruit of the material world. Al-Razi says this, and I find him extending the claim to the whole world — its foundation and its roots. Matter is likewise the basis of the substrate formed within it by motion, stillness, and certain other factors that lead to the formation of the different souls. Al-Sijistani calls this a philosophy of *al-dahr*, one that does not acknowledge the existence of the soul after its separation from the body.

Earlier I traced how the human being stands as the fruit of the material world. I turn now to the shape of the book itself, chamber by chamber, for al-Riyad is a garden laid out in ordered beds, and it is worth walking its paths in sequence.

One school yet remains — one that al-Kirmani weighs without reaching a fully decisive answer. This group is held to follow the school of Democritus and Epicurus, as further pursued by Lucretius. Al-Kirmani says that both schools are right on one side and wrong on another. Man, they hold, is the result of the interaction of natural-planetary and physical action, and man is the purpose of the Second and the purpose of the work of nature. The meaning is this: man is created and divided into two parts, one part belonging to lifeless nature and another part belonging to God, and is therefore the highest of creatures.

We say: how can this be correct, when order and arrangement come only from the Intellect? How can it be right that universals are made preferable only by the Intellect, while the soul is unaffected by its effects, standing on a level with the animals? It is for this reason that God has lifted the pen from the soul until the effects of the Intellect take hold within it. We have said above that the matter lies with the agents, not with totalities: not every agent seeks, through its action, to achieve a goal or purpose decreed for it. By the same token, the First Intellect itself is not a compound, and so it has order and arrangement, though the Intellect perceives only true perception.

The sixth chapter, in six sub-chapters, rehearses the theme of motion, stillness, and the image. It maintains that what is reported in The Harvest*, and what Abu Hatim al-Razi and al-Sijistani transmitted on this question, is incorrect, and it refers the reader to his own book The Lamps*.

The author of *al-Nusra* then proceeds to define body and soul. He defines the body by its acceptance of the three dimensions, and defines the soul as the similarity of parts, placing the soul in the same class as water, fire, and air, each of which is similar in its parts. From this premise it would appear impossible to say that the soul is not embodied. The soul (نَفْس) and the spirit (al-ruh) are, however, synonymous names for one thing, and that thing is not embodied. Were it a body, it would necessarily possess the nine qualities and accidents that the body requires and would be apprehensible by a sense. Nor would the imagination remain fixed within it as to the nine accidents, or any one of them; and when different physical norms hold within it, it cannot be a body. Our well-known book *al-Masabih* contains what fully addresses this.

The seventh chapter, divided into seven sub-chapters, deals with the classification of the composite materials of the world. In truth it is devoted entirely to explaining the status of the human being — who forms a class of his own — and his relationship to all else. For this reason al-Razi says that man is a plant that sleeps upon the substances of the world. Al-Sijistani agrees with him on one point but disagrees on another: the human body disintegrates and dissolves into such substances, and al-Razi therefore classes the human being and likens him to an animal because he possesses the senses and attributes. Al-Sijistani replies that this sensory capacity belongs to the growing vegetative soul. Al-Razi argues that the speaking human himself constitutes a point of difference from the animal, while al-Sijistani counters that the animal too possesses the senses. When al-Kirmani weighs the two positions, he rules that al-Razi is closer to the truth than al-Sijistani. He then says that the resemblance is not only between man and the material world but also between man and the spiritual world, the one who is united in himself being such that everything preceded him in the scale of existence, and he displays the analogies between himself and everything in both the material and the spiritual worlds.

In the world there are different motions, and in the human being there are likewise six motions corresponding to the six. In the world there is a mover that moves it, and in the human being likewise there is a mover that moves him — namely the soul. In the world there are the four investigations, that is, the elements, and in the human being likewise there are the four natures. In the world there are rivers, valleys of different tastes, mountains, and minerals, and in the human being likewise — as the author of *al-Islah* has shown — there are bones, cartilage, flesh, blood, and what flows from him and issues out of him. In the world there are things that grow and increase without sensation, and in the human being likewise there are things that grow and increase without sensation, such as hair and nails; and his body itself grows and increases from his youth to his old age. In the world there is the sentient animal as well, and in the human being there are the sentient faculties. The whole world is structures and compositions, complete in what exists. From all this comes the analogy with the world, though what I have mentioned is only a summary, which our writers detail under the title *Peace of Mind*.

The eighth chapter is divided into twenty-four sub-chapters, and its subject is الْقَضَاء (judgment) and اَلْقَدَر (destiny). These two expressions appear in the Holy Quran and point to distinct meanings, though both concern judgment. This topic had its impact on the long scholarly debate that took place in the early Islamic era and never reached a decisive conclusion. Thousands of works in Islamic theology have been written on it, though in truth it is a mistaken and time-wasting matter to revisit.

Al-Razi, the author of *al-Islah*, says: the statement in *al-Mahsul* that الْقَضَاء corresponds to the First and the Preceding, and اَلْقَدَر to the Second — that is, the next — is entirely wrong, because destiny precedes judgment just as the First precedes the Second. The meaning of اَلْقَدَر is estimation: that He made the thing possible. The meaning of الْقَضَاء is detail, the separation of the thing or its cutting; and the Second, that is, the manifest الْقَضَاء, cannot be set before the First. Al-Razi cites several verses from the Holy Quran in support of his interpretation and likens destiny to the cloth that takes shape in the tailor's imagination before he cuts it; once he has put it into its form — that is, judged it — it is no longer possible to change it.

Al-Sijistani objects to this explanation in defense of his fundamental theory, which agrees with what is stated in *al-Mahsul*. He says that الْقَضَاء does not mean detail, although اَلْقَدَر means estimation: the first describes and obliges, while the second — which is the judgment — is a void and an end. He adds that the tailor analogy does not fit the case, because الْقَضَاء is the idea of the garment that results from the art of tailoring, which is the sewing, while the second rests upon the soul as the estimation, and so it became destiny. Then al-Kirmani enters and examines the ideas and positions. He says: if الْقَضَاء corresponds to the First, and the First to the Preceding, and the Following to the Second, then we must admit that the Speaker is suited to the Following, as shown in *al-Mahsul*. Although the Speaker is responsible for defining the Sharia, it is wrong to combine the Speaker and the Following, because the Speaker is concerned with the material world while the Following — which is the Foundation — is concerned with the spiritual world. The Speaker and the Foundation stand in a likeness like that of the sun and the moon. Here a point deserves attention: some preachers inclined to place the rank of the Foundation above that of the Speaker, holding that spiritual worship is higher than legal and natural worship.

The author of *al-Nusra* reports a saying from the scholars: when God Almighty created the Intellect, He emptied the two worlds into it, so that it might be the sum of the images of the two worlds. We say: the Intellect that is the sum of the images of the two worlds is not the First Creative Intellect, nor the Second Intellect, which is the First Emanator. Rather it is the Intellect that ascends from the world of nature through acquisition — in work and in knowledge — until it parallels the First Emission, whereupon a Second Emission emerged, and thus it became a collection of the images of the worlds despite their multitude, capable of receiving the imprint of the images of created things.

The ninth chapter al-Kirmani divides into thirty-three sub-chapters. It treats the basis of the principle and the law of Adam, and the laws that came after him, and then proceeds further. In this connection he speaks of the Great Resurrection, saying that it will come when the doors of teaching are closed, the call that has been perfected ceases, and the الْقَائِم sets aside the ranks in the administration of the دَعْوَة; those ranks are then no longer needed, for a new creation has come.

This chapter also contains sub-chapters devoted to the question of whether Adam — named in the Holy Quran as one of God's interlocutors conveying revelation to humanity, and as the Speaker, or in Ismaili idiom the one who holds the role of the age — truly preached a new law, and to whom and how. Here I find no attachment to purely philosophical conjecture, only an effort to gather and explain the few symbols and signs scattered through the Scriptures, and to bring those often contradictory references together into a convincing theory.

The Christians turned the Adam of the Old Testament — the simple man named in the Torah of the Bible — into a towering figure unlike any other. Islam, alongside a great variety of other views, has inherited this new story of Adam; and of the various groups the Ismailis came closer to the truth than the rest in defining his role, because they considered him to stand at the very beginning of the world, it being enough that the angels were commanded to prostrate to him in honor. Within the Ismaili system it is acknowledged that the seven speaker-prophets are the first and not the last, so Adam, the first, and the others who follow differ only slightly in their work and their duties: Adam did not bring the closing decree, and the الْقَائِم will not come with a new law, which is why Muhammad is taken as the seal of the apostles and his law as the last.

Al-Kirmani mentions the Great Resurrection because at that time the call will have been completed, the activity of the حُدُود — the ranks of the call — will have ceased, the task of education will have ended, and a new creation will have begun. This idea seems to reverse what is expected from the Ismaili call when it reaches its summit, when the whole world becomes united under their authority and follows a single religious doctrine — namely the true Islamic law that they preach.

The author of *al-Nusra*, after his interpretation of God's words in the story of the son of Noah, comments on the verse:

> سَـَٔاوِىٓ إِلَىٰ جَبَلٍۢ يَعْصِمُنِى مِنَ ٱلْمَآءِ
>
> "I will take refuge on a mountain that will protect me from the water."

Here, he says, the son of Noah shows his meaning by his words:

> سألتجىء الى لاحق
>
> "I will seek refuge in a subordinate."

Then Noah replies:

> لَا عَاصِمَ ٱلْيَوْمَ مِنْ أَمْرِ ٱللَّهِ
>
> "There is no protector today from the command of God."

The matter, in other words, could be received only through those who bear it. The son of Noah did not know the rank of revelation — not even when it came through a subordinate bearer. Had Adam held a revelation for his son, the son would have recognized this.

The tenth chapter, which closes the book, is a critique of all the passages in *al-Mahsul* that Abu Hatim al-Razi had left uncorrected. There are roughly nine such passages across the fifteen sub-chapters of this section, and I have considered it necessary to translate the essential passages of *al-Mahsul* to give some sense of its style. Al-Kirmani's response to the second passage illustrates his method well. He says: when the author of *al-Mahsul* first undertook the propagation of the call and began explaining the foundations and rules of the دَعْوَة, he opened new directions by writing a number of books for those within it; and the majority accepted his judgment on *al-Mahsul* despite the defense offered by the author of *al-Islah*. This acceptance served as evidence of both the error and the complexity of the subject. It is unfortunate to observe that the truth is the opposite of what they saw and imagined, for al-Razi's corrections show that he did not challenge the value of *al-Mahsul* or its author — neither by exposing faults nor by issuing judgments — because al-Razi was among those men particularly devoted to repairing and elevating religious knowledge, explaining the truth of the faith, and affirming the duties of man toward God.

From the opinions of al-Kirmani preserved in his books, I judge him to be a great author, a writer formed in sound manners and trained in right principles. From one of his epistles I can see that he was capable of making an important criticism and setting it down with restraint, where another man would have resorted to insults and abuse. Here are some of the passages he corrects:

1. The author of *al-Islah* says, on the subject of monotheism, that God is the Creator of things and of other-than-things, of the intelligible, the intellectual, and the rational — meaning that such materials or topics may fall under the rule of such elements, or may not. Al-Kirmani replies that this contradicts the theory that reason (al-aql) is the foundation of creation, behind which there is no other deity, and that nothing can precede the intellect in existence or stand alongside it in existence, for this theory entails an association (الشِّرْك) and contradicts the law of monotheism. And to speak of creating things and other-than-things, material and non-material — unless one has confused himself and imagined the illusion that there is no world beyond this one and nothing that negates creation — how then is it possible to say that God is the creative Creator and at the same time deny this?

2. In the fourth sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says: the Creator made things from nothing alone, and there is nothing with Him, and He has no end; and if we said only "He and nothing," we would have denied things and other-than-things and made both ourselves and Him creative. We have said that His Self, His identity, whatever idea it may be, is a simple form (sura), and that He made everything continuous and non-continuous, bringing it into this existence by a caused, limited, and finite effect, because nothing comes after the thing, and because the negation of a name is itself a name that comes into being once that name exists, and because the word "no" is not an idea but is itself one of the ideas — a negation of every idea; and this is part of الشِّرْك and a refutation of the theory of monotheism. Al-Kirmani refers the student to his own thesis *al-Rawda* and to the book *Tafsir al-Sharia* by the Fatimid Imam al-Muizz li-Din Allah.

3. In the eighth sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says, in the section investigating the First Creator, that God Almighty created the world all at once — meaning that He created the intellect with a single utterance, and by His power and design displayed within it the images of existing things, the ideas of the world, and everything it contains, without anything else having priority over it, so that the intellect has seniority. Al-Kirmani protests against this because it implies that the intellect operates independently, relying on its own thinking; and this is a grave error, for the intellect is only a mediator in the divine work and not another creator.

4. In the ninth sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says that eternity and its causality are the unity of God, and the unity of God is eternal. I see al-Kirmani refusing the idea of an eternal unity, regarding it instead as a mediator between God and reason — the second being itself the substance of unity for one primary cause and at the same time the first cause of the act of creating and of the creature, the perfect and the perfecting, the eternal and the everlasting, the existing and what exists.

5. In the eleventh sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says that the image is visible in the intellect by way of the unity of God, so that the abstract image becomes eternal within it, because the intellect itself becomes wholly eternal and not partial. Al-Kirmani likewise tends to speak about basic facts even while considering the manifestations of the physical world, and suggests that this applies more to the mind of the Speaker, or the Foundation, or the Imam, than to the underlying cause. This is the Nearest Angel, or the Hidden Pen that writes upon the Tablet of existence.

6. In the twelfth sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says that, since the intellect is caused by the eternity called the Word of God, and since there is no third between them, the intellect becomes similar to it. Al-Kirmani objects, saying that the intellect and the Word are one and the same thing.

7. In the thirteenth sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says that the intellect received the name تَامَّة, the Complete, because its becoming was by the Creator, and because creativity was done by the perfect Creator, so that the creator is thereby perfect — which is itself created; that is, creativity can only be complete. I see al-Kirmani objecting, saying that God has no attributes whereby the creator or the state of being created imposes its superiority over the creature.

8. In the fourteenth sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says that the intellect carries the ideas, and the source from which it draws is the Word exactly — like the sun which sends out light by its own brilliance and not by any natural friction or material cause. If the work of the intellect is the result of the power of the Word and of the Word's ability to convey it, then it becomes clear that the Word is the cause of everything that proceeds from the intellect, and not the intellect itself; and the second, which is the intellect, is only an intermediary between the Word and the حُدُود, the ranks beneath it. Here too al-Kirmani objects, saying that such an arrangement applies only to the minds of the speakers.

9. Finally, in the fifteenth sub-chapter the author of *al-Mahsul* says that the act of creativity is a medium between the Creator and the created, and the same is true of the agent entrusted with it. He adds that the idea of creativity depends on the agent who influences the image, which is transmitted from the creature to the Creator. The closing section contains scattered sentences referring to the same topic, drawn from different clues. As one would expect, al-Kirmani rejects these statements and says that this does not agree with the belief of the monotheists, because the act of the creature here is taken to be independent of the Creator.

In truth the idea of monotheism and the understanding of the knowledge of the حُدُود is hard — indeed very hard; and it is by reaching this knowledge that souls survive, grow, find stability, and endure beyond the world of matter.

The author of *al-Mahsul* said that creativity is a mediator between the Creator and the created, an effect of the influencer upon the influenced. And he said that the image of creativity is intermediate between the doer and the effect, its existence proceeding from the side of the creative doer; therefore the effect of the image is on the side of the creative in the effect, and this image — I mean creativity — has come into the Creative One from the Creative Almighty.

This is the book *al-Riyad* of the great preacher Hamid al-Din Ahmad al-Kirmani, and the sum of the philosophical opinions that were the subject of controversy in his era. Al-Kirmani brought them into the light, drew them with care, refined them, and produced one of the finest philosophical texts in their exposition. In truth the author of *al-Riyad* deserves the gratitude of generations and the appreciation of the Islamic world, because his book is a fruit of thought, a tall tree that grew up in the shadow of this vast world.

## 5. The Author's Purpose

Praise belongs to Allah, Lord of fathers and mothers, Creator of animal and plant, whom all that is in the earth and the heavens glorifies, and who in His generosity bestows His blessings upon His creation. Nothing that exists has existence except by His command, and nothing that comes to be comes to be except by His decree and His determination. He is so firmly established that the intellects cannot deny Him, and so enduring that no change can befall Him. Glory be to Him: all greatness and splendor rise to His omnipotence, the favors and the bounties overflow by His command, and the earth and heaven stand firm by His leave. Those who liken Him to His creation are ruined, and they have strayed a far straying.

I praise Him, I thank Him, I believe in Him and I do not disbelieve in Him, and I bear witness that there is no god but Allah:

> لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ

the true object of worship, who originated all things in the difference of their essences — from the simple that possesses radiance and majesty to the composed that possesses order and perfection. Nothing among them resembles Him, and He is exalted above them in every respect. And I bear witness that Muhammad — His chosen and pure servant, His righteous and Godfearing Messenger — was sent by the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful, the Benevolent and Generous.

He was sent when idols stood worshipped in the sanctuary, when the landmarks of the اَلْحَنِيْفِي religion were threatened, when the fire of disbelief and unbelief blazed, when the edifices of polytheism and idol worship had been raised high, and when the people had sunk deep into the wastes of misguidance and had lost their sight in the blindness of affliction. So he rose up for Allah, striving and contending. He stripped away from them the confusion and darkness that had overshadowed them, and shattered the idols and images they had been worshipping. He set them upon the worship of the Most Merciful and the rites of faith and submission, and replaced the houses of fire with the Sacred House of Allah. He abrogated the rule of the crosses by turning toward the Corner (al-Rukn (رُكْن)) and the Station (al-Maqam). He guided the servants of Allah in His worship onto the level path, delivered them from the claws of the seducing devil, joined them by his blessing to the most trustworthy handhold, and brought them to the courtyards of right guidance and Godwariness. By him the word of التَّوْحِيد rose luminous in its risings, the word of الشِّرْك was abased and darkened in its dawnings, the tongues pronounced the clear signs, the winds drove the ships of salvation across the seas of dispute, and the ruling of Allah the Exalted came to pass in what He willed of the testing of the people of opinions and religions.

To him belongs a prayer that reaches him on behalf of his followers in general, and on behalf of the company of summoners who are from the light of his lineage in particular — that it may make easy for them, on a day when neither wealth nor sons avail:

> يَوْمَ لَا يَنْفَعُ مَالٌ وَلَا بَنُونَ، إِلَّا مَنْ أَتَى اللَّهَ بِقَلْبٍ سَلِيمٍ

a day when nothing avails except one who comes to Allah with a sound heart — the path of his intercession, and may discharge to them the right of his following and his obedience.
