And a prayer upon the Axis of the Religion (Qutb al-Din) and the pillar of the believers, the receiver of his lights and the one who upholds the affair of his followers and his helpers: al-Kazim who restrained his rage in fulfillment of the covenant of Allah the Exalted, the patient one under the abasement of what was usurped, seeking thereby the face of Allah the Exalted — the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, whose glories cannot be numbered and whose virtues cannot be counted. And a prayer upon his progeny, the blessed Imams, by whose lights souls are made to endure, by whose following ill fortunes are lifted, by whose obedience hopes are attained, and by whose intercession perfection is reached. And a prayer upon the Imam who rules by the command of Allah, the Commander of the Believers, and upon his forefathers, the radiant and rightly guided, the family of Taha (طَه) and Yasin (يَاسِين). (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

To proceed. The worthiest matter to which the one who professes the divine Unity should turn his care — and upon which he should fix his resolve — is a matter that leads, in affirming the Unity of Allah the Exalted and knowing His limits, to an ordered system of belief for those who hold certainty, that calls toward lifting confusion and darkness from the souls of the believers, and that guides toward the affirmation of the Unity of the Lord of the Worlds.

I saw that Shaykh Abu Hatim al-Razi had set out to correct, in the book *al-Mahsul*, what he claimed had become corrupt — and that Shaykh Abu Yaqub al-Sijzi (أَبَا يَعْقُوبَ السِّجْزِيَّ), who is al-Sijistani, had come to the support of the author of *al-Mahsul* and stood as a witness to the soundness of his statement. I saw that what they disputed over was not among the branches in which difference may be permitted while the soundness of their principles is preserved. I found Shaykh Abu Yaqub al-Sijzi truthful in some of what he brought forward, in the manner he intended in his refutation, yet biased against Shaykh Abu Hatim in part of it — with some of the words of the two of them issued out of order. Beyond this, the book *al-Mahsul* contained, in the words of its author in the chapter on التَّوْحِيد and on the First Intellect in particular — apart from what pertains to the branches — that which it was more incumbent upon Shaykh Abu Hatim to correct and to address than the long passages of the branches with which he lengthened his book, yet which he left aside and neglected. All of this together stirred the people of the guiding summons to disagreement whenever they came upon a point of it, and to disorder in the paths of التَّوْحِيد and the knowledge of the limits.

I therefore resolved to bring forward the sayings of each of the two: the one from the book The Reform, by way of reform, and the other from the book The Support, by way of rebuttal, each set down in its own terms.

I resolved to speak upon what is entailed by them and what is intended by them, and upon the aspect by which it is correct to attribute their meanings to their authors — so that the truth of the one may be made clear from the other, so that the biased and the transgressing of the two may be shown, and so that the meaning of that over which they differed may be settled, that one may then believe according to it. Then I resolved to speak upon what was neglected of correction in the book *al-Mahsul*, among the principles in which difference is not permitted, and to make the truth plain, seeking reward.

So I did this and gathered all of it in this book, and I named it the book The Gardens: on the ruling between the two, the author of *al-Islah* and the author of *al-Nusra*. For in what it gathers of their sayings and what it brings forward, it is a separation between them, and a clarification of what error persisted and what correction was neglected in the book *al-Mahsul*, and a rendering of the due therein — from what we have gained of the blessings of the Friends of Allah (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). I named it so because it is like gardens: a greenness that delights the eye, a fruit that comes into the hands. And in Allah we seek help:

> وَبِاللَّهِ نَسْتَعِينُ

that in setting forth the matter He may keep us safe from the traces of error, even though we are not wholly cleared of it.

The book gathers ten chapters, and all of them together comprise one hundred and fifty-seven sub-chapters. And Allah is the Helper, and He is our sufficiency and the best Trustee:

> وَهُوَ حَسْبُنَا وَنِعْمَ الْوَكِيلُ

## 6. Is the Soul the Equal of the Intellect?

**Section One**

The author of *al-Islah* said: the soul is complete in itself, because it was emitted from the First Intellect already complete. It is a complete emanation from what is complete, for the Intellect is perfection.

The author of *al-Nusra* said in rebuttal: the author of *al-Islah* does not know that perfection is better and more complete than the perfect. Perfection is an attribute; the perfect is what that attribute describes. Perfection is what is borne; the perfect is the bearer. And the described and the bearer are better and more complete than the attribute and what is borne — for the perfect is described by perfection and bears it, while perfection is the attribute of the perfect and is predicated upon it. This is what they say.

We say: what the author of *al-Islah* intended when he said that the soul is complete because it is emitted from perfection was not a claim about whether perfection is an attribute or not, nor about whether the perfect is described or not. His point was that perfection stands as the cause from which the existence of the effect proceeds. He saw that perfection is the cause of the perfect and prior to it in rank, for without perfection the perfect would not exist. The proof of this is that if perfection is removed from consideration, the perfect is removed with it — just as with the root-forms from which the past verb, the future verb, the subject, and the object are derived: remove the root, and everything that would have been conjugated from it is removed with it. He therefore treated the First Intellect — first in existence, the source from which existing things arise — as the cause, and called it perfection. He treated the emanating soul as the effect, and called it the perfect. That being his intent, his claim that the First Intellect is perfection is true. And since it is true, what the author of *al-Nusra* said in this vein is an overreach against the author of *al-Islah*.

**Section Two**

Now consider the claim of the author of *al-Nusra* that the perfect is better than perfection. Since the discourse here about perfection and the perfect concerns the First Principle — the first effect and the first emanation, nothing else — that claim would require that nobility belong to the effect rather than to its cause. The argument stands as follows: the perfect is perfect only through perfection, and without perfection it would not be perfect; whatever has its being through a thing, that thing is its cause. If so, and the perfect has its existence through perfection, then perfection is the cause of the perfect, and the perfect is the effect of perfection. Yet if the perfect is the effect of perfection and the author of *al-Nusra* still assigns nobility and superiority to the perfect over perfection, he has required the effect to be nobler than its cause — which is impossible. For whatever has its existence through the existence of another, such that when the other is removed its own existence is removed with it, stands beneath that other in nobility and priority. Since this is impossible, the author of *al-Nusra*, in what he has put forward, has overreached against the author of *al-Islah*.

**Section Three**

The author of *al-Nusra* argued a second time: perfection is an attribute and the perfect is what is described; perfection is what is borne and the perfect is the bearer. He offered this as evidence that the perfect is nobler than perfection, and that the bearer is nobler than what it bears.

We say: this does not hold without qualification. In our own experience, within the realm of coming-to-be and of the second emanation, we find things that are borne yet are nobler than their bearers. The nutritive faculty is the proximate cause for the existence of the growing faculty and is its bearer; the growing faculty is a form borne within it and a completion of it, and the growing is nobler than what bears it. The growing faculty, in turn, is the proximate cause for the existence of the sensitive faculty and is its bearer; the sensitive faculty is a form borne within it and a completion of it, and the sensitive is nobler than that. And the sensitive faculty is the proximate cause for the existence of the rational faculty and is its bearer; the rational faculty is a form borne within it and a completion of it. The nobility of this last is unlike the nobility of those that came before it.

Yet from the side of origination and the first emanation, it is impossible that the First Intellect should be what is borne and the second Intellect — emanating from it — be the bearer, or that the perfect which is the First Intellect should be an attribute of the perfect which is the second Intellect. Since this is so, the inference the author of *al-Nusra* draws in his rebuttal is an overreach against the author of *al-Islah*. His words hold true only if carried over to natural things, which are far removed from what the author of *al-Islah* is discussing — the principles of origination and emanation. The saying of the author of *al-Islah* is sound in what pertains to the world of origination and emanation; and the saying of the author of *al-Nusra*, though he errs in directing it against the author of *al-Islah*, is sound in what pertains to the world of bodies and their accidents.

**Section Four**

Beyond this we say: perfection is a name that applies to the first thing — which is the first cause — and it is also the first perfect, which is the first effect. Its completeness lies in the ascription of its existence to that from which its existence comes, and its completeness lies equally in its ascription to that upon which its own essence rests. This is like origination and the originator on this same order. Origination is not a thing separate from the originator; it is rather that by which the originator is itself. What is it, then? It is a single essence: when ascribed to that from which its existence came, it is origination; when ascribed to that upon which its essence rests, under the mark of coming-to-be, it is the originated. So the First Intellect is perfection, and it is the perfect in truth. It deserves to be the perfect because nothing of its own kind can exist beside it. The perfect thing is that which has nothing outside itself resembling it in its kind — as with the beings found in the natural realm, let alone in origination. The body, too, is a name that applies to a thing complete in its three dimensions, such that nothing like it exists outside it; whatever resembles it belongs to it and is counted from within it. Likewise the sun is complete, for nothing like it exists in its kind and outside it.

In sum, the existence of what lies below the First Intellect, when ascribed to it, is a deficient existence. Yet that deficiency is not in its own essence but in its rank, by ascription to what is above it. All things are complete in their own essences, since their existence is from the perfection that is the First Intellect, and there is no deficiency in them in themselves. Were they deficient in their own essences, their existence from perfection would be impossible; and it would also be impossible that from the deficient there should come anything other than itself, since the deficient — which stands in need of its own completion — cannot give existence to something other than itself. Deficiency attaches to a thing only in its ascription to what is above it in rank, not in its own essence. The essence, insofar as things are the essences of one same thing, is like the numbers: numbers are complete in their own essences yet come to seem deficient only in their ascription to something else through which they come to be. So too with all the beings of the world, which are complete and lack nothing insofar as they are bodies, but are deficient in respect of their ranks among the forms — and they differ insofar as some forms are nobler than others, such as the human being, who is the noblest of all forms. In itself the human being is complete like the rest, and deficient only in ascription to what is above him. If the matter stands thus in the corporeal realm, then so it is, by way of rank, with what is not body on this same order — like to like.

Therefore Allah, exalted is He, said:

> سَنُرِيهِمْ آيَتِنَا فِى ٱلأٓفَاقِ وَفِىٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَهُمْ أَنَّهُ ٱلْحَقُّ

We shall show them Our signs in the horizons and within their own selves, until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth (the verse). That is: We shall raise up for them the Imams and the limits, in the outward and the inward, that these may guide them to the state upon which the witnessed bodies stand and the souls whose actions are found, so that what had been hidden from them — the knowledge of the limits of Allah, which are the truth — may become clear to them, and the truth of the form become manifest.

**Section Five**

The author of *al-Islah* said: what stands in need of perfection is the act of the soul, not the essence of the soul, for the act of the soul is completed only in time, and the emanation of the soul is together with time, just as the First Intellect and time are one — since the First Originator, together with origination and time and perfection, is a single reality. So the Fashioner originated all the realities at a single stroke. The soul, though it needs to draw benefit from the First, is not thereby deficient in its essence, just as the First was not deficient though it stood in need of the matter. Completeness and deficiency appear in the act of the soul, not in its essence.

The author of *al-Nusra* said: he did not know that the Intellect stands in need of nothing among things in the way the soul needs to draw benefit from the Intellect. The relation of the Intellect to the matter is not like the relation of the soul to the Intellect, since the matter, from the side of the Intellect, is nothing other than the Intellect — their identity is one. It is impossible to say that a thing needs what it itself is; need of one thing for another arises only when the other is distinct from it. This is what they say.

We say: the saying of the author of *al-Nusra* is a reply to what the author of *al-Islah* maintained — namely, the need of the Intellect for the matter only. Since the discourse concerns the principles that stand outside the world of nature, the saying of the author of *al-Nusra* in this is nearer to the truth than the saying of the author of *al-Islah*. For what truly may be said to stand in need of perfection — what is completed only in time because it is an emanation from the world of nature and not from outside it — is the souls, which do not attain the degree of perfection (I mean the ascent to the degree of the second completion) except in time, whose existence is an emanated existence. If the saying of the author of *al-Islah* is carried over to this sense, it is sound.

**Section Six**

As for the saying of the author of *al-Islah* that the Intellect and time are one — because the Originator, together with origination and time and perfection, is a single reality — this is not sound. Time is the number of the motions of the degrees of the sphere: the imagined span between the motion of the first degree and the motion of the degree that follows, until the first returns to where it was. But origination, which is the Intellect, and its perfection, are prior in existence to the sphere by whose degrees' motion the existence of time comes to be. To attach the existence of time to the existence of the First Intellect, and to hold this as belief, is contrary to what the law of the guiding call establishes. We have already set forth, in our book known as *Rahat al-Aql*, what is sufficient for seekers concerning the nature of time and of what is prior to it in existence, together with the evidence from the deposits of the prophetic law.

**Section Seven**

The author of *al-Nusra* said: were it not that the Intellect denies divinity of itself, the one that follows would have no way to carry its knowledge past the rank of the Intellect to the Originator. What deficiency is plainer than this — that he knows the Originator only by way of the First?

We say: this may not be held of the one that follows. The power that runs through all the noble beings in the world of unity grants them the knowledge that they have an Originator; and were it not for that power, no thing would have existence or subsistence — it would fall to nothing. For this reason every thing deserves the name of oneness, and though it be manifold in itself, its share of that power is not withheld, and the justice within it is plain. It is impossible to conceive that a single individual of the human kind should not know that it has a Creator, or should fancy that it has no Creator — let alone that great and majestic Limit. We say rather: he apprehends that from which his existence comes as the true Object of worship, even while there is within him a directing toward the divine. Then, if we apply to the First Intellect what the author mentioned, and to the one that follows what he said, it would be obligatory that the Foundation, whose standing is in the world of the body, stand in the place of the one that follows in the world of the Intellect — since without the Speaker one would not know that one has a God. But the matter is not so, for each of the two has a portion from Allah; the Speaker is prior to him only in rank, by his nearness to the First and his being later than it. And therefore Allah said:

> لَا الشَّمْسُ يَنْبَغِي لَهَا أَنْ تُدْرِكَ الْقَمَرَ وَلَا اللَّيْلُ سَابِقُ النَّهَارِ وَكُلٌّ فِي فَلَكٍ يَسْبَحُونَ

It is not for the sun to overtake the moon, nor does the night outstrip the day; each swims in its orbit. In their circling, all are one — yet in their ranks they differ.

**Section Eight**

The author of *al-Nusra* said: proof enough of the deficient thing's deficiency is that all the ancients placed the Soul in the position of form and the Intellect in the position of prime matter, saying that forms forever yearn and long toward matter, and that matter has no subsistence without the forms.

We say: his claim that the Soul is a form is sound. But his claim that the Intellect stands in the position of prime matter — since the argument here concerns the First Intellect — presses unjustly against the author of *al-Islah*.

For in holding the author of *al-Islah* to this, I say: the word "intellect" is a shared name. What the author of *al-Nusra* says does not apply to what is supra-natural — that is, to the first creative and emanating Intellects. It applies only in the world of nature, to the other intellects: the intellect in potentia, the intellect passing from potentia to actuality, and the intellect in act. If the argument concerned the supra-natural, then he has taken what is said of the natural and applied it to the supra-natural, and his contradiction was a pressing wrong.

He said that forms are forever longing and yearning toward matter, and that matter has no subsistence apart from the forms. This is not sound. Forms do not exist as things longing toward matter for matter's own sake, nor are they what matter acquires. Rather, it is matter that yearns for the forms in order to exist — for the sake of the forms — and it is matter that acquires the forms, matter standing in relation to both the baser form and the nobler form. If that is so, and he has run the Soul in the course of form and the Intellect in the course of matter, and has ruled that matter has no subsistence apart from form, then it is as though he has placed the Intellect's existence within the Soul. How can such a belief hold for something whose existence was originative? And how can one imagine that the Intellect is the cause of the existence of both the Intellect and the Soul, when the argument concerns the supra-natural, which stands outside the world of nature? It cannot. It is established that the Next has its existence through the Prior, not the Prior its existence through the Next. This is a manifest fallacy, intended only to lead him toward what is said about natural things. As for what belongs to the world of origination and the first emanation — the matter of the existents of the abode of nature, from the Intellect and the Soul, which are of the order of the second emanation — the matter stands as he described, and from that standpoint it is sound.

On the matter of emanation, the author of *al-Nusra* said: the Intellect is the first mean, the Soul is the second mean, and the first mean is nearer to the Cause than the second mean. The nearness and rank of one thing to another can arise only from perfection and nobility, and distance can arise only from deficiency and want of nobility.

I say: this statement does not hold for the creative and emanative Intellects. To be a mean requires two ends. It is not admissible to call the First Intellect a mean unless its existence is such that it has an end on one side and an end on the other, standing in a comparable position in existence on both sides. How then can one call the first of all ones a mean, when nothing precedes it in existence that it might stand intermediate between that and two terms? Even to suppose the First Intellect a mean is impossible, because its existence is a first existence, and what has a first existence is not called a mean. A mean deserves that name only because beyond it lies something of its own kind of existence — so that there is an affinity between the two — and likewise because something follows it that suits it in some respects, so that it has an affinity with the one from one face and with the other from another face, standing intermediate between them with two ends. As for the First Intellect, there is no affinity at all between it and its Originator — exalted be His mention — nor is it of the kind of His existence, that it might be a mean between Him and what is below it. Nor is there any occasion for its being a mean between itself and its Originator. What stands between it and its Originator, through which it was caused to exist, is something else altogether; and what is called the mediator between it and what is below it is the very essence of origination — the Cause that is said to be both originating and caused — as I have shown in the Illuminated Epistle and in our well-known book *Rahat al-Aql*. This being so, there is no basis for the inference the author of *al-Nusra* draws here in support of what he wished to establish. What he already advanced concerning deficiency and nobility is sufficient. Deficiency attaches to the Next only in relation to the First Intellect, not in itself: its essence stands without deficiency and possesses perfection, while in relation to its predecessor it is deficient in rank — as with the Speaker, the Foundation, the Sun, and the Moon. Each of these — the Foundation and the Moon — is complete in itself, lacking nothing; yet when set beside its counterpart, the Speaker and the Sun, it is deficient in rank. The author of *al-Nusra* binds the Next to deficiency by relating it to its predecessor; the author of *al-Islah* binds its essence to completeness because it emanates from the Perfection that is its cause. Each saying is thus sound on its own terms, except that the author of *al-Nusra* presses upon the author of *al-Islah* from where he did not intend and from what was not his aim.

The author of *al-Nusra* said: if he says, "I do not mean, speaking of the author of *al-Islah*, that the Intellect is like the Soul in the matter of completeness," we say to him: then it is either superior to it or more deficient than it; and since he does not hold that it is superior to it, it remains for him to say that it is more deficient.

I say: the author of *al-Islah* may well not say what the author of *al-Nusra* has undertaken to report of him. Rather he says: all things are complete in their essences, and none merits deficiency except in relation to what is higher in degree, so that what is higher holds a rank not found in the thing related to it. Since the thing's dependence is upon that, the aspect of the wrong pressed upon him is made plain.

The author of *al-Nusra* said: among the clearest proofs of the Soul's deficiency and the Intellect's completeness is the word of Allah the Exalted:

> وَمِنْ آيَاتِهِ اللَّيْلُ ، وَالنَّهَارُ ، وَالشَّمْسُ ، وَالْقَمَرُ
>
> "And among His signs are the night, and the day, and the sun, and the moon."

The night and the day stand for the two Foundations, and the sun and the moon for the two Origins — the sun for the First and the moon for the Next. There is no dispute among the people of knowledge and wisdom that the moon is more deficient in light than the sun, less in light and radiance, and that it draws its light from the sun while the sun bestows that light upon it. This is his saying.

I say: it has become clear from his own saying that the moon is more deficient in light than the sun, less in light and radiance, and that it draws its light from the sun — yet this deficiency attaches to it only through its relation to the sun, while its essence has no deficiency in it. For were its essence deficient, it could not have received the light from the sun; and because its essence is complete, it stood ready to receive from the sun what it is able to receive. When the author of *al-Islah* speaks of the completeness of the essence, and the author of *al-Nusra* speaks of its deficiency in the states and accidents through which its perfection comes, there is a real difference between the two sayings, and the author of *al-Islah* is as one wronged — pressed with what was neither his aim nor his intent.

The author of *al-Nusra* said: among the things that also indicate the Soul's deficiency is that the sages placed the Intellect in the position of spirit to the Soul, and placed the Soul in the position of body to the Intellect. They did this only because they recognized the Soul's deficiency and the Intellect's perfection, likening the Soul to the deficient thing and the Intellect to the perfect thing.

I say: by this saying he has added a clarification to what I said — that the deficiency is not in the essence but in something else, namely the states — even though the sense of his words holds only for the beings of the second emanation. For acquiring belongs only to the souls in the world of nature, so that they may attain their second perfection. In its essence, the Soul is complete and whole, save that it is deficient in respect of the intelligibles it does not yet possess, which flow to it as spirit to body. As for the second — which is the first emanation — its way is not the way of these souls, because its perfection and completeness were given at the beginning of its existence, not through time and acquiring, but only through a deficiency in relation to its predecessor in rank, and through precedence and posteriority.

The author of *al-Nusra* said: even if there were no deficiency in the Soul save that nature falls upon its horizon, the Soul still has two extremities — one toward the Intellect and one toward nature — while the Intellect is not so, for its two extremities face the Word on one side and the Soul on the other, and nothing of it clung to dark nature, on account of its completeness and perfection. That an extremity of the Soul clings to nature, because nature falls upon its horizon, is itself something that, when an observer contemplates it, reveals the reality of the Soul's deficiency and the Intellect's perfection. This is his saying.

I say: this statement proceeds without order. The falling of nature upon the horizon of the Next — which he calls the Soul — does not confer deficiency upon it. Rather it indicates a completeness in it: nature moves toward it because nature is deficient in relation to it, while it, in relation to nature, is noble and complete, being singled out for what nature is not singled out for. This holds together with the fact that both essences — I mean the essence of the Next and the essence of nature — are complete, in that they emanate from the Perfection. As for his saying that the Intellect has two extremities, one toward the Word and the other toward the Soul: the Intellect is not a thing other than the Word, nor is the Word a thing other than the Intellect. The essence is one and the entity is one; it is by relation that it merits the several names whose meanings are distributed, as has already been said when the assertion of its being a mean was discussed — which is not hidden. And as for his saying that nothing of the Intellect clung to dark nature on account of its completeness and perfection, while an extremity of the Soul clung to nature because nature falls upon its horizon: it is not the Next that clung to nature and loved it and desired it. It is nature that loved the Next, and it is nature that moves toward it and joins itself to it. To believe otherwise — to imagine that an extremity of the Next clung to nature — is an error. The most charitable reading of his saying is that his aim was that the Next pours forth upon nature, and that this outpouring is what he called clinging.

The author of *al-Islah* said: what is deficient is the act of the Soul, not the essence of the Soul; and it is its act that is perfected, not its essence.

The author of *al-Nusra* said: this statement is contradictory. If the act is deficient, that deficiency must arise from one of three sources: the deficiency of the essence of the agent, the deficiency of the matter from which the act of the agent proceeds, or the deficiency of both. If the deficiency of the act is from the deficiency of the essence of the agent, then he has affirmed the deficiency of the Soul, since it is the agent. And if it is from the deficiency of the matter, then he has affirmed an eternal matter — a departure from the doctrine of religion and an inclining toward the position of Abu Zakariyya al-Razi (أَبُو زَكَرِيَّا الرَّازِي) and those who held the pre-eternity of prime matter. I have suspected the author of this book of leaning toward that, because he made time eternal alongside the Intellect, and that is the substance of his doctrine. And if the deficiency of the act is from the deficiency of both, then he has held both positions at once, affirming the deficiency of the Soul — since it is the agent — and affirming the pre-eternity of prime matter. Whichever he says, he has said: the Soul is deficient. This is his saying.

I say: what the author of al-Islah said, and the aim he pursued in speaking of deficiency and completeness, has already been set out. What the author of al-Nusra said in response is sophistry, forced argument, and a departure from the bounds of proper reasoning.

He made three objections. First, after dividing the matter of deficiency into the three divisions he described, he said that if the deficiency of an act comes from the essence of the agent, and the agent is the Soul, then the Soul is deficient. But the author of al-Islah made plain that deficiency does not fall upon the essence of the Soul — which is the Next — but upon its act in the sense of its object, and so he is not caught by this division.

Second, he said that if the deficiency comes from the essence of matter, then he has affirmed an eternal matter. Yet the Perfection and the perfect one — which are the First and the Second — have their pre-eternity affirmed as a pre-eternity that is not first and original. How much less, then, can that be affirmed of what is deficient? If that is so, deficiency belongs only to the things that receive their completeness, not to that from which completeness is received. This is well established in the natural sciences: deficiency arises from the side of the recipients, not from the side of what is received from. Consider the sun's heat. Bodies suited for swift affection — such as wax, which softens under it — receive it readily, while other bodies, such as stone and iron and the like, resist it. The source is one, the heat is one, and the deficiency lies in what stands apart from both.

Third, he said that the author of al-Islah inclined toward the doctrine of Abu Zakariyya in the manner he described, and that this was a departure from the bounds of knowledge and a transgression. In none of this did he do justice to al-Islah — neither in the way he framed his division nor in what he drew from it when he charged him simultaneously with affirming deficiency and the pre-eternity of prime matter.

The author of al-Nusra said: as for his argument that the Soul became complete only because it emanated from the first Intellect, which is a perfection, and that what emanates from perfection can only be complete — this is not true. It does not follow necessarily that the Soul is complete simply because it emanated from the complete Intellect. Were that a necessary consequence, then what emanates from the Soul would also be complete — namely prime matter and form — and what emanates from prime matter and form would be complete in turn, all the way down to the individual, indivisible persons, so that everything becomes complete. His argument, taken to its conclusion, requires that the whole world be complete, with no deficiency anywhere in it.

I say: the substance of his argument is this — if the Soul were complete by virtue of emanating from the first Intellect, which is the Perfection, then prime matter and form, by virtue of emanating from the Soul, would likewise be complete, and so in turn would everything that emanates from prime matter and form, down to the individuals, and everything that exists in the world would be complete, with no deficiency in any of it. I have already set out, in what preceded, what the author of al-Islah meant by saying that the Soul is complete, and what the author of al-Nusra meant in this passage.

The author of al-Nusra held that the Soul is complete because it emanated from the first Intellect, which is perfect — on the ground that what emanates from perfection cannot itself be other than perfect. But what I have already set against this does not exhaust the matter, and I must now complete the argument. The Soul is not what emanates the Soul, nor is it what emanates the individuals, as he claimed. The Soul is indivisible, whereas the individuals are all fragmented into their parts. And prime matter is brought into being together with the Soul, out of what precedes it in existence, not out of the Soul itself. The proof of this is that the Soul — the second in rank — does not have a first existence. Nothing exists alongside it that shares its own kind of existence, and nothing stands above it in rank but the First, which is the first Intellect. Its existence is a second existence, and whatever has a second existence cannot exist without something of its own kind existing alongside it, so that the two are partners in kind.

That from which this Soul, the second, came into being is the first thing — nothing of its kind precedes it, and it is one in its existence. This was for the sake of the transcendence of God, exalted be He, who brought it into being free of relations, additions, and multiplicity, and free of all that a thing is described by according to its divisions. For those very relations and additions in the acting causes are what make it possible for more than one thing to come to be from a cause, since multiplicity in effects follows the relations belonging to their causes. But God, exalted be He, is too transcendent to be preceded by any other, or to have another exist alongside Him, or to be differentiated by attributes, relations, and additions. He, exalted be He, is the Originating Maker and the Cause that has no cause. With His purity, His holiness, and His exaltation above all multiplication, it is impossible that anything should come to be from Him except one.

This one — the first Intellect — stood in two relations. One is its being a cause for the existence of what is below it; the other is its being an effect, since it is a thing originated and invented. It followed necessarily that what comes to be from it should be two, corresponding to these two relations in its essence. What came to be from the first relation is the second Intellect; and what came to be from the other relation is prime matter, from which are the celestial spheres and the rest of the existents in the world of body. When existence passes beyond the one into coming-to-be, it can only become two. For after the rank of the one in existence there is nothing but two, and after the odd there is nothing but the even, which is multiplicity. Since the first existent in the number of existents is one, what follows it — when the Intellect apprehends it — is two, which is the even, or multiplicity. And since it is two, one of the two is what we name the Soul, and the other is what we name prime matter, from which are the spheres, the stars, and all that is below them.

The foundation of religion and its ordinances was laid upon the balancing of existents and their correspondences, so that their arrangement might point toward the existents among the ranks hidden from the eyes. The Book and the Law came to be by way of the Speaker, and the establishing of the Foundation stood as the interpreter of them both, again by way of the Speaker. This was the clearest indication that what came to be from the first Intellect — which in its own world stands as the Speaker stands in the world of body — is the second, which is the Soul and prime matter together, just as the Book and the Foundation came to be by way of the Speaker. Bearing witness to this is what the Prophet did in joining together the Book and the Progeny, whose existence is from him, when he said:

> إِلَّا أَنِّي تَارِكٌ فِيكُمُ الثَّقَلَيْنِ كِتَابَ اللَّهِ وَعِتْرَتِي، حَبْلٌ مَمْدُودٌ مِنَ السَّمَاءِ إِلَى الْأَرْضِ، طَرَفٌ مِنْهُ بِيَدِ اللَّهِ وَطَرَفٌ مِنْهُ بِأَيْدِيكُمْ، فَتَمَسَّكُوا بِهَا لَنْ تَضِلُّوا مَا إِنْ تَمَسَّكْتُمْ بِهِمَا، وَقَدْ سَأَلْتُ رَبِّي أَنْ يَرِدَا عَلَيَّ الْحَوْضَ كَهَاتَيْنِ

I am leaving among you the two weighty things: the Book of God and my Progeny, a rope stretched from the heaven to the earth, one end of it in the hand of God and one end of it in your hands. Hold fast to them, and you will not go astray so long as you hold fast to them. I have asked my Lord that they be returned to me together at the Pool, like these two. And he joined together the two forefingers of both his hands. Then he said:

> وَلَا أَقُولُ كَهَاتَيْنِ

I do not say like these two, and he pointed with the forefinger and the middle finger of one hand, the one of them going before the other, and said:

> نَاصِرُهُمَا لِي نَاصِرٌ، وَخَاذِلُهُمَا لِي خَاذِلٌ

Whoever aids them has aided me, and whoever forsakes them has forsaken me. His likening of the two, in existence, to his two forefingers was because neither goes before the other. He thus set himself — in the abode of body — in the course of the first Intellect in the abode of origination, and he set what he brought of the Book, along with what he joined to it of his Progeny, who is the Foundation, together with the rest of the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), in the course of what came to be from the first Intellect as the second and as prime matter, in the manner I described in the book Rahat al-Aql. Thus prime matter does not display its nobility, nor does the goodly form that lies within its potency appear, except by the power of the Soul — which is the second — and by the shining of the light upon them both. Just as the Book and the Law do not announce themselves, nor make manifest the wonders of the wisdom within and beneath them, except through the interpretation of the Foundation and the one who stands in his place. Prime matter, then, has its existence from the first Intellect together with the second.

Then I say: I have said that the first Intellect has nothing of its own kind of existence alongside it, because its existence — which came from that which is above it — is nothing but a first existence, and the First can only be one. Beyond this, were it otherwise, the paths of declaring the divine unity would fall into disorder. For if it had a likeness in its existence, then the existence of the two together would be caused, and neither would precede the other, and this would require something preceding them both — yet what their existence might come from in that case would be an impossibility. The existence of two-ness is a multiplying of essences, and multiplicity in essences does not have its existence from multiplicity in essences; rather, it has its existence from what precedes it, which is one in essence from one side and multiple in meanings from another. And that which is one in essence yet multiple in meanings has its existence not by its own essence but by another which is not multiple — neither in meanings nor in essence. It is thereby made clear that if the first Intellect had alongside it something of its own kind of existence, the two would be things brought to be from a one preceding them, and the existence of that one would consist in its being multiple in meanings though not in its essence, which in turn would require something preceding it and rendering it one. Since nothing stands above the Intellect, it is impossible that it should have a likeness of its own kind of existence. It is therefore established that it is one. In the establishing of the Intellect as one lies the being-two of what comes from it — namely the Soul and what is named prime matter. And since the Soul and prime matter come into existence together, in the way I have shown, it is plain that the claim that prime matter appears out of the Soul is an error.

The author of al-Nusra committed himself to affirming deficiency in prime matter and form, and deficiency in the world and all that is in it. I say: prime matter and form are both complete, and the world with all that is in it is whole and complete, and its parts in their own essences are complete and whole. The proof is this: the complete is that outside of which nothing of its own kind of existence is to be found. The world is the sum of prime matter and form together with all the parts of body, and body in its own essence is complete in its three dimensions; it has nothing whose limit binds it save that which is within it and not outside it. So it is complete. And since it is complete, the position of the author of al-Nusra — that the world should be complete — is refuted, and the impossible follows from him. If the impossible follows from him, his words are mere overreaching.

It is enough against him to cite what he himself wrote in the book known as al-Iftikhar, in the chapter of the Command, as an indication of the world's perfection, where he said: the mark of the world's being sufficient in its totality, needing nothing besides itself, is the absence of any other, and the sufficiency of wisdom with what is in the totality of the world of richness and perfection. I do not know how this escaped him. Perhaps he composed al-Iftikhar after al-Nusra.

As for the disparity found among the parts of the world — on account of which the author of al-Nusra fastens deficiency upon it — that arises only from the addition of some parts to others in respect of their forms and their states, which is the first perfection already present. Among the parts of the world are those given their first perfection and their second at the outset; and among them are those given their second perfection only at the last, by acquisition. The argument has thus made plain that the deficiency the author of al-Nusra speaks of belongs only to the later states that attach to the natural essences and appear when some are added to others, whereas the completeness the author of al-Islah speaks of belongs only to the essences whose existence is a first existence.

The author of al-Nusra said: if it were necessary — because the Soul is emanated from the Intellect, which is the perfect — that the Soul be complete, then it would likewise be necessary, because the Soul is emanated from the Intellect, which is the first intermediary and the first originator, that the Soul be an intermediary like the First and an originator, so that the Soul attains of the excellences what the Intellect attains, and likewise the whole world and the individuals. This is his saying.

I say: the argument against holding the Intellect to be the first intermediary has already been set out. In sum, what he has advanced is a flawed proposition.

When one thing receives its existence from another — one being cause, the other effect — it does not follow that the effect shares every state of its cause, such that the cause's identity would become the effect's identity and the cause's due would become the effect's due. Were that so, nothing could be said of the effect that was not equally said of the cause itself; the two could not be found to be more than one; and it could not then be said that the effect came to be from the cause as something in any way distinct from it. Yet neither does it follow, from the effect's not sharing all the states of its cause, that it shares none of them. For if it shared none of its cause's states, that thing could not be a cause of the effect's existence at all, nor could it bestow existence upon it. Since the very way multiplicity comes to be among things rests upon this principle, it is necessary that the effect resemble its cause in one respect and differ from it in another.

Since this is necessary, it follows that the Second — named the Soul — being emanated from the First Intellect that is the cause of its existence, must resemble it in one respect and differ from it in another. And since it must resemble in one respect and differ in another, it is clear that it is not necessary, simply because the Soul's existence derives from the Intellect, that the Soul should be like the Intellect in all its states. Nor does it follow that whatever is said of the Intellect in its capacity as cause must be said of the Soul. With this, the argument of the author of al-Nusra — that if it were obligatory for one it would be obligatory for the other — is refuted.

The author of al-Nusra said: this has many parallels in things we observe — among them, thought and the particular soul. The particular soul that is in us stands in the place of the Intellect, and thought stands in the place of the Soul. Thought, though it is emanated from the soul, may not be said to resemble the soul, because thought apprehends what it apprehends part by part, moment after moment, whereas the soul apprehends all that it apprehends at once, outside of time. This is his saying.

I say: the soul — I mean the soul of the human being — has acts, and each of its acts has a name proper to it. When it seeks to apprehend something, that seeking is called thought. When it surveys what has gathered in its essence of the forms of known things, that surveying is called memory. When it stamps its essence with the marks of knowledge it has pursued — whether through inference or through the senses — that stamping is called conception. And when it knows that there are things outside itself which it desires to conceive, that desire is called yearning. Since the soul has acts, and thought is one act of it that does not pass beyond its own essence into any existence separate from that essence — how can it be sound to liken thought to the Second, named the Soul, which does have an existence distinct from that of the Intellect? And how can it be said that the soul apprehends part by part, when apprehending belongs to the soul's essence through its act called thought — an act that stays entirely within the soul's own essence? Or how can the soul — I mean the soul of the human being — apprehend the whole outside of time, when it has no path to the objects of knowledge except through the senses and through inference, moving from particulars to universals step by step, in time? Moreover, from the fact that thought may not be said to resemble the soul, it does not follow that the Second Intellect — named the Universal Soul — must not resemble the First Intellect. For thought, as it operates within human souls, does not run the same course as the Second Intellect proceeding from the First. The Second Intellect is a second existence proceeding from origination; it subsists by its own essence, though in the order of existence it stands a rank below the First. Thought, by contrast, has no existence set apart from the soul — it is the soul itself in one of its acts, with no subsistence of its own. Every act of the soul has a cause that occasions it: the soul's dwelling in the world of nature. When the soul departs from that world, its acts cease together with their cause, and nothing remains subsisting but the essence itself, conceiving as pure intellect. He has thus made the Second Intellect — named the Soul — proceed from the First Intellect in the same manner as thought proceeds from the soul. But thought's existence is no existence other than that of the soul he likened to the Intellect, nor does thought possess, of its own essence, the soul's power of apprehension. He has all but denied that the Second Intellect, named the Soul, has any existence at all.

If thought does not belong to human souls, then the Second Intellect would run the same course as the First Intellect — the First Intellect would simply be the Second Intellect, which is the second existence from origination, self-contained, though standing below the first rank in the order of existence. And thought has no existence of its own apart from the soul; it is the very identity of the soul, with no subsistence of its own. Every act of the soul has a cause that occasions it, and that cause is its being in the world of nature. If the soul is separated from that world, its acts are nullified together with their cause, and what remains is only the perceiving self as pure Intellect. Then he made the Second Intellect — called the Soul — proceed from the First Intellect in exactly the way thought proceeds from the soul, so that thought's existence was no existence other than that of the Soul he likened to the Intellect, and thought had of its own essence no apprehension of the soul. He has all but denied that the Second Intellect, called the Soul, has any existence distinct from that of the First Intellect, or any perception resembling the first perception. And that is impossible. For what follows from the First has an existence distinct from the existence of the First, and a perception like the perception of the First. It is not fitting to say that it is creative, just as the First Being does not deserve to be called emanated. Both are pure intellects; each encompasses itself and is preserved within itself. They are similar in one respect and dissimilar in another, as the foregoing discussion has made clear.

If this is so, there is no validity in the analogy he draws, and no validity either in placing the human soul in the position of the First Intellect, or in assigning thought within it the rank of the Universal Soul. The honor of the human soul, and its passage from potency to act, lie entirely in acquisition — and acquisition comes only through the senses in practice and through conception in knowledge. If the honor of souls lies in the acquisition by which they think and reason, and thought belongs to souls precisely as the means of their honoring, then he has in effect made the Second Intellect — called the Soul — the cause of the First Intellect's honor. And that is an inverted proposition. The honor and rank of what follows come from what precedes it in existence, which is the prior; and the honor and rank of the prior are ordered by what comes after it. What exists later, in the order of perfection and completion, must be the reverse of what exists first; the one does not run in the other's course in this respect, nor the other in the course of the one — even if they share something on another side. Yet the author of al-Nusra has judged what exists first by the standard that belongs to what exists later, and that belongs among the impossibilities.

Chapter Twenty-One of Part One.

The author of al-Nusra said: One of the clearest proofs of the Soul's deficiency and the First Intellect's perfection is that the ancients and the author of al-Islah all agreed that action belongs to the Second, attributing none to the First. For action is always a sign of deficiency and need, while the abandonment of action is evidence of perfection and self-sufficiency. That is to say, action is only for drawing a benefit, repelling a harm, or for vain sport.

Abu Zakariyya said: That the Soul initiates action by way of play and vain sport, the people of religion do not hold. What remains, then, is only the drawing of a benefit or the repelling of a harm — and wherever these are found, there is deficiency. The Soul, in its action, seeks to draw a benefit, which is its reaching the rank appointed for it, and for that reason it begins to act. The Intellect, since there is no rank above its own and since it is complete and perfect, does not move to action in any way. The Soul, finding a rank above itself, remained in motion, persisting in its quest. By this argument we speak of the Soul's deficiency and the Intellect's completeness. This statement is clear, and praise be to Allah.

The gist of his argument in this chapter is as follows: the Second is the doer; action is only for drawing a benefit or repelling a harm; the drawing of benefit and the repelling of harm are signs of deficiency alone; and the Intellect, being without deficiency, is at rest from action — the cause of its rest being that there is no rank above it for it to seek. The Soul, finding a rank above itself, moved toward it to attain it.

We say: had the words of the author of al-Islah concerned the soul of human beings, the argument of the author of al-Nusra would have been more fitting. But his words concerned what is called the Soul — not the human soul. And so the author of al-Nusra's inference from the condition of human souls — that they act only to draw a benefit, repel a harm, or engage in sport — as grounds for overturning that judgment and refuting the claim that the Following is complete and perfect, is a manifest prejudice. For it has already been established that what is said of what exists first, with respect to completeness and perfection, is not to be said of what exists later — nor is what pertains to what exists later to be said of what exists first.

Moreover, the author of al-Nusra's account of action and its divisions is not sound. Not every act is for drawing a benefit, repelling a harm, or for sport. There may be an act whose benefit falls entirely upon the object of the act rather than upon the one who performs it. The proof of this is that heating is an act of fire, yet that act confers no benefit or harm upon the fire itself; the benefit and harm return upon whatever the fire's heat reaches. So too the sun's act of illuminating the world — enabling human beings to conduct their affairs by means of it, and by its rising bringing plants, animals, and minerals into being through its warmth — all its benefits return upon others, not upon itself; the sun neither benefits nor is harmed by this act. So too with the whales of the Sea of Oman and those called the dolphin, whose custom it is, when a man is drowning and sinking in the sea, to come to his aid and push him toward the shore. That act returns no benefit or harm upon them; the benefit falls entirely upon the one acted upon.

If this is found among natural things that do not reason — things deficient in their states and dependent upon others in their acts — that they perform such wondrous deeds, then what is there to deny in the pure, abstract, and perfect intellects, endowed with their perfections from the very first of their existence, that they should perform acts greater still than these that appear from natural things? Their perfections require it — in accordance with the nearness of their ranks and the loftiness of their noble stations — not for drawing benefit to themselves or repelling harm from themselves, but for the sake of others. Such acts, then, are acts of wisdom, not acts of drawing benefit, not of repelling harm, and not of sport. Indeed, the perfect power from which the perfect and well-ordered act proceeds belongs only to the perfect.

The Following — which he named a soul — is not in motion as he claimed, in the manner that motion attaches to natural things; for that motion belongs only to bodies and to their natural forms. It stands at a rank above natural things and below the Intellect, as we have shown in the Book of the Repose of the Intellect. By what we have now set forward, his overreach and his argument stand exposed — they do not hold when applied to the one under discussion. The saying of the author of al-Islah stands sound: the Following is complete in its completeness, as we have explained in what preceded. It is not permissible to apply to what dwells in the abode of origination — the First and Second Intellect and the higher letters — the terms of motion and rest, for we have shown that these two belong only to what is a body or possesses a body, and in this there is sufficiency.

The author of al-Nusra said: What follows from the author of al-Islah's position is that it is the soul's act that is completed, not the soul's essence — while he holds that the soul is already complete. This would mean the soul's act becomes a soul, which is a plain and evident impossibility. We have never observed acts becoming agents. Consider the writer and the writing, the builder and the building: writing never becomes a writer, nor does a building become a builder. It is the man — a writer in potency, a builder in potency — who becomes a writer and a builder in act. And so we must say of the soul and the Intellect: the Intellect is complete in act, while the soul is deficient in act, subsisting in potency, and it comes to subsist in act when it gains completeness. For its act to become complete is impossible.

We say: When the author of al-Islah says it is the soul's act that is completed and not the soul's essence, he means what attains the second perfection from the soul's side — what flows from it as something the soul pours forth upon, issuing from potency to the bound of act. If what he means by "the soul's act is completed" is what issues through the soul's overflow from potency into act and thereby attains the second perfection — and if nothing crosses from potency into act except through what already subsists in act — then when what had subsisted in potency issues into act and attains the second perfection through acquisition, it cannot be denied that it becomes like what subsists in act in the perfection bestowed upon it at the first of its existence. They thus share in this: both subsist in act, even though each attained that shared condition from a different side. Nor is it impossible that they be alike in the respect they share, for it is known that everything gathered with another thing in some common meaning is, in that meaning, its like — as our own argument attests: everything that issues from the bound of potency to the bound of act subsists in act; everything that subsists in act does so through the occurrence of the second perfection to it; and everything that issues from potency into act has the second perfection occur to it. If what subsists in act subsists through the occurrence of the second perfection, and what subsisted in potency and then came to subsist in act does so likewise through the occurrence of the second perfection, then they share and become alike — both attained the second perfection — even though they differ in other respects. Since what we have set out is neither impossible nor to be denied, but is in fact necessary, the objection of the author of al-Nusra amounts to nothing but overreach.

Chapter Twenty-Three of Part One.

The author of al-Nusra then said: This is a plain and evident impossibility, for we have never observed acts becoming agents — and he brought forward the example of the writer and the builder as proof that what he declared impossible is indeed impossible.

We say: Acts are in truth agents. The writing he cited as the act of the writer is itself what converses with its reader and addresses him. If writing — in its forms and its tracing, while being the act of another — were not itself an agent upon the reader's soul, speaking for its author, interpreting him, and inscribing him in its forms, the reader would never come to know what lives in the writer's soul. And the building, through the form the builder brought into being out of what was in his own soul, is what imparts its form to the beholder through its shape and situation, until the beholder conceives it. Had the building's form not subsisted in act so as to impart itself to the soul of the beholder — which takes on its forms — the beholder would never have conceived it. So too the act of the striker, which is the striking, is the agent that produces pain in the one struck and afflicts him, in the manner we set out in our two well-known epistles, al-Rawdah and al-Inarah. And the word of God the Exalted is a still firmer proof that acts are likewise agents, where He says, exalted be the Speaker:

> وَإِن مِّن شَىْءٍ إِلَّا يُسَبِّحُ بِحَمْدِهِۦ

"And there is nothing but that it glorifies Him with His praise." The thing is the act of the Maker, exalted be He, and while being the act of an agent, it is His glorification. And His saying, exalted be He:

> وَأَرْسَلْنَا ٱلرِّيَحَ لَوَقِحَ

"And We sent the winds fertilizing." The winds are a thing, and the fertilizing is His act. With this clarified, it is plain that the objection of the author of al-Nusra against the author of al-Islah in this chapter cannot stand.

What the author of al-Nusra means when he says we have never observed acts becoming agents is that acts have not become identical with their very agent — just as writing has not become identical with its writer, nor has a building become identical with its builder. This is a correct statement, as he put it: an act does not become identical with that from which it issued. Rather, it is an agent like its agent in respect of the act's issuing from it, even though the ranks differ and the relations vary. But the author of al-Nusra wished to defend the one he stands in for by pressing too hard against the author of al-Islah — may God sanctify their two souls — and truly many associates wrong one another.

Chapter Twenty-Four of Part One.

The author of al-Nusra said: He likened the intellect and the soul, in their completeness and their deficiency, to the man and to the drop of seed, because the drop of seed is in truth the form of the man in potency, and it becomes upright — a man like the man. Likewise, the soul is in truth the form of the intellect, and it becomes complete like the intellect when it gains from the intellect the benefits appointed for it. This is his saying.

We say: His claim that the drop of seed is in truth the form of the man in potency, and that the soul is in truth the form of the intellect and becomes complete — this is not sound. The drop of seed is not the form of the man, nor is the soul that is in potency the form of the intellect. The drop of seed is a matter prepared for conception, empty of forms, which it acquires under conditions. Likewise, the soul is only a natural disposition, void of forms, whose nature it is to acquire the forms of the things known, so that its essence is completed by them. If the soul were truly a form of the intellect, it would follow that the intellect must have that for which the form is a form — making the intellect one thing and that for which the form is a form another thing. How can this hold for what is of an originated existence, in which otherness is found only by relation while the identity is one? No indeed. This manner of speaking and likening holds only for what is natural, not for what is external and separate from bodies.

Chapter Twenty-Five of Part One.

The author of al-Nusra said: And again, the rank of the rational one in the corporeal world is like the rank of the intellect in the world.

We say, in answer to him: the Second is like the First, even though it stands in a rank below it — just as the Foundation (أَسَاس) is like the Speaker (اَلنَّاطِق), even though it stands in a rank below him. The Second is complete in its very existence. Nothing remains for it in the form of a degree to be attained later, by which it would afterward acquire completeness — just as the Foundation is complete and whole in its rank, with no further degree left for it to ascend to and thereby acquire a completeness it did not already possess.

The deed of the Prophet is sufficient witness to what we have said, and his own indication points to it, in what he made obligatory when he required that the camel be sacrificed by piercing the throat (nahr) rather than by cutting it (ذَبْح), and that other animals be slaughtered by cutting rather than by piercing. The piercing of the throat is the driving of the iron to the head of the fuad of the pierced beast, so that whatever turbid blood is there may issue out. This stands for the degree of full attainment, the contemplation of the secrets, and the dispelling of doubts and confusions within the ranks. The cutting of the throat stands for the taking of the covenant and the release into the call; and the pierced beast stands for the Foundation.

It is as though the Prophet, by displaying this act, informed the community that followed him. The proof of his role is this: the Foundation was not set in its place in the summons to the inner call — which is the completion of the worship of God that leads to the affirmation of God's oneness and the knowledge of His limits — until the Prophet had first taught the Foundation what its perfection consisted in and made it aware of what its completeness consisted in. He raised its degree by the imparting of the report. Its way of attaining perfection and completion is not like the way of the rest of his community, who receive the report and the completion only afterward — after the covenant has first been taken and instruction has second been given. The Prophet did this with his Foundation only to indicate that the Second, which stands over against the Foundation in the world of the Intellect, attained perfection first. There is no inconsistency in taking the covenant first from the children of the call and then conveying to them the degree of perfection. The conveying is only a pointer and an indication: that the natural souls, which are below the Second, are at first in potency, and then issue forth by acquisition, at the last, into act, which is their perfection and completion — so that in each of these matters one may hold the belief that is fitting to hold.

Nor did he stop at this indication. He indicated the completeness of the Second from another side as well, in the two units (rakahs) of the dawn prayer, which stand for the First and the Second. In the first unit, which stands for the First, he made it obligatory to recite the glorification, the praise, and the sura; and in the second unit he made obligatory the recitation of اَلْقُنُوت in place of the glorification recited in the first, so that the second unit should be in the same state as the first with respect to perfection. Their only distinction, then, lies in rank within existence: this one is the first of existence, like the first unit, and that one is the second of existence, like the second unit.

**Chapter Twenty-Six of Part One**

Then we say, in answer to his statement that — were it permissible to say the soul is as perfect as the Intellect — it would be permissible to say the Foundation is like the Speaker, and that this is not right: how could it fail to be right, when the Foundation is the one who stands in the place of the Prophet and his family? Were it to fall short of standing in his place, and were it not sufficient, complete, and whole like him, God would not have made obedience to it obligatory. How could God, exalted be He, impose obedience to one who is like others in deficiency? That cannot be, nor is it permissible in wisdom. For if the Messenger is a messenger to all who live in his time and to all who are born after him — a proof against His creation — it cannot be that the community is left without one like the Messenger present among them, so that the proof would stand against them without warrant. The Foundation, therefore, is like the Speaker after him from one side, and unlike the Speaker from another side. It is proof enough that the difference between them lies in rank and not in essence that the objector could argue only from rank, in his words, "because the rank of the Foundation is below the rank of the Speaker." It is now clear from what we have set out that the position of the author of *al-Islah* stands secure against the objection of the author of *al-Nusra*, and that his saying "the Second is deficient" is sound only in the particular sense in which it must be understood.

**Chapter Twenty-Seven of Part One**

The author of *al-Nusra* said, by way of argument:

Bowing (الرُّكُوع) is upon the Foundation, and prostration (sujud) is upon the Speaker. The supplication in bowing,

> سُبْحَانَ رَبِّي الْعَظِيمِ وَبِحَمْدِهِ
>
> Glory to my Lord the Great, and to Him be praise,

is the purification of the pure one who bows, from the side of his greatest cultivator (murabbi), who is the Second. And the supplication in prostration,

> سُبْحَانَ رَبِّيَ الْأَعْلَى
>
> Glory to my Lord the Most High,

is the purification of the Speaker, from the side of his cultivator, who is the Second; and the Most High is the one who is upon the Prior, since his highest cultivator is the Prior. The Speaker (ع) has mentioned this meaning in the revelation, in His saying,

> ثُمَّ دَنَا فَتَدَلَّىٰ
>
> Then he drew near and came down.

That is, the Speaker drew near to stand upon the rank of the Intellect, and the Foundation hung down from him, so that he was

> قَابَ قَوْسَيْنِ
>
> at the measure of two bows' length

which are the two principles,

> أو أدنى
>
> or nearer,

meaning the Prior. For the Prophet and his family, from the height of their rank, drew so near that no veil remained between him and the Prior, just as, for the Prior, there was no separation between him and the Word (kalima). The Foundation did not reach this rank and station; and likewise the Second did not reach the rank of the Prior, but remained below his rank. This is found in every pair, for the male is always more complete and more noble, and the female more deficient. So too with the heaven and the earth: the heaven is in the position of the male, and the earth in the position of the female; the heaven is nobler and more complete than the earth, and the earth more deficient than the heaven. This is what he said.

We say:

The claim in *al-Nusra* that the rank of the Prior is higher than that of the Second is sound — and it is what the author of *al-Islah* said as well. Yet the Second's lower rank does not indicate any deficiency in its essence. As for his claim that the Prophet and his family reached, by standing at the rank of the Prior, a completeness the Foundation did not attain — this can scarcely be sound. The Foundation, though it did not reach that degree on its own, attained the degree of completeness through the Speaker: through his teaching and through his making it aware of the blessings sent down upon him. In receiving this from him in full, its soul was illumined and became complete, as the disc of the moon becomes complete in light on the fourteenth night of the month.

The conduct of the Prophet and his family bears witness to this. He placed the sermon before the prayer on the day of Congregation — Friday — and placed it after the prayer on the day of the Feast of Breaking the Fast. God made Friday, in one of the faces of تَأْوِيل, stand for Himself, since it is one of the seven days. His ascending the pulpit for the sermon comes first, and then the prayer, signifying that he attained the degree of perfection and completion and rose to the highest ranks of honor from the side of the two principles: he came first, and then he called to his sacred law, to the acceptance of his revelation, and to obedience to his Foundation. And God made the day of the Feast stand for his Foundation, for it is one of the days of God, exalted be He, on which the religion of God is completed through obedience to Him. Placing the prayer before the sermon on that day signifies that his Foundation stands under obedience to Him and under acceptance of His command and His law: the Foundation first received perfection and completion, rose in the ranks of honor, and was made aware of the degree of full attainment by the mediation of the Speaker. So the Successor (wasi) is complete in his essence, even though his attainment of the degree of completeness comes from the side of the Speaker, and his rank lies below the rank of the Speaker.

Nor is the existence of the female a deficient existence, such that one might draw on it to support what the objector wishes to support. Its existence is a complete existence. Were the female deficient on account of what she lacks that is proper to the male, then the male too would be deficient on account of what he lacks that is proper to the female, and it would follow that the male's existence is not a complete existence — which is absurd. Since the male is complete in himself and the female complete in herself, and it is only by the joining of the one to the other that their ranks become unequal, with the female standing below the male in rank, this is proof enough that the disparity is in ranks and not in essences. So too the Second has not reached the rank of the Prior. As for his saying that the Foundation did not reach this rank: the statement of the author of *al-Islah* is therefore sound, that the Second is complete.

**Chapter Twenty-Eight of Part One**

The author of *al-Nusra* said:

Among the proofs of the soul's deficiency and the Intellect's perfection is that many speaking souls submit to nature, follow wherever it leads and wherever it commands, and incline to its desires and pleasures. They forget their own knowledge, their light, and their splendor, and find the world of nature — its forms and traces — pleasing, remembering nothing of the splendor of their own world and its luminous delights. This is because of their deficiency; and because of their deficiency they crave the desires and pleasures found with nature and turn away in aversion from their own world, its delights, its goodness, and its splendor. The Intellect, on account of its own perfection, goodness, splendor, and completeness, does not forget the delights of its own world, its goodness, or its splendor. It does not incline to nature, nor crave the pleasures and desires found with it, nor look toward it; and if it does look toward it, it looks only from the side of compassion, mercy, and disdain for what nature has — informing nature, out of itself, that what nature possesses is little when set against what belongs to its own world. Had the soul been complete like the Intellect, it would have held firm upon what it beheld in its own world and would not have craved what is with its effect.

This is what he said, arguing that many speaking souls submit to nature and are led to whatever it commands, inclining to it on account of their deficiency.

We say:

The submissive, inclining thing that is led by self-indulgence and pleasures, that follows the desires of nature and abandons acts of worship, is the sensual soul (نَفْس al-hissiyya), for it belongs to nature. It is not the speaking soul (نَفْس al-natiqa) that has become attached to it. The sensual soul is brought around and restrained from committing base and vile acts only when the traces of the Intellect work upon it, by way of the bonds of discipleship, through instruction, so that it holds the soul back from perpetrating what would destroy it. These souls are not the Second that issues forth from the First Intellect — the Second of which the author of *al-Islah* speaks. Enough has already passed in our discourse to imply that the Second is other than these souls, and these souls other than it; that the Second is of such completeness and perfection that nothing in its essence remains unattained that would leave it deficient — as is the case with these souls, which exist at first in a first perfection and have a second perfection that they attain through time and acquisition.

**Chapter Twenty-Nine of Part One**

We say, secondly, that the Second — which is complete and whole, and which was granted its perfection and completeness at the very beginning of its existence, all at once — is existent, and it is through it and through its overflow that the deficient thing comes to receive the degree of perfection. The proof of this is that the existents in the world of nature stand first in potency and then, at the last, pass into act; and nothing that stands in potency can pass into act except through the existence of that which already stands in act. Since there is no passage from potency into act except through the existence of that which stands in act, and since that which passes into act is existent, the existence of that which stands in act is a necessary existence. And if the existence of that which stands in act is necessary, then in its existence it must be either the First Intellect or another.

It is ruled out that it should be the First Intellect, because of the impossibility that the First Intellect's own act should exist except through the existence of another, and because that would require that something exist outside the Intellect for it to act upon. But the First Intellect is self-sufficient in its act — the complete one, needing nothing outside its own essence to act upon. Its existence is from Him from whom it came to be — exalted and magnified — an existence that is one, having no parallel and no other beside it, so that this other might be present with it for it to act upon. Were that so, the Worshipped, exalted be He, would cease to be Worshipped. Enough has already passed in our discourse upon this — and more than what we have set out here — in our book known as رَاحَةُ الْعَقْلِ (Rahat al-Aql, The Repose of the Intellect). That which stands at this station of oneness has nothing present with it for it to act upon, and so no multiplicity issues from it except by its acting in its own essence. And since its act is in its own essence, and its act is what brings the potential thing forth by it and by its overflow into another, it is ruled out that the one through which existents pass from potency into act is the First Intellect.
