And since it is not the First Intellect, it is established that it is other than it, and is the Second.

The truth of what we say is borne witness to by this: that the Prophet and his family did not need, for all the greatness of his rank and his completeness, any other of mankind to complete his work in his place — the work that is the revelation and the sacred law.

...He had no need of any other among mankind to complete his deed in his place, the deed that is the revelation and the law. In that uniqueness — being himself and issuing forth from himself according to what he derived from that which granted him his degree — the Prophet (ع) found what he recited and prescribed it in its perfection. So he conducted himself, in a bodily mode, in the descent (tanzil), along the course of the First Intellect, in that same creative manner, so that his action in its rank would lead whoever contemplates it to the soundness of belief, to knowing what is hidden from the senses among the principles of origination (إِبْدَاع) and renewal (اَلْاِنْبِعَاثُ), so that the path of monotheism (التَّوْحِيد) would remain unaltered.

As for the Foundation, even though he stands at the highest degree of perfection, he did not possess that degree by which he could act alone — ordering the affair of the call (دَعْوَة), the تَأْوِيل, the teaching, and the guidance by himself. So he did not dispense with what he found alongside him, which is the revelation and the sharia. I do not mean that the Foundation dispenses with the revelation and the sharia, nor that the Speaker dispensed with something existing alongside him in the bodily world through which he acts, as the Foundation does. For that reason his action follows the course of the Second.

What obtains in him is the very thing I have mentioned as proof of knowing the rank of the Second. The Prophet (ع) and his family follow the course of the First — the perfect one, who dispenses with anything other than himself in his action and employs nothing other than himself. The Foundation follows the course of the Second, in whom there is no action without the revelation and the sharia; without these he does not dispense in his action, just as the Second does not dispense with that through which its action comes to be. The subordinate community follows the course of what lies beneath the sphere, in the natural world, in which its existents pass from potentiality to actuality through the overflow of the Second, since the souls that stand in potentiality need that by which they attain the degree of arrival and perfection, which comes from the call of the Foundation.

Just as the Second supports the one prepared for acceptance from the world of nature by its addition upon it, so too the Foundation — and whoever stands in his place in the call — brings the willing and obedient to the degree of arrival and completeness. For the Speakers, being from the world of nature, did not attain their completeness and their lofty degree from the side of the First Intellect, but from the side of the Second Intellect first, just as none of us reaches the degree of arrival from the side of the Speaker, but from the side of the Foundation's call first. So they run their course from those above them as we run ours from them, as the saying goes:

> حَذْوَ النَّعْلِ بِالنَّعْلِ

sandal matching sandal, the one exactly as the other. The perfection of the Speakers and of those who stand in their place is from those above them, and our perfection is through them. May God gather us with them. Our discourse has thus made plain that these souls are not the Second, so that whatever is found of their conditions is evidence of their deficiency, and that the objection of the author of *al-Nusra* against the author of *al-Islah* on this ground is an impossible objection.

**Chapter Thirty of Part One**

He said:

The Second is deficient because it forgets its world and does not remember it. Yet how can this be sound, when forgetting occurs only after knowledge? If these souls had existence before these individual persons, and were knowing, it would be impossible for change to come over them by their coming into the abode of nature — not after they had dwelt in majesty and attained the perfection that is intellectual knowledge. For the existence of what exists in the world of origination and emanation, among the intellects, is an existence resembling what is necessary in the bodily world, upon which no change befalls of what it was. For this reason it is called eternal. How, then, can conditions come over what is eternal, so that knowledge belongs to it at one time and is stripped from it at another? The abode in which it dwells is not the abode of change that he has affirmed for it by his word "forgot" —

> نسي

In sum I say: if these souls came to these persons from that world, and came in order to learn and to be trained in the sciences, then their state of ignorance at the outset of their existence in this world is not free of one of two cases. It is either from their essence or by accident. If they are ignorant in their essence, then they are barred from knowing anything, and ignorance is intrinsic to them — which is impossible, for we find that the soul comes to know after not knowing. And if it is by accident, then that accident is not free of having befallen them either in that world or in this one. If it befell them in that world — and that is the abode of eternity and completeness, not the abode of change and deficiency — then it is impossible for an accident to befall them by which they are altered. And if it befell them in this world, and their end is to know and to return to the majesty they were in before change befell them, then that is impossible in wisdom, since their remaining in the first state is more fitting for them than their being altered from it and then returning to it. So it is void to hold that they accept an accident — whether ignorance or otherwise — in this world, given that their existence is not from this lower world. For their existence not being from this lower world is impossible to reconcile with this, since through the completeness that is in them the benefit derived is the completeness that is the blessing of that world, "for the people of knowledge," not of this world. If that is so, and it is impossible that their ignorance be from their essence or by an accident whose origin is from that world, then the claim that their arrival is from that world is void; and once it is void that their arrival is from that world, it is void to say that they forgot their world.

**Chapter Thirty-One of Part One**

If their arrival is from the world of origination, their existence stands first on the side of origination and emanation from the highest relation. Whatever exists in this manner stands in actuality. What stands in actuality is barred from accepting an accident, for the reception of accidents belongs to things that hover between potentiality and actuality within the abode of the body — to whatever is in the process of being divided and determined. What exists in the abode of origination, however, has been granted its perfection and completeness from the very beginning of its existence, and it is eternal. Nothing in that state can accept an accident, nor can anything come over it that would alter it from what it is. This being so — and since the author of al-Nusra holds that it forgot, forgetting being for it an impossible accident — the claim that it forgot its world falls away entirely.

**Chapter Thirty-Two of Part One**

I say: forgetting befalls only those souls that are prepared, from within the world of nature, to receive knowledge — through the entry of accidents upon them by way of the causes from which their existence proceeds. States come over them consequent upon what they follow in coming to be. These are the richest of souls when they are trained by the rites of the sacred law and come to grasp intellectually the things that precede them in existence, all the way to the First Principle. This we call the intellect and the second revival, just as the Second is the first revival — as I explained in my well-known book Rahat al-Aql. It is impossible for these souls to arrive from the world of intellect, and there is no proof for such a claim at all.

What the author of al-Nusra himself mentioned in the forty-fourth key (iqlid) of his well-known book al-Maqalid — concerning the impossibility of these souls arriving from the world of intellect — suffices as an indication of the refutation of what he said in his defense, and of the soundness of what I have set forth. I do not know how that escaped him. As I reckon it, al-Maqalid was composed after al-Nusra. May God gather its author with our pure friends in the abode of holiness, and seal our religion and our world with good, by His grace.

**Chapter Thirty-Three of Part One**

His saying that the intellect, in its completeness and perfection, does not forget its world, does not incline toward nature, and does not desire it — this is sound, if said of souls that have acquired their perfection through knowledge and works, so that they became attached in the abode of nature and came to resemble the intellects that have gone forth in perfection: souls like those of the Speakers and the Foundations who stand in their place (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). As for the intellects that have gone forth — the First, the Second, the Preceding, the Following, the Originator, and the first Emanated — these are too high and too exalted for the natural world to have any measure with them, let alone any inclination toward it. Their overflow upon it is continuous, and through it whatever is not yet perfect is perfected.

His further saying — that if the soul were complete like the intellect, it would have held fast to what it beheld in its world and would not have desired what comes about in its effect — amounts to an affirmation that the soul was altered from its first state and did not hold fast to it. Enough has already been said on this point.

And his saying that it did not desire what is with its effect amounts to an affirmation that nature is the soul's effect. But how can it be sound to say that nature is the effect of souls, and that its existence comes about through the existence of persons who belong to the world of nature? How can one conceive that the heavens, the planets, the natures, and the generated things proceed from them, when they are confined to persons that number can encompass? And how can it be sound that the complete one should desire, in its conditions, what is beneath it? That is impossible. Since it is impossible, it necessarily follows that the existence of the natural world belongs to the first Emanated, and that the perfection of the existents within it comes by its overflow. To explain this is a matter that lays bare the bias of the author of al-Nusra against the author of al-Islah — even though the words of the author of al-Nusra are sound in the manner I have explained, not in the manner he intended in his rebuttal.

**Chapter Thirty-Four of Part One**

The author of al-Nusra said:

The Sage mentioned in logic that all things exist in four senses: either in the essences of things, or in thought, or in speech, or in writing. The things that exist in their essences are those that the intellect has encompassed, none of which escapes it, for there exists nothing without an essence — and if a thing had no essence it would escape the compass of the intellect. The intellect has therefore encompassed all that has an essence. The soul, by contrast, has encompassed only what thought has presented for it to encompass. Since among things there is that which has an essence which thought has not presented for the soul to encompass, the soul has not encompassed all that has an essence. It therefore remains deficient below the rank of the intellect, which has encompassed all that has an essence, for there is nothing without an essence. This is among the clearest demonstrations of the intellect's perfection and the soul's deficiency. This is his saying.

I say: the discourse in this matter is not well ordered. What the Sage meant by that saying — if indeed he said it — was to show that existents fall into four divisions.

The first, that a thing exists in the essences of things: what he means is that among things there is that whose existence is outside the soul and which has a meaning in itself by which it is what it is — like the body, whose essence exists outside the soul, and whose meaning by which it is what it is in itself is length, breadth, and depth.

And he said, in thought: what he means is that among things there is that which has no essence outside the soul but exists in the mind — like genus, matter, place, and the circles traced in the soul from the motion of the celestial spheres by supposition, and the like.

And he said, in speech: what he means is that among things there is that whose existence is in speech — like propositions by affirmation and negation concerning essences, essences that are not changed thereby.

And he said, in writing: what he means is that among things there is that whose existence is in writing and whose existence is governed by it — like the overflowing divine matters from the world of intellect upon the Speakers and the sustained ones (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), which, if they are not fixed by composed letters and their forms not wholly deposited so that the soul may have through them a conception and an existence, their existence and the benefit of them fall away.

The Sage had restricted existents to these four divisions, though upon inquiry the divisions diminish and reduce to what lies beneath them. It may therefore be that the Sage wished by that saying to indicate that writing serves as a deputy, in exposition, for what exists in speech; that speech serves as a deputy for the soul in what it wishes to set forth; and that the soul likewise serves as a deputy for the things outside it by conceiving them, standing in their place through its attachment to them — in the sense of becoming like them through the acquisition of the meanings by which they are what they are. There is nothing in the Sage's words that would constitute proof of what the author of al-Nusra wished to support — namely, the deficiency of the one spoken of below that great degree. Had his words been carried upon the souls within the natural world that have risen in rank and become intellects standing in actuality, called the second revival, which still stand in potentiality beneath the first Emanated in the world of holiness, he would have been right.

**Chapter Thirty-Five of Part One**

Here the author of al-Nusra has made it obligatory, in his interpretation of the Sage's words, that the deficient one has thought. That is not sound, given that his discourse is upon the Second, of which the author of al-Islah speaks. For thought, in souls existing in the world of nature, is a power by which they acquire what they do not yet have the form of. This power ends when it is dispensed with: a soul does not need thought for what it has already encompassed, whose form it has obtained, and in which it stands in actuality. It needs thought only for what it has not yet encompassed — what it has not yet acquired the form of, the form through which it comes to stand in actuality and attains the degree of perfection and self-sufficiency. And when the soul at last dispenses with thought —

We say: the Second, from which the outpouring and the blessing descend upon the existents of the world — by which I mean the newborns — gave its perfection first and was enriched by what we have already set down concerning the perfection it attained at the very beginning of its existence, so that it became richer still. This being so, the partisanship of the author of al-Nusra against the author of al-Islah stands exposed in what he argued, and it becomes clear that the soundness of his words holds only for what is natural, not for what the Holy House contains.

The author of al-Nusra said: as for the one who held that the deficiency lies in the action of the soul and not in its essence — on the ground that the action of the soul cannot be completed except in time — this is a mistake and an error, for it does not follow that whatever is done in time is deficient. Were time the sole cause of deficiency in things, it would follow that whatever takes longer is the more deficient, and whatever takes less time is the more complete. Yet we observe the opposite in what is witnessed: the things that are more complete are often those accomplished in less time, and would be the less complete for taking longer. Had the matter been as the author of al-Islah described, the case would have to run to the contrary. Time, then, is not the cause of the deficiency of things or of their perfection. Rather, the deficiency of a thing comes from the deficiency of the agent, or from the deficiency of the matter, or from the deficiency of both together.

We say: the author of al-Islah neither claimed nor said that the action itself is deficient by being in time. What he said is that whatever attains its completeness only through time and duration is, until then, deficient; and that once it attains completeness, it is complete; and that everything present in what is witnessed has a completeness which, once received, makes it complete. He had already advanced the point that when he says the deficiency is in the action of the soul and not in its essence, his meaning is this: that the soul should do that by which it attains the degree of perfection from its own side and acquires the form through which it becomes complete by that soul's outpouring. His saying that the deficiency is in the action of the soul and not in its essence indicates nothing other than that the deficiency is in the receptive essence of the soul upon which the Second pours its outpouring — an essence that cannot receive all at once, but only across time: days, months, and years. This being so, the words of the author of al-Islah, taken on the ground on which he speaks, are sound; and the words of the author of al-Nusra, if carried upon the natural order, are also sound. Yet he wished only to be an advocate for his teacher.

> حشرهم الله وایانا مع الموالي صلوات الله عليهم

May God gather them and us together with the loyal, the blessings of God be upon them.

The author of al-Islah said: the Most High Creator, اَلْبَارِئ, originated the intellect and time together in a single act. The author of al-Nusra said: it is known that time is the measure of motion, and motion belongs only to the spheres. It must therefore follow from this saying that the spheres, and the time generated from their motions, were present with the intellect in a single act. It would then be permissible to say that the bodies capable of place and the corruptible beings were also present with the intellect in that single act. And if it is permissible to say that the intellect and time were together in one act, then by this analogy we are compelled to say that time receives of the virtues and the lights poured upon the intellect just as the intellect receives them — so that time too would be complete, which is impossible. Moreover, granting that the intellect and time were together in one act, they cannot but be either two things or a single thing. If they are a single thing, a plural expression does not apply to a single thing by its names, for it is impossible to say that the stone was with the rock, or the tide was with the knife. And if they are two distinct things, then the intellect grasped its own essence either without time or with time. If it grasped its essence without time, then it was without time; and if it grasped its essence only with time, then time precedes it — yet what the intellect precedes is not time, but eternity and pre-eternity.

We say: the substance of his words — though wanting in proof and demonstration — is that the coexistence of time with the intellect is impossible, and this is correct in our doctrine. We have already established that it is impossible for anything to be co-present with the first intellect, and that suffices for the point. The matter is therefore other than what the author of al-Islah stated in this section. But where the author of al-Nusra said that what the intellect precedes is not time but the eternal and the pre-eternal, he erred, for nothing at all stands ahead of the first intellect, as we have shown in what has gone before in this book and in the book رَاحَةُ الْعَقْلِ. What is correct — and upon which the law of belief and doctrine rests concerning the eternal and the pre-eternal — is that it belongs to the world of origination, as we have explained in the epistle known as al-Riyad. What we have set down there is sufficient without further length.

The author of al-Nusra cited the saying of Banidhaqlis (بَانْذَقْلِيس) in support of his argument: the intellect became complete because it submitted to nothing but اَلْبَارِئ, the Mighty and Majestic, while the soul became deficient because it submitted to nature.

We say: the philosophers — and the foremost among them, virtuous as they were in their own age — remained under the sway of error in much of what they discussed concerning the intelligibles. Had they lived in our time and seen the springs of grace overflowing from the House of Revelation with the realities of things as they truly are, according to the Alawite call, then, for all their striving on their own behalf, they would have done themselves justice. They would have withheld judgment on much of what they believed concerning what lies beyond the senses, and they would have been content to follow the traces of the one to whom God has granted a light in the hidden depths of things. In their own times, they followed their own opinions and intellects in what they pursued, so each of them arrived in his belief only at what his own writings indicate — much like the people of the outward meaning in the upright creed, who leaned upon their intellects in treading the paths of religion. Their opinions diverged, and among them there was disparity: some struck the truth more often, and some erred more often. The reliance is upon what the balance of religion attests, namely the horizons and the souls; and in what has already been said concerning this, and in the showing that such a belief as they held is false, there is enough without affectation or length. So Banidhaqlis brought forward nothing but what the author of al-Nusra brought forward in his argument, and the saying of Banidhaqlis is more fit to be set against as a reproach.

> ونسأل الله ان ينور عقولنا ، وَيَخْتِمُ بِالسَّعَادَةِ أُمُورَنَا ، وللخيرات والاعمال الخيرات الصالحات يوفقنا ، بمنه وجوده ولطفه وكرمه

We ask God to enlighten our intellects, to seal our affairs with happiness, and to grant us success in good deeds and in righteous, virtuous works, by His grace, His bounty, His kindness, and His generosity.

Now to the adjudication between the two disputants concerning the intellect that is the first origination.

The author of al-Islah said: motion and stillness are two effects united in the First and the Second. When the First originated, it became united with origination, and that union had two effects within it in potentiality. We say two effects because origination and the originated are two: the one being the essence of origination, the other the essence of the originated. Origination and the originated, though two, are not really two but one — the whole. These two effects are present in the First in potentiality. Then the Second is emitted from the First, and that emission has two effects within it by the intellect: the one being the essence of the emission, the other the essence of the emitted. So the two origins are united by the command. One of the two effects is motion, and it is higher than the other, which is stillness, just as one of the two origins — the First — is higher than the other, which is the Second. Motion is the First's bestowing upon the Second of all the existents, and stillness is the Second's reception from the First of all the existents and its comprehending of them. I do not say that motion and stillness are generated from the soul by the power derived from the intellect. Rather I say that they are two effects of the soul, by the power derived from the intellect, in matter and form; and it is for this that matter and form became the foundation of this world.

The author of al-Nusra said: this saying contains some raving, for motion occurs only for the sake of a thing sought. As for the motion of bodies, it is a seeking of places — the motion of fire upward seeks its place, and the motion of earth downward seeks its place — and the motion of spirits is a seeking of benefit. Whenever a seeking is established, a need is established there, for seeking arises only from some need. It is impossible to say that the intellect is in need of a thing such that it should move toward it. And even were it permissible to ascribe motion to the two origins, its ascription to the soul is more fitting than to the intellect, since the soul needs to draw benefit from the intellect, moving toward it with the motion of longing — the motion of the effect toward its cause. If it is established that motion belongs to the soul, then stillness belongs to the intellect, for the intellect is still in what it receives of the virtues and lights poured upon it by its principle, seeking no increase, because its principle has poured upon it lights and radiance in a measure beyond which the intellect knows no increase that it should move to seek. Yet the intellect has not poured upon the soul the whole of what was poured upon itself, but only a part, so that the soul might rest in what was poured upon it and grasp it; it has withheld a part for itself, so that the soul might move in seeking it. So motion arises only from need, the intellect is not in need such that it should move, the ascription of motion to the soul is more fitting than to the intellect, motion belongs to the soul and stillness to the intellect, and the intellect has not poured upon the soul all that it has but only a part, so as to be the cause of the soul's motion in seeking the rest. This is the sum of what both of them say.

We say: as for the saying of the author of al-Islah that motion and stillness are two effects united in the First and the Second — that in the First they are in potentiality and in the Second in actuality — this affirms that the First is at rest and in motion in potentiality, since motion and stillness are in it in potentiality, and that the Second is in motion and at rest in actuality, since motion and stillness are in it in actuality. That is impossible on several counts. The saying of the author of al-Nusra, that the intellect is not in need and therefore is not in motion, is correct, because motion in all its kinds is found only in natural bodies. The first and second intellects, being a first existence, are not bodies; it is therefore inadmissible that they be in motion or at rest. The proof is that a body requires that it be matter and form together, and that the one need the other in existence, so that its existence is not a first existence from God, glorified be He. For that whose existence is a first existence from God, glorified be He, is an essence with no otherness in it — being the very act which is the first existence from Him, exalted and magnified — and the essence of that act is one essence, not two. If its existence were not a first existence, another would have to precede it in existence. It follows that if the intellect were a body, another would precede it in existence; and it is void that anything precede the first intellect in its existence, since it is the very essence of the act which is the cause and the origination, and behind it there is nothing but God, the Originator, the Bringer-into-being, exalted be He, from whom is its existence. And if it is void that anything precede it in existence, it is void that it be a body; and if it is void that it be a body, and there is no motion except in what is a body, it is void that there be motion in the intellect; and if it is void that there be motion in it, the saying of the author of al-Islah that the intellect has motion in potentiality is annulled.

The author of al-Islah said: we said two effects because origination and the originated are two — the one being the essence of origination and the other the essence of the originated — and origination and the originated, though two, are yet not two but the whole.

This is a formulation that cannot be held, for the matter in origination and the originated is other than what he mentioned. They are not two. Origination is the originated, and the originated is origination, and there is nothing there but one. When it is referred to that from which its existence comes, it is origination — being an act belonging to that source and being the essence of the act; and when it is referred to its own self, it is the originated, being an effect of another from whom its existence comes.

Then, upon his saying that motion and stillness in the First are in potentiality and in the Second in actuality, we say: that whose existence is a first existence is nothing but pure act and the very truth of act. Were it admissible that motion and stillness be in the First in potentiality, the ruling of the Second would be nobler than that of the First — since the ruling of what subsists in actuality, in nobility, is not as the ruling of what subsists in potentiality — and it is impossible that the Second precede the First in nobility. What he mentioned belongs rather to the attributes of that whose existence comes later, which needs, in its coming forth, both act and something already subsisting in actuality. The first intellect is intellect in actuality, and so likewise is the second, and what follows it of the intellects that are separate from matter. It is inadmissible to hold, of what necessarily belongs to an existent of the realm of nature and is proper to it, that it applies to what lies outside that realm among the high limits.

The author of al-Islah said: motion and stillness are two effects of the soul, by the power derived from the intellect, in matter and form.

What is meant is that bodies — which are matter and form taken together — are moved, those among them that are moved, by the natural force whose presence within them comes from what lies outside the first and second intellects. This is a motion of longing. So when he placed the two effects, the mover and the moved, in matter and form, the claim taken in that way is impossible; yet it is sound in the sense that what is learned from the intellect is learned because its existence is from the intellect. Understood thus, it is correct, and this is the best interpretation to which his words may be referred.

In the fifth section of Part Two, the author of al-Nusra said: Motion belongs to the soul and stillness to the intellect, so it is not permissible to believe such a thing of those transcendent limits.

We say in summary: the world of origination, which gathers the Preceding and the Following, and the pure intellects, which are the upper letters separate from bodies, are too high and exalted for motion or stillness to be imagined in them. For motion, in whatever is moved, comes only from a mover within it that is other than itself, and by that mover the existence of its essence subsists. When a thing's essence exists only through the existence of differing things, its essence is an essence of deficiency and need. But the Following, which he named the soul, belongs to the world of origination, which is distinguished by completion and perfection; its essence is not an essence of deficiency or need. It is therefore void that there be within its essence any differing thing; and if it is void that there be upon its essence what is other than itself, then it is void that it be other than itself, void that it be moved, and void that there be motion in it.

Moreover, motion is that by which the thing in which it resides is found passing from its first state to a second, whether in its essence or in its states. But the Following, which he named the soul, has an eternal existence, since it falls, by way of origination, in the Second, being an intellect. And since it is eternal, its transfer from that upon which it was first found is impossible; so it is void that there be motion in it. What is said of stillness is exactly what is said of motion: the First intellect has no stillness, and the Following, which he named the soul, has no motion.

Rather, by what he said the author of al-Nusra intended those souls and intellects that are in the House of Nature, sent forth by a second emanation; the generated thing is moved, according to what we explained in our well-known book Rahat al-Aql. For motion and stillness are proper to the world of body and what is in it, since they are among the causes of its perfection, and not to what belongs to the House of Origination. But he transferred the meaning, pressing it too far into the deficiency he inclined toward in support of his sheikh. May God house them in the abode of His honor, and gather them, and us, with our pure masters (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

In the sixth section of Part Two, the author of al-Nusra said: Motion is for need, and it is more proper to the soul than to the intellect.

It has already been shown from the foregoing words that motion and stillness befit only what is a body or is in a body. The First and the Second of whom we are speaking are not a body, so the attribution of motion and stillness to them is barred, as we have shown earlier in our well-known book Rahat al-Aql. His statement holds true of what is natural, by way of the second emanation, not of what is originated, whose existence is by the first emanation. Nor is motion in everything for need, as he mentioned. Motion in a natural thing may be among the causes of its perfection — such as the motion of the spheres: their motions are not for a need in their own essence to attain what they were not granted at the first instant of their creation, but rather for a completeness belonging to them. Such too is the motion of the اَلنَّاطِق and his striving to gather the word upon worship, and his exertion in the affairs of governance; that is out of a completeness required of him, because something is required of him by which he is completed after attaining perfection and rising to the rank of اَلنَّاطِق while he yet exists. Since this is so, the saying of the author of al-Nusra — taken absolutely — that motion is for need, is not exhaustive. His ruling is sound only of some natural things, on the ground that motion is inseparable from them, as we have mentioned.

In the seventh section of Part Two, the author of al-Nusra said: He does not pour out all that he has upon the soul, but only a part.

What he meant is that the First is distinguished by its rank of precedence and the loftiness of its station — distinguished by qualities not shared by anything below it, which he calls the soul. That is the best his words can be made to mean by interpretation. If, however, the matter stood as he described, and each of the حُدُود withheld from the next some portion of what it holds, then one might imagine that the blessings reaching the Prophet (ع) from the world of al-Quds (قُدْس) as an outpouring were not conveyed by him in their fullness to the wasi and his followers — that he taught them to no one, so that their benefit was lost and came to nothing. That would make the wasi (ع) doubtful in his perfection, his merit, and his standing in the place of the Prophet (ع), and the obedience owed to him would become doubtful as an obligation — reduced to mere conjecture. To believe such a thing is not permissible: not of the اَلنَّاطِق, not of the wasi, and, in sum, not of the حُدُود.

Rather, I say: the blessings are poured out and bestowed from the side of all the حُدُود. When any of those blessings by which perfection is attained in all existents are absent, the cause is not withholding on the part of the حُدُود, but the incapacity of the receivers to receive them. Each receives the blessings according to the strength of the receiver: some receive fully, and some receive only what they are able. This is found among natural things, among compassionate teachers and eager learners. It is not permitted to any of the حُدُود to withhold its benefit from one who would draw benefit, so long as he is a receiver standing in his right, when he wills, out of fear that he might rise to a rank; for that would be before the spiritual birth. Concerning this, the اَلنَّاطِق spoke from God the Exalted, and said:

> ولا تقتلوا اولادكم خشية املاق ومن فعل ذلك فقد أثم وتعدى طوره واسقط نفسه عن مرتبة الفاضلين

Do not kill your children for fear of poverty; whoever does that has sinned, has overstepped his proper stage, and has cast himself down from the rank of the excellent.

How, then, could it be permitted to imprison the spiritual wealth — the sciences and the luminous blessings — away from their rightful owners, when the proof of their deserving stands, and the Prophet (ع) has commanded that it be handed over to them, saying — exalted is He who says:

> فَإِنْ آنَسْتُم مِّنْهُمْ رُشْدًۭا فَٱدْفَعُوٓا۟ إِلَيْهِمْ أَمْوَلَهُمْ وَلَا تَأْكُلُوهَآ إِسْرَافًۭا وَبِدَارًا

If you find in them sound judgment, hand over to them their wealth, and do not consume it wastefully and in haste.

No indeed: the blessings are poured out only that they may reach those who deserve them and be settled in their proper place, as I have explained in my hermeneutic epistles and in my Basran and Baghdadi assemblies. May God make me, and a company of my brethren, those who render the trust to its owners and fulfill its conditions. Perhaps the author of al-Nusra, by way of analogy, advanced what he advanced.

In the eighth section of Part Two, the author of al-Nusra said: Action, moreover, is the substance of encompassing, and by it things are encompassed; and this is not the condition of what is moved, for the moved does not encompass things, but rather needs something at rest, at the utmost of rest, so that from its rest it may encompass things. Were the intellect moved, it would encompass nothing, and the soul would encompass nothing by drawing benefit from it. And since we found it encompassing all things, nothing escaping it, and found that whoever encompasses does so by what he attains and gains of its lights and its excellences, we knew then that the intellect is at the utmost of rest, and no motion is imagined in it; while the soul moves only to reach the innermost of the intellect, and then to be still. It does not move so as to encompass, by drawing benefit from the intellect, what it wishes to encompass. So long as the soul is in motion it does not encompass, nor does it attain the excellences and the lights, until it is still by drawing benefit from it; then it encompasses things. The soul, then, is moved with a motion of subsistence, coming to rest by drawing benefit from the one who is at rest. This is what he said.

I say: the words have already passed that show the intellect is not moved, and they spare me from lengthening the argument here. It does not follow, from the intellect's being unmoved, that it must be at rest in the manner of bodies — for bodies are not free of being either at rest or moved, whereas the intellect's existence is not like the existence of bodies. What has already passed is sufficient, and it indicates that motion and stillness do not befit the intellect. As for what he made necessary by his saying — that so long as the soul is in motion it encompasses nothing and attains no excellences — the meaning is not sound: not of what is separate from the Second, as I mentioned, nor of what is not separate. For the souls in the House of Nature acquire only by being moved with their bodies, through the use of the senses, and inference, and drawing benefit. He means that the matter is so when a proof of it is established; and that suffices as a statement of the persistence of the error in it.

In the ninth section of Part Two, he then said: The soul is moved with a motion of subsistence, coming to rest by drawing benefit from the one who is at rest. He is truthful in this if he assigns it a meaning, and in error if it is carried upon the surface of his words. He is truthful if he takes the soul to mean the natural souls. On that reading, his statement that the soul is moved is true, because the natural souls — which subsist in potentiality — are moved for the sake of acquisition. His phrase with a motion of subsistence is true, because their acquisition drives them toward subsistence. His phrase it comes to rest by drawing benefit is true, because the soul passes from potentiality to actuality by acquiring and drawing benefit and hunting out cognitions and excellences, in knowledge and in action. And his phrase from the one who is at rest means: from the one who subsists in actuality. He is in error if his words are taken at face value, for the Second is not moved, nor is the intellect at rest as bodies are at rest — as though both were of the House of Body, upon which motion and stillness are compelled.

Care must be turned to knowing the ranks of the حُدُود, so that the matter of conceiving things in their realities does not fall into disorder and lead to the corruption of the substance of souls. May God illumine my intellect by their aid, and grant me the happiness of following the pure ones through the stages of the divine sciences and the treasuries of the holy lights (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

## 7. Soul, Spirit, and the Parts of the Whole

The judgment now to be given among the ranks concerns what has been said about the soul and about اَلْهَيُولَى, the dark prime matter — whether either of them resembles the First, or neither does.

**The First Section**

The author of al-Islah said:

What the author of al-Nusrah attributes to us is not our position. We do not say that nature and the dark prime matter are born from the Second in the way that the Second is born from the First. For that world is the world of honor and virtue, while this world is the world of darkness and turbidity. The Second, though it is born from the First, resembles him — but al-Hayuli's condition is nothing like the Second's.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

This is an error. If that world were wholly luminous with no darkness in it, and this world wholly dark with no light in it, no connection could exist between them. Rather, we say that the soul has two extremities. One faces the intellect and lies on the intellect's horizon — this is its luminous, noble extremity. The other faces nature, and nature lies on its horizon — this is the extremity in which some darkness and turbidity reside, because nature falls on its horizon. Likewise, we say that nature has two extremities: one faces the partial soul and carries some light, because it falls on the soul's horizon; and the other faces the elements, and it is the dark, turbid one in which there is no light. By these divisions we speak of soul and of nature, of their darkness and their light. We do not say that that world is wholly a world of light with no darkness in it, nor that this world is wholly a world of darkness with no light in it. For if it were as he described, there would be between them neither neighboring nor coupling.

He said:

The statement is therefore sound that the dark اَلْهَيُولَى is born from the soul without resembling it, just as the soul is born from the First without resembling him. اَلْهَيُولَى became dark solely because of its remoteness from the First Cause, owing to its being born from the soul. Likewise, the deficiency befell the soul only because it is more remote from the First Cause — I mean the Antecedent. This is his statement.

We say:

What the author of al-Islah actually said in his book differs from what the author of al-Nusrah has made of it. The kernel of his statement — which is a response to the claim of the author of al-Mahsul that the Second is born from the First and does not resemble him — is that he said: We do not say thus. Rather, we say that the Second is born from the First, does not resemble him, yet is his counterpart. Though the First is of loftier rank and nobler station, the two stand as male and female stand across all genera and substances. For the male, though higher and nobler than the female, is still the counterpart of the female and her likeness.

Then we say:

The Follower is too noble and too lofty for its way with what comes after it to be as its way with the First. The First and the Second are two counterparts, and they are the mine of honor, of perfection, and of exaltation — there is no honor above them. God has made them the mine of perfect honor, of perfect light, of perfect exaltation, and of perfect knowledge, with no ignorance there, no turbidity, and no darkness whatsoever. The two of them are the foundations of the higher world, which is the mine of purity, of al-Quds (sanctity), and of life. اَلْهَيُولَى is a trace of the Second, which is the substance of this world; it is a turbid, dark substance characterized by neither mercy nor power. The Second brought اَلْهَيُولَى forth in act from itself, by the power derived from the First; then it brought forth from it the form, which is nature, in act.

Then the author of al-Islah said:

It is not permissible to say that اَلْهَيُولَى is born from the Second. Rather, we say that it is a trace of it, and therefore does not resemble it. The Second is born from the First and does resemble him, even though the First is of loftier rank. This is his statement.

Its gist is this: the Second issues from the First and is like him, even though the First is loftier in rank; and اَلْهَيُولَى is a trace of the Second, its substance dark and turbid, because al-Hayuli's relation to the Second is not as the Second's relation to the First. For the First and the Second are counterparts, whereas اَلْهَيُولَى does not resemble the Second, since it is a trace of it.

If this is the gist of his statement, then what the author of al-Nusrah answered is no answer. The author of al-Islah did not claim in his statement that the whole of that world is light and radiance. What he said, describing the condition of the First and the Second, is that God Most High made them the mine of honor and exaltation. That statement is no proof that nothing exists outside a condition like theirs. The demonstration already given — that اَلْهَيُولَى is not born from the Second — is sufficient, and there is no need to extend the discourse and prolong it with what might turn suspicions upon us regarding what is not our aim in this section.

We say:

اَلْهَيُولَى does not resemble the Second, nor the First, because its existence, in its issuing from the First, is by the lower proportion that we set forth in our earlier words and in our book known as رَاحَةُ الْعَقْلِ, the Peace of Mind. And in that is sufficiency.

**The Second Section of the Third Book**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

This statement is an error, because if that world were luminous with no darkness in it, and this world dark with no light in it, then no connection would fall between this world and that world.

We say:

This statement is an error in that it attributes to the author of al-Islah something he did not say — but in its own wording it is sound. For among the world of origination and emanation there is that which is shared: it accords with what is above it by its being emanated, and accords with what is below it by its being a body. It possesses two matters. It does not resemble the First from which its existence comes; it resembles the Second from one side and does not resemble it from another. The side from which it resembles is its being emanated from it; and the side from which it resembles is its being a natural body. That is the nature which, when it is joined to the world of origination and emanation, is a life flowing from that world into what is below it, granting each thing what it is fit to receive of persistence and conception; and when it is joined to this world, it is a principle of motion and rest in the thing in which it inheres, essentially, not by way of accident. So nature came to be, even though in its existence it is emanated, a medium between the two emanations, the First and the Second.

**The Third Section of the Third Book**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

By the soul, meaning the Second, it has two extremities: one extremity toward the intellect and upon its horizon, and it is the luminous, noble extremity; and one extremity toward nature, and it lies upon its horizon, and it is the one in which there is some darkness.

We say:

That is not sound. The proof is that whatever has extremities is a thing with dimensions measured by area. But the Second — which he named a soul for reasons we have already explained — is not a body; and if it is not a body, it is impossible that it should have two extremities. Moreover, his statement commits him to holding that it has a luminous extremity on the horizon of the intellect, and an extremity of some darkness on the horizon of nature — differing from the intellect — which means, by what he mentioned, that the First is upon the horizon of nature. But it is impossible for there to be in the Second anything by which it differs from the First, because its existence, in its emanation, proceeds from the nobler proportion that is in the First, namely its being a cause.

We have already said that the First, by its being existent, is not in its essence both cause and effect: it is a cause in relation to what is below it — that is the nobler proportion in the First — and an effect in relation to itself — that is the lower proportion in the First. From its intellection of its essence as being a cause, there emanates from it its like, since it is in the nature of a cause that its effect come to be from it; and from its intellection of its essence as being an effect, there emanates from it that which is not its like, that emanation proceeding from the lower proportion in the First, namely its being an effect. For the honor of the effect's proportion exceeds the cause's proportion: from the cause there results elevation, and from the effect there results only what is below the effect that exists from the cause.

Witness to what we have said is the condition of al-Asas, the Foundation — the likeness that stands for the Second in the knowledge of the body, and its not being different from al-Natiq, the Speaker. For all that is existent in the Foundation is existent in the Speaker from whom the existence of the Foundation came; and thus it is not different from him. Yet not all that belongs to the Speaker is existent in the Foundation, for the Speaker has that by which the Foundation differs from him — namely, the vision in which the Foundation does not share. It has become clear, therefore, by the Foundation's being, through what exists in it, a sharer with the Speaker, and by there being found in it nothing that is not found in the Speaker, that it is not different from him. And when it is not different from him, and it is the likeness by which, and by whose rank, the existence of the Second and its rank are inferred, then the Second is not different from the First; and when it is not different, then its existence as he described it — upon the horizon of nature — is impossible.

**The Fourth Section of the Third Book**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

اَلْهَيُولَى became dark only because of its remoteness from the First Cause, on account of its being born from the soul.

We say:

Al-Hayuli's being unlike the First — and the Second's being so too — is not because of remoteness from the First on the score of the proportion upon which the First's condition stands, namely its being an effect. For the First is a confluence of two proportions: a proportion by its being a cause, and a proportion by its being an effect. What comes into existence from the effect-proportion is not like what comes from the cause-proportion, for the cause is forever nobler than the effect and prior to it in rank and in nature. Of the First it is said that it is a cause and that it is an effect; yet it is a single essence, which acquires these proportions through relations.

**The Fifth Section of the Third Book**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

The deficiency befell the soul only because it is more remote from the First Cause, I mean the Antecedent.

We say:

There is no deficiency in the Second — which he named a soul — as we have already stated, nor is it remote from the First; rather it is its like. The deficiency is a necessary consequence, not the very substance of nature; and nature became deficient not from the side of its own essence, which is its perfection, but from the side of its second perfection, which it acquires by way of benefit.

**The Sixth Section of the Third Book**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

The proportion of nature's being born from the soul is no greater than the soul's being born from the intellect, since the soul in respect of the intellect is less than nature in respect of the soul. And if it is admissible that the soul be born from the intellect, with its perfection, its nobility, and its nearness to the First Cause, then it is admissible that there be born from the soul the like of nature — for nature is more similar to the soul in all its conditions, in its acts and its powers, than the soul is similar to the intellect in its acts and its powers. Among the ancients, nature is defined as the principle of motion; some of them said it is the active power. Consider nature under these two definitions and you will find in it the ampler portion and the more abundant share. For the soul, among the ancients, is the moving, abiding substance, marked withal with intellect; yet there is nothing in the intellect of the mark of the soul's definition, nor is the soul defined by the definition of the intellect. The definition of the intellect is that it is the first of things, that it is the Originated, that it is the first effect, and the first intermediary; and it is not admissible to say that the soul is the first of things, nor that it is originated, nor that it is the first effect. So if it is admissible that there be born from the intellect, with its perfection, its nobility, and its nearness to the Word, the like of the soul, which is not defined by the definition of the intellect — and this cannot be denied — then it is equally admissible that there be born from the soul the like of nature, which resembles in its definitions the definitions of the soul — and this too cannot be denied. And were one to look upon the matter from the aspect of reflection, this degree of knowledge would not be hidden. Had the author of al-Islah attended to the wonders and wisdom in nature, and to the splendor, nobility, beauty, and comeliness of its acts, by reason of which the partial soul desires to incline toward nature and to follow it, he would have known and been certain that nature is born from the soul, even though it does not resemble it — for causes forever find beautiful the order of their effects. From this the partial soul came to desire to follow nature, and to know that every beauty, splendor, nobility, and adornment is from the higher, noble world. For had nature not been born from the soul, it could never have spoken of any beauty, comeliness, or adornment; but because it was born from the soul, it told of what it saw in the world of its cause — the beauty and nobility there — a deficient telling, falling short of the telling of the soul. So know this. This is his statement.

We say:

In what has already been discussed — the discourse on the intellect, the soul, and nature, on the existence that precedes other existence, and on what lies beside that — the sound answer to what has been raised is already held and enclosed. We need not prolong it with further demonstrations against the errors in conception that persist. In that is sufficiency.

**The Fourth Book of the Book of Riyadh, on the judgment among the two contenders concerning what has been said of the soul's being parts and traces**

**The First Section of the Fourth Book**

The author of al-Islah said:

Regarding what the author of al-Nusrah has established: the parts that are within us are traces of the First Substance, not parts of it. Our union is through the traces that come from the First Substance, not through its parts; and the union of the First is not through its parts, because the union of the First is through the very essence of the Word. The parts within us, though they are traces of the First, are not parts of it and are not united with it; rather, they are united with its traces, as we have shown of the union of its products with our traces within them. The union of the First Substance with the Word is not of this kind, for the First Substance was united with the very essence of the Word until it and the Word became one thing. This is his statement.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

This statement contains confusion and manifest contradictions.

This statement is confused, and its contradictions are plain. For he claimed that the parts within us are traces of the First Substance. But how can the parts be traces, when they are substances that subsist in their own right, while traces have no subsistence of their own? If he calls the parts within us traces of the First Substance, then, having already applied to them the name "parts," he is bound to show of what substance they are parts. Had they not been parts of the First Substance, he would not have called them parts of any substance at all; and once it is established that they are parts of some substance, it becomes void to call them traces, for the parts of a thing are not traces of something other than it. This is his statement.

We say:

The author of al-Nusrah is right in what he said: that the souls are substances subsisting in themselves after acquisition, and that it is impossible for traces to subsist in themselves. But the author of al-Islah, in what he said and in his refusal to give the soul the name "part," acted as he did because he saw that the things which admit division are bodies — bodies that are never free of the nine accidents and are grasped by the five senses. The soul is not a body, and neither is what flows upon it. He would not therefore give it the name "part," lest he run it in the course of bodies — for that would no longer match what its own essence is and what its essence has taught him of it. Unwilling to do that, he said "traces," and named them as that by which the souls become substantial and endure. What he means is that these souls have their substantiality and their endurance through the traces of those separate substances that reach them by way of the حُدُود, the intermediaries (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). This being so, it is clear that the author of al-Islah, in saying "traces," means what runs in the soul the course of the form — that by which the substantiality of the souls is completed and by which they endure when they receive it. This is what he was obliged to affirm when he says: we said that honor and merit belong over these worlds only because we received the trace of that noble substance in full, and were prepared to receive it. It is thus established that by "trace" he means that which receives; and that the author of al-Nusrah, in his word "part," means what runs in the soul the course of the اَلْهَيُولَى — that which receives; and that both are a likening and an approximation to the understanding.

The Second Section

As for the author of al-Nusrah's saying that the souls are parts of the Universal Soul, this rests on no proof that holds. Whatever has been divided, its whole is the totality of those parts; and if its whole is the totality of those parts, then he has made it necessary that the souls be parts of the Universal Soul. But as for the souls in the abode of this world, what has been established by proof, in what came before and after, makes it plain that the Second — which he named the Soul — has its existence not in the abode of the body, which is the abode of this world. Since it is not in this world, it is void that the Second be a whole of these souls; and once it is void that it be a whole of these souls, these souls are not parts of it.

The Third Section

Moreover, his assertion that they are parts of the Universal Soul does not hold together with his own intention that the Universal Soul is the Second. For if the soul has parts belonging to the Second, the matter cannot escape one of these: either the whole of them — which is the Second — is in the abode of this world; or some of them are in this world and some in the higher world; or it is not, in the sense of partition, in this world at all. It is void that the whole which is the Second should be, in its entirety, in the abode of this world. For what is in this world subsists by potentiality and has no passage into actuality except through what already subsists in actuality; and the First Intellect is precluded from being that by which the souls subsisting in potentiality pass into actuality, because of what that would require of the precedence of something whose existence is impossible for God, glory be to Him, according to what has been discussed in a book other than this. And it is void, too, that some be in this world and some in the higher world. For the part in the higher world, were it like this one in the world, subsisting by potentiality just as this one's condition is, would be voided by the proof already established — that the existence of the Second is an existence subsisting in actuality, not like the existents of the abode of this world, which subsist by potentiality. So, once the two divisions are void, it is established that the Second is nothing of partition in this world, and it is void that the souls be parts of it.

The Fourth Section

Then, concerning parts: whatever is given to a whole is given to all its parts, in all their states, according to their measure. If these souls were parts of the Second, and we have found them neither knowing nor wise, then the whole whose parts are of this description would be of this same measure; and to hold such a belief concerning that mighty حَدّ is unbelief. We seek refuge in God — since it is known that the materials of the Messengers (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) are from it, and that it is the proximate cause of the overflowing upon the lower world. The souls, then, are not parts of the Following. Since it is void that these souls be parts, we say that the souls are of the nature of those separate things whose existence is an originated existence; their manifestation and their existence are only from between the two worlds, the natural and the emanated. It is a natural power that attains, by what it acquires from the direction of the حُدُود through obedience and the two worships, so that there is emanated from its essence an abiding, abstracted form — one that resembles the abstracted things in the abode of the Hereafter on one side, and differs from them on another.

Hence we say:

It is the second emanation, and our epistles on the Return contain, God willing, the mention of that.

The Fifth Section

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Likewise, our traces united with our products do not receive our traces after we have finished the work; nor do the products prepared to receive them from us accept anything further, except by the entry of corruption upon them and their change from their states — because they are not parts of us, nor are they substances subsisting in themselves; rather they are vanishing accidents. This is his statement.

We say:

What is meant by this is that bodies reach a limit in receiving what they can receive, a limit beyond which they receive nothing further. It is like iron held in the fire: once it reddens, that is its utmost, and it grows no hotter; and like a black body, which grows no blacker, for it may reach a limit of blackness beyond which it is not further blackened. That, in meaning, is more fitting to be held than what his own expression requires. It is not the traces that are the receiver; the receiver is other than them, namely the body set down for the succession of accidents and traces upon it.

The Sixth Section

The author of al-Nusrah said:

As for the rational souls within us, they cease not to acquire from the light of the higher, noble world, and to increase thereby in nobility in their essence, without any change occurring in their essence, or any corruption entering upon them from the Soul when it wishes to benefit them. So it is established that they are parts and substances of the Soul, not traces of it.

We say:

The matter of the soul's being unlimited in receiving is as he said, and it is one of the proofs that the soul is not a body. But the matter of the soul's being parts of the Second is not as he held and set forth by what it entails and encloses, of what has already been discussed. The best to which the matter can be ascribed, in his saying that, for one who intends by "parts" a sound meaning, is that these souls, which are the receivers, when they intellect their own essences by that which is such, then they and what they intellect are one thing: it is that, and that is it, distinguished from it only by rank. So the soul is like the part of a thing in which is found what is found in the whole of it, their way in their essence being one and the same from this side.

The Seventh Section

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Again, to say that the rational souls existing in this world are not parts of the Universal Soul nullifies prophethood, the imamate, the executorship, revelation, and the sacred laws. For it would not then be permitted to the soul to grasp that which influences it, or to encompass it, or to benefit from it. Yet the prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) claimed that they beheld the noble upper world, and stood upon the spiritual حُدُود, and benefited from what they delivered to those after them of light and clear exposition, from those above them, the two roots and the three branches; and that they beheld the angels. If their pure souls were traces of the Universal Soul, they would not have been able to comprehend what was revealed to them, nor to stand upon the spiritual entities which are parts of the Universal Soul.

We say:

It has already been said that the souls are neither parts nor traces, but a second emanation; that the meaning of the one who called them "traces" is that by which the substantiality of the souls is completed and which earns them endurance; and that the meaning of the one who called them "parts" is that, by their intellecting their own essence in that manner, they are like those things which are their causes. Neither prophethood is nullified by this, nor the imamate, nor anything else, for it is established that these ranks are not from this side. The prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) were able to grasp those things absent from them, from the upper world, only after they reached the rank of perfection, so that by it they came to be at the utmost of completeness; and when they return to their own essences, it is as though they had grasped those transcendent essences, being, from the side of completeness, one thing with them, with no difference except in rank. We have set out, in our book known as *Rahat al-Aql*, enough of that to satisfy and to heal, for whoever would know it.

The Eighth Section

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Rather, the necessary thing to say is that the Intellect is originated through the Word, that the Soul is the image of the Intellect, that nature is born of the Soul, and that the rational souls are parts of the Universal Soul, so that by this order التَّوْحِيد, reward, punishment, prophethood, the imamate, the sacred laws, and revelation may become sound.

We say:

The soundness of التَّوْحِيد, of reward and punishment, of prophethood and the imamate, of the mission and of the laws drawn from revelation, and their establishment, is not from the side that the Intellect is originated through the Word and that the Soul is the image of the Intellect. Rather, their establishment is from another side, which we have set out in our epistles and our writings concerning them. As for the Intellect, it is not permitted to say that it is originated through the Word, for that would require the precedence of the Word over the Intellect — a requirement that disorders the paths of التَّوْحِيد, according to what we have expounded in our discourse.

Then we say:

The Intellect is nothing but the essence of the Word, and the Word is nothing but the essence of the Intellect. This is confirmed by the saying of our master the Imam al-Muizz li-Din Allah (ع), in the book *Tawil al-Sharia*, where he speaks of the intelligibles: that the Word is an expression of the Intellect, and that these names are merited by it by relation. And there has already preceded, in our exposition and our discourse upon the Originator, the Intellect, the Word, and what is said of the world of origination and of the first emanation, in *al-Risala al-Mudia* and in the rest of the epistles, what suffices the inquirer. As for the Soul and nature, they are two emanations from the First Intellect, each of them according to its own relation; and for that reason one of them became more excellent than the other, which is the first emanation, while the rational souls are the second emanation. What ran in the first of them is emanated in the last of them. Our singular epistles on the Return contain, of the mention of that, what makes length unnecessary, for expansiveness of speech upon the like of that, in every place, is forbidden; a gesture suffices the seeking inquirer, while another discards it for the difficulty of its reach and the hardness of its knowledge. God grants the knowing believer success to know that according to his strength in sincerity to God and to His friends, and aids him when he is sincere to His face, exalted be He, and gives his allegiance to Him and to His friends. May God make us among the followers who receive, who keep faith with His covenant, and grant us success to praise Him, by His grace and His majesty.

## 8. The Human Microcosm

**The First Section**

The author of al-Islah said:

Man is the fruit of this whole world, and the world was brought into being in all its parts, from its very origins and foundations, for his sake. First among these parts is prime matter (اَلْهَيُولَى), the principle from which individuals arise through motion and rest. From those individuals come the vehicles; from the compounds that follow, all that this world generates; and from the natures that combine, the human form is composed — a form made ready to receive the three souls: the vegetative, the sensitive, and the speaking.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

To say that human beings are the fruit of this world is the saying of the materialists as well — those who denied the survival of the soul after its separation from the body. For if human beings were the fruit of this world, this world would hold them fast and would not release them to pass on to another.

But we would say:

Human beings are the fruit of the highest world, the noble and luminous world. This is precisely why the prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) came with their warning: so that men might provision themselves from the house of mortality for the house of permanence, and from this world for the abode of the Return. The prophets knew that this world is not a lasting home, and that man is not its fruit. So he said.

We say:

Each of the two claimants speaks truly from one side and falls short from another. Both are truthful insofar as each affirms something that genuinely belongs to the human being. Both fall short insofar as each denies him the other portion that is equally his — his being the fruit of both worlds together. The human being issues from between the two worlds. From this world he receives his first perfection; from that world he receives his second.

The end of the natural world is to bring its existents to their first perfection: to bring forth the bodily form by means of the revolving bodies, the luminous planets, and the proximate causes, disposing that form to receive what belongs to it. And how should it not receive it, being its fruit, beyond which scarcely anything further remains? The end of that other world is to bring what has already received its first perfection to its second: the overflowing upon the spiritual form — the thing made ready by nature — of that by which it becomes substantial and endures. That is the knowledge of the Oneness of Allah the Exalted by means of all the حُدُود, and obedience to them in the two modes of worship, in knowledge and in deed. That is his fruit.

What confirms what we have said is the word of our master, the Commander of the Faithful, al-Muizz li-Din Allah اَلْمَعَاد ibn Ismail (ع), in the book *The Interpretation of the Sharia*:

> ان الانسان هو غرض الثاني ونهاية فعل الطبيعة

that man is the purpose of the second world and the end of the action of nature.

**The Second Section**

As for the statement of the author of al-Nusrah — his objection that if human beings were the fruit of this world, this world would hold them fast and not release them to pass on to another — he has in fact guided his opponent to the reversal of the matter against himself.

So his opponent says:

If, as I have maintained, man is the fruit of the higher world, then that world would hold him fast and not release him — would not send these persons back below, to be forgotten and to pass away, as I have stated and held necessary. Yet this is not a statement that proves in the abstract what he intends to establish. Nor does what he adduced — the sending of the prophets and their command to men to provision themselves — prove that human beings are the fruit of this world. For the human being who is truly human is such by the gathering of the overflow of both worlds: from the natural world, what runs in the channel of prime matter; and from the world of the Intellect, what runs in the channel of form. The one comes by intermediaries, and the other by intermediaries.

**The Third Section**

The author of al-Islah said:

The Soul emanates from the First Intellect in perfection, and prime matter emanates from the Soul in perfection, and form emanates from prime matter in perfection. So the whole was perfect.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

If the matter stands as he says — that the Soul is perfect like the Intellect — then what he says about what is spread across prime matter, form, and the compounds is void and self-contradictory. Fashioned things cannot come to be except from the pairing of one that is perfect and self-sufficient with another that is deficient and in need, so that a union may take place between them — as we see among things we can witness, such as the heavens and the earth. The heavens stand in the rank of the male: perfect and self-sufficient in all their motions and manifest orbits. The earth stands in the rank of the female, deficient, her need established within her. For this reason a union took place between them, and from between them appeared the various kinds of metals with their differing forms, and the plants, and the animals — none of which is actually present in the heavens, nor in the earth, nor in the mothers, but all of which were present in them in potency. When the two were joined, all appeared in actuality. Had their state been otherwise, nothing would have appeared from them. So he said.

We say:

The statement of the author of al-Islah that the Soul is the second, emanating from the First Intellect in perfection, is correct, as was established above. But his statement that prime matter emanates from the Soul we have already addressed: its existence is not from the Soul alone, which is the second; rather its existence is from the First Intellect and the second together, neither being prior to the other, and neither having existence in isolation from the other except within the soul by way of imagination. Outside the soul the two are found only together.

As for the statement of the author of al-Nusrah — that fashioned things cannot come to be except from the pairing of one that is perfect with another that is deficient, as he illustrated with the heavens and the earth — he has already refuted himself in the words that follow. He says that from between them appeared the various kinds of metals with their forms, and the plants, and the animals, none of which is actually present in the heavens or the earth or the mothers, because they were present only in potency. To say this is to say that the generated beings, even if not actually present in the heavens and the earth, are present in potency in both alike — and so no superiority of one over the other appears.

It is not the case that when existents come into being from between two things, one must be perfect and the other deficient. We have shown that deficiency is disclosed not in things as they are in themselves, but in their ranks when set beside others, and in their acts when those acts are completed only through something else. In themselves, each is perfect. If they were not perfect in the existence that befits them, no act could proceed from them, whether by themselves or through another. The state of things in the world bears this out. The heaven, which he singles out for perfection and mastery, is deficient in its act: its act is completed only by that upon which it revolves. The earth, which he singles out for deficiency and need, has her deficiency not in herself but in her act, which is completed only by that which revolves upon her. The same holds for the animal and for newborns.

This being so, the claim of the author of al-Nusrah — that the statement of the author of al-Islah is contradictory and biased — is itself the contradictory and biased statement.

**The Fourth Section**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

He did not answer what was raised at the end of this section: that the name of perfection, اَلتَّمَامِيَّة, falls upon the Originator, glorified be He, on account of its falling upon the complete origination.

We say:

The author of al-Islah mentioned nothing of the sort. What he quoted from the sages in the book of al-Islah — that the Originator whose origination is complete is complete, and that the one who is the perfection could only be perfect — is a saying than which none is more sound. By those words he affirmed that the perfect and the perfected are the origination and the originated, not the Originator, glorified be He; and that the name of perfection does not, by what he mentioned, fall upon the Originator, glorified be He; and that nothing of the sort is folded into his words. The claim of the author of al-Nusrah here is a bias against the author of al-Islah.

**The Fifth Section**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

All of us say that origination is the cause of the originated, and that it, together with the originated, from the side of the origination, is one single thing — and it is perfect — and that what is originated by the complete origination can only be perfect.
