Nor are we obliged to say that the Originator, glorified be He, is perfect, or that He is a cause, or that He, glorified and exalted, and the origination are one single thing.

We say:

That is a sound statement in its meaning. Yet we do not say that what is originated by the complete origination can only be perfect because origination is perfection; we say rather that what is originated by the origination which is the perfection can only be perfect, and that the originated is the origination, and it is the cause, and it is the effect (al-malul), and the perfection, and the perfect. These names fall upon it in succession according to the relations, because there is in that place a multiplicity of essences, so that the meanings of all these names fall upon them all, even though the thing there is one. What we have adduced on this matter in this book and in others is sufficient, without need to prolong the discussion.

**The Sixth Section**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

When it is established that this world is the act of the Soul, and that it is moreover in the utmost order and arrangement — with perpetual circular motions — we know and are certain that by her act she sought to attain the rank that had been withheld from her before she began to act. Since this is so, and since there is nothing in the world nobler than the human being, nor anything more fit to receive the effects of the Soul and in which her benefits take better hold, we know and are certain that the human being is the fruit of the Soul's attainment of the end appointed for her by her cause. So understand that.

We say:

We have already addressed, in treating of the perfection of the consequent — which in his view is the Universal Soul — what shows that in her act she did not seek to attain any rank she had not already gained. The Universal Soul, which is the second, overflows upon this world not in order to draw to herself what is not hers. Allah enriched her, when He brought her into being, with perfection, and she has no need to seek what was not given her. She acts rather to establish wisdom: to bring what is in potency into actuality, to make what is base excellent, and to make what is later in existence a follower of what precedes. On reflection, the benefit returns upon what is brought forth into act and upon the one who bears the burden of obligation — for training in perfection — not upon her who is neither obligated nor in need. To hold such a thing of that mighty limit is error and sin.

Since this is so, and since the human being has two sides — one natural and one divine — he is neither the fruit of this world purely, nor the fruit of that world purely, but the fruit of both worlds together. He is the last among created things, united with eternity by what he has acquired in time. It is for this reason that it has been said:

> مَا جَرَى فِي أَوَّلِهِمْ، جَرَى فِي آخِرِهِمْ

what came to pass in the first of them came to pass in the last of them.

And whoever has come to know from that the stations of the prophets, the guardians, the imams, and the righteous (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) is made ready for deliverance. May Allah gather us with our pure masters in the Abode of Holiness, Dar al-Quds.

**The Seventh Section**

As for the statement of the author of al-Nusrah — that every agent acts only in seeking what he wishes to attain, especially when the act proceeds from the agent with motion, order, and arrangement; and his holding that this is not necessary in the First Originator and in origination, because the act there is metaphorical, not proceeding by motion or order or arrangement, its first being its last and its beginning and its middle the utmost of its end, its end being its middle, with no order and no arrangement in it; order and arrangement belonging instead to what the consequent gains from the lights of that in which there is no arrangement and no order, which is the First Intellect; and the Soul having become very like the Intellect, so that when you look through her toward it you apprehend the powers of things that are not ordered and not arranged, apprehending all things at once — which belongs to her only when she is united with the Intellect and has pushed back the material things, the dark things of nature —

We say: how can his argument hold, when order and arrangement proceed from the Intellect alone? And how can it hold, when things in their totality are made excellent only through the Intellect — while the Soul, until the Intellect's effects take hold in her, is no different from the beasts? It is for this very reason that Allah has withheld knowledge from the Soul until those effects take hold in her. We have already established that the proposition about agents is not universal: not every agent acts in order to satisfy a want or achieve a purpose. What I take him to have meant by that saying is that the essence of the First Intellect is not composite — it does not admit of arrangement or order. As for apprehension: the Intellect apprehends in the only way it can, which is true apprehension.

The author of al-Nusrah said: this is not the case with the soul and the body, where the distinction between them is only a matter of the gentler and the denser. If it were as he stated, then tangible essences would have to be bodies for the accidents — since they are denser than the accidents — and the accidents would have to be souls for them, since they are gentler. Rather, we must look to what defines the body and what defines the soul, then liken to the soul whatever resembles the soul's limit, and to the body whatever resembles the body's limit. We say: we find the body to be that which admits the three dimensions — length, width, and depth. We find the soul to have parts that resemble one another in the same way. And we find motion to be divided into three sections, just as the body is divided: motion from the center, motion toward the center, and motion around the middle. Motion therefore resembles the body in its division, splitting as the body splits into length, width, and depth. The hyle is otherwise, since its parts resemble one another. Because motion resembles the body in the matter of division, while the hyle resembles the soul in the likeness of its parts, it is more fitting to liken motion to the body — which is divided — and the hyle to the soul — whose parts are undivided and alike — than to liken motion to the soul. This is what they said.

We say: in this chapter both of them have strayed far in their use of representation and simile, and both have built upon a corrupt foundation on which no proof can stand — with the result that neither the likeness nor the thing likened to it holds true. The author of al-Islah made it necessary that motion and stillness stand to the hyle and the form as the soul stands to the body, and that is impossible. Motion is the act of the mover, and the mover is precisely what we call the soul — the soul itself. Likewise, stillness is the mover's releasing of what it moves. If the mover is the soul, then to use the soul's own act — motion — as a representation of the soul is to represent the impossible; and the same holds for stillness. If the comparison fails for motion and stillness, it also fails for the claim that motion and stillness are the hyle and the form. Moreover, the nature of the hyle and the form is altogether different from the nature of motion and stillness. Motion and stillness, even as acts, are two opposites that cannot meet in one place, in one state, and at one time. The hyle and the form, by contrast, exist together and are inseparable: one of them is an agent — that is the form — and the other is acted upon within it — that is the hyle. Our book known as The Rest of the Mind contains the full explanation of this.

Then, in what follows, he was compelled to say that the soul is gentler than the body — on the assumption that the soul is a body — and that is impossible. The soul cannot be a body such that density or gentleness could attach to it, for density and gentleness belong only to what is a body, just as air, being a body, is characterized by gentleness, and earth, being a body, is characterized by density. The soul is the soul, and the proofs have established that it is not a body. If the soul is not a body, it is impossible to argue that it is gentler or denser than anything, or to use it as a term of comparison at all.

The author of al-Nusrah then undertook to define the body and the soul. He defined the body by its admitting the three dimensions, and defined the soul by the likeness of its parts, placing it in the same class as water, fire, and air — each of which is alike in its parts. That is impossible, for the reason already stated: the soul is not a body, and this kind of characterization cannot attach to it. نَفْس and al-ruh (الروح) are two synonyms for one and the same thing, and that thing is not a body. If it were a body, it would necessarily require the nine qualities and accidents that a body requires, and it would be perceivable by the senses. But it is not perceived by the senses; it is not perceivable; and the nine accidents are neither conceived in it nor found in it. Since the accidents that belong to bodies are not found in it, it is ruled out that it is a body. Our well-known book With Lamps contains what settles this.

Then he made motion, in its division into three sections, resemble the body — on the ground that the body divides into length, width, and depth — and that too is impossible. The three sections into which he says the body divides are the three dimensions, and none of those three dimensions is itself a body, nor is it called a body. Each section of motion, on the other hand, is itself a motion and is called a motion. How, then, can the body — which divides in form into nine sections, as we have shown in our well-known book Milestones of Religion — be likened to motion, which divides into three sections or six? That comparison is far removed from the truth. The discourse of the author of al-Mahsul, the author of al-Islah, and the author of al-Nusrah in this chapter is disorder without order.

We say: motion is that by which the very being of a thing is altered — whether in place, in coming to be, in corruption, in increase, in decrease, or in transformation — and it proceeds from the mover, which is the principle residing in the thing moved, by nature and not by accident. The hyle and the form are what is sent forth from the First according to the lower relation within it, just as the second is sent forth according to the nobler relation within it. The hyle has no existence apart from the soul except together with the form; and from it come the heavens and the earth and all that lies between them. God Almighty brought all of this into being from the First Intellect, which He originated not out of any thing. Exalted is God with a great exaltation.

The author of al-Islah said: the first cause, which is origination — and which the learned call the word of the Maker, glorified and exalted — takes for its form كن ("Be!"), which is two letters: a moving كاف (kaf) and a still نون (nun). The letters, the كاف and the نون, stand for the word in the position of the body, while motion and stillness stand in the position of the soul. On this basis the example is grounded in the motion and stillness brought about by the word, and the first cause — which is the word — is the hyle of the whole world. The same example holds for motion and stillness in all substances, from the hyle and the form down to everything below them, throughout all the worlds. The account of the three hyles in its entirety is this: the first hyle is the imaginary one — it is motion and stillness, and it is the very substance of the hyle and its form; the second hyle is the individuals and the things generated from the first hyle; and the third hyle is the four composite mother elements, composed from the second hyle.

Then we say: the hyle and its form are the act of the divine command — which is origination. And just as we have said that the hyle is imaginary, so too origination is imaginary, its essence appearing only in the First Originated.

The author of al-Nusrah said: as for what the author of al-Islah advanced concerning كن — saying that the كاف is moving and the نون is still, and that these stand in the position of the body while motion and stillness stand for them in the position of the soul — that is not conceded to him. These two letters were used to express the matter only in this context; there is no كاف there, nor نون, nor motion, nor stillness, for it is not said that above the First Effect there is motion or stillness or anything of that kind. There is only pure origination and the complete cause. This is what they say.

We say: the saying of the author of al-Islah — that the first cause, which is origination, is the word of God Almighty and its form, and that this form consists of two letters — is a remote likeness as he intends it, and it barely holds. For when we say "form," we require that there be something of which it is the form, and the word has no such thing. When we say "form" and mean what is above nature, we mean the abstracted form; when we say "form" and mean what is in the natural world, we mean the form that is in matter. Yet had he said that origination — the word of God Almighty and His command, expressed by كن — consists of two letters, one of them moving and the other still, and that this indicates an essence gathering two attributes, then he would have drawn from that the noble Essence and its attributes.

He said: it gathers two states, just as the word كن gathered two states — motion and stillness. He thus attributed the two states to the two noble relations that the Essence bears: one is its being a cause, and the other its being an effect. He set the motion whose end is stillness against the relation of the cause whose end is the effect, and the stillness whose end is motion against the relation of the effect whose end is the cause — not because there is motion or stillness there, but by way of instruction through likeness. Had he said this, it would have been far more fitting, and what he said would have reached the very nature of the thing spoken of — the thing that the likeness represents.

Then he said that the first hyle is imaginary, and he made it motion and stillness; then he made motion and stillness into the hyle, while the form is not the hyle — rather, it is motion and stillness together that are the hyle and the form. Yet the hyle is a substance sent forth from the First, as the discourse on it has already stated, and from it proceeds the lower world. Motion and stillness, for their part, have their existence in bodies according to the principles from which their appearance comes. And the hyle and the form together are the body — the body being hyle and form.

Then, when he set the first hyle upon the matter that is origination and said that the first hyle is imaginary — so that origination is imaginary, its essence appearing only in the First Originated — his saying requires that there exist from eternity, alongside the Maker, exalted be His majesty above that, some thing in which origination appeared, so that through origination He came to be an originator. That is a grave error. The doctrine and the belief in the affirmation of unity is that whatever is other than God Almighty is originated and brought into being, and had no reality in existence at all; and if that is so, then origination is nothing other than the Originator. We have established the proof of this in what has gone before in our epistles, with sufficient demonstration.

The saying of the author of al-Nusrah — in what he said and in the objection he raised against the author of al-Islah — is sound, except for one point. His saying that it should not be said that above the First Effect there is motion and rest implies an affirmation of motion and rest for the First Effect and for what is below it. We have already shown that the First Effect is not moving, that motion does not befit it, and that there is no stillness in it either, since it is other than a body.

The author of al-Nusrah said: opposition occurs in bodies, and there is no opposition in spirits.

We say: bodies, in so far as they are bodies, have no difference between them and no opposition. The difference and the divergence fall only in the forms that reside within them and move them — not in bodies as such. His saying, that it is more fitting to liken that in which opposition is found to its like, holds as he said, provided the very nature of the thing in question is of that kind.

The author of al-Islah said: the whole world rests upon four jewels, four compositions, and four classes of humankind — or rather upon three jewels apart from humankind, the fourth being humankind itself; and the congealed and the molten are all one jewel. We do not say that the pair of humankind and its likeness is the horse. Rather, the pair of the horse and its likeness belongs to the horse's own jewel — just as the pair of gold is silver, and the pair of the ruby is the emerald — and likewise the pair of the horse and its likeness among animals is the elephant, among birds the falcon, among beasts of prey the lion, and so on. Since they are all animals, it is not permissible to say that the horse is the pair of the human and its likeness. For the human is alone in possessing the rational soul and has no counterpart among the parts of the world and its generated beings. Rather, he is the counterpart of the world itself, because he has gathered within himself all the jewels of the world. For this reason he is called the small world — the microcosm — because he is its counterpart, gathering all its parts.

We say: the counterpart of the whole of humankind — that is the sum of his argument. The world has four divisions; the horse is not the likeness of humankind; humankind has no counterpart among the parts of the world. Its counterpart, rather, is the whole world.

The author of al-Nusrah said: pairing is not a matter of genus alone; pairing is a matter of acceptance.

We say: this dense lower world is the pair of the subtle higher world, and when you look at what lies between them you find the gap far wider than the gap between the horse and the human. The horse does not belong to the genus of the human — they share only life as a genus. He made this dense lower world the pair of the spiritual world for one reason only: the bond between them in giving and receiving. The powers of the higher world flow into this world, and this world receives those powers from the higher world — and it is for this reason that he made this world the pair of that world. The same logic applies to the human and the horse: he made the horse the pair of humankind only because it accepts teaching from the human more readily than any other animal does. His argument, in brief, is that the horse was made the pair of humankind solely because of its acceptance of teaching, and that no other animal matches it in that acceptance.

We say: neither of them, in what they discussed, rested on sound inquiry. The author of al-Islah placed the world upon four jewels, four compositions, and four classes alongside humankind — and that is impossible, because the divisions of the world are more numerous than what he brought forward. Our word "world" is a name pointing to many things, from the center to the encompassing ocean: spheres, stars, fire, air, water, earth, and the generated beings, according to his own reckoning. It is clear from his argument that he means the generated beings of the world, not the world together with its generated beings; and those generated beings are three genera, with no fourth, each with many species beneath it. The first is the nearest to the four elements, which is the minerals — everything whose existence is confined to one place in the earth and not another, without its parts increasing; this includes what dissolves and what does not dissolve, what is malleable and what is not. The second is the plant, which is everything that grows from the earth; this includes the shrub and the tree. The third is the animal, which is everything that moves among things that have species; this includes what flies and what does not fly. The generated beings of the world do not exceed these three. The human being, whom he counts as a fourth, belongs to the genus of animal and is one species among its species.

The Second Section of the Seventh Chapter

He said:

That humankind has no peer, and that the horse has no pair, is impossible from two angles. The first is that humankind is a type within the genus of animal, and as a type it is the pair of every other type within its genus, since all types are forms of one another in respect of quality — and if humankind is a type, it is the pair of the other types. The second is that humankind resembles, in its structure, its morals, its actions, all its conditions, its motions, and its natures, everything that preceded it in the universe of existence — as he himself stated, and as is precisely the reason it is called the small world. By that correspondence and likeness, humankind must be the pair of all existing things. And if this is so, then when humankind is good, virtuous, wise, religious, knowing, and pure, it is paired with the angels by the correspondence between them; when it is courageous, strong, bold, and valiant, its pair is the beasts of prey; when it is unjust, oppressive, and murderous, its pair is the wolves; when deceitful, its pair is the foxes; when it is a treacherous thief, its pair is the crows, the magpies, and the cats; and when it is cunning, wily, and overpowering, its pair is the tiger.

When it is adulterous, immoral, and given to amusement and play, its pair is the monkeys. And on this pattern, according to whatever a person trains himself to resemble — whether by his actions, his morals, his customs, or in any of his ways — that becomes his pair. If this likeness holds, then it is clear that what he claimed in the chapter where he said humankind has no peer cannot be sustained, for humankind has many forms and many pairs from many sides.

The Third Section of the Seventh Chapter

As for the author of al-Nusrah, he was right about the meaning of pairing, but he fell into absurdity in his claim that the horse was made the pair of humankind because it accepts humankind's teaching. For if acceptance and instruction are what make the horse humankind's pair, the horse has no greater claim to that standing than any other animal, since there are animals besides the horse that accept teaching and discipline. And if the basis is type, the horse again has no greater claim than the remaining types of animal. Humankind therefore has pairs — not one pair.

In sum we say:

In truth, the pair of humankind can only be the angels. By "humankind" here we mean those possessed of all the virtues: the prophets, the guardians, and the imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), and those who follow in their footsteps, uphold obedience to them, and worship Allah with both worships — not those beneath them, people who are merely in the form of humankind while their souls are the souls of beasts and predators, as we have said and shown: like the monkeys, the donkeys, the foxes, the beasts of prey, and the wolves. Those who follow the traces of the imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), and above them the prophets and the guardians (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), are distinguished from the beasts only by their striving in obedience and in both worships and their holding fast to the command and the prohibition; and so they come to resemble them (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). Thus they are saved within their company and among them, and no other, for:

> وَمَن يَتَوَلَّهُم مِّنكُمْ فَإِنَّهُۥ مِنْهُمْ

"And whoever takes them as guardians among you is one of them." May Allah make us among those whose gathering and raising is with the prophets, the guardians, and the pure imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), all together.

The Fourth Section of the Seventh Chapter

The author of al-Nusrah said:

He cited Bandhaqlis, who said:

The bestial soul returns to the speaking soul by love; and the speaking soul, when it finds good what returns to it from her, inclines toward her with benefit and mastery, and finds her image beautiful. There is no image among the bestial souls more beautiful than the image of the horse, and none more disposed to receive the effects of the speaking soul; and for that reason he made it its pair. This is clear.

And we say:

Had the author of al-Nusrah drawn his evidence from the Book of Allah, Lord of the Worlds, and from the speech of our masters the pure imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), for what he sought concerning the matter of the soul by way of interpretation, what he found there would have spared him any dependence on the speech of Bandhaqlis. For if Bandhaqlis and those like him among the ancients had lived in our time, they would have needed to draw from the seas of the knowledge of the House of Revelation — overflowing from the treasuries of Allah in His earth — and from our pure masters (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ); and they would have turned back from much of what they believed concerning what is hidden from the senses, of which they spoke — the intellects and their ranks, the soul and its principles — and would have believed otherwise. Moreover, what Bandhaqlis intended is not what the author of al-Nusrah supposed, namely that the bestial soul is the horse and the speaking soul is humankind, when his words are weighed by the balance of sound consideration. Rather, since the sensory soul — which is the bestial soul — is by its nature disposed to accept the ruling of the speaking soul when no impediment hinders it, and since the speaking soul, when it sees the other receptive to its ruling, inclines toward it with benefit and sets it aright, we say: what Bandhaqlis intended by "the sensory soul" is the one in the form of humankind whose nature is to accept teaching, discipline, and reasoning — not the horse; and what he intended by "the speaking soul" is the teachers, who are the prophets, the guardians, and the imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) and their followers, whose nature is to incline toward those in the form of humankind, to teach them their interests in their religion and their world, to benefit what lies in their right guidance, and to raise their degrees according to their acceptance — being in truth the mothers of humankind, though not of every one. And this agrees with what is indicated by the interpretation of the saying of Allah the Exalted:

> يَا اَيَّتُهَا النَّفْسُ الْمُطْمَئِنَّةُ اِرْجِعِي إِلَى رَبِّكِ رَاضِيَةً مَرْضِيَّةً

"O reassured soul, return to your Lord, well-pleased and well-pleasing" (the verse). To carry his statement upon the sound sense is preferable to carrying it upon the unsound; for the horse is not more accepting than the one in the form of humankind, nor is its image more beautiful than the human image, nor are the guardians, the imams, and their followers less inclined toward those beneath them among people than a man is inclined to the training and gentling of the horse — rather, far more so. And if this is so, and there is nothing in the plain sense of Bandhaqlis's words to establish that no image among the bestial souls is more beautiful than the image of the horse, and the matter with existing animal images is not such as to place the horse's beauty above all others, and the case is not as he said, then singling out the horse to be the pair of humankind on the basis of teaching and beauty of image, and arguing from it, is impossible.

The Fifth Section of the Seventh Chapter

The author of al-Islah said:

Humankind is the counterpart of the whole world, and is preferred over it by a noble essence that is not of this world. Its body stands opposite all bodies in their genera — the hard and the solid and the soluble — because in his body there is that which corresponds to each of those genera: bone, flesh, nerve, veins, and cartilage, and all the flowing substances that issue from his body, such as blood, semen, and sweat, and what flows from the eye, the ear, and the mouth, and the rest of what is within him. All of these belong to the essence of bodies and to their genera, and they correspond to the genera of the essence that is one of the three essences of the world — the essence of bodies, which is not united with any of the three souls. His growth, set opposite the growing kinds among the genera of trees in their varieties, corresponds to the genera of the second essence, which is united with the growing soul. His sense, set opposite the animal in its differing genera, is of the genera of the third essence, which is united with the sensory soul, the bestial soul. This is his correspondence with the three jewels of the world; and he is set apart in the fourth rank, becoming the fourth essence, which is the essence of the speaking soul — alone in this station. The upshot of his saying is that humankind is the counterpart of the whole world: his body stands opposite all the bodies of the world in their differing forms, his growth opposite the plants, his sense opposite the animal; and he possesses that which raises him above all others and sets him apart — the speaking soul that is his own.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

As for his saying that the soluble and the congealed are one essence, that cannot be rejected; rather the soluble, the congealed, the growing, the sensory, and the speaking are all one essence in respect of essentiality. But in respect of divisions and properties, the congealed essence is not the soluble, and the soluble is not the congealed; they are distinguished only in the place where they were divided, not in the place where they were not divided. He needed to distinguish between them only because he made the human being an intermediary between the dense corporeal world and the subtle spiritual world.

For in the human being is all that is in both worlds, and so he likened everything in the lower world to the human, leaving nothing of it out. He set the congealed bodies opposite his body — which is itself congealed, like the congealing of other bodies — then he set the soluble bodies opposite his growth, for the dissolution within them; and dissolution occurs only by the motion of the jewels that dissolve, just as growth occurs only by motion, so when the two resembled one another in respect of motion he likened each to its fellow. He set the growing bodies opposite his sense, and set the animal's semen before it. Thus the remainder of the world is gathered in him — congealed and soluble and growing and sensory bodies alike — and he surpasses all the rest of the jewels by speech. The upshot of his saying is that humankind has come to resemble the world until nothing of it is left out: his body stands opposite the congealed bodies, his growth opposite the soluble bodies, his sense opposite the growing bodies, and his speech opposite the animal; and he surpasses, by speech, all the rest of the jewels.

And we say:

The dispute between the two contenders in this section is this: the author of al-Islah set the human being's growth opposite the plant of the great world — that which grows and increases — while the author of al-Nusrah set opposite it the soluble bodies. The author of al-Islah set the human being's sense opposite the animal of the great world, while the author of al-Nusrah set opposite his sense the growing bodies. And the author of al-Islah made the speaking soul the very thing by which the human being is set apart, preferred over the world and made its master, while the author of al-Nusrah set that opposite the animal. The saying of the author of al-Islah is the closer, since what he likens is of the same genus as that to which it is likened, and it carries a correspondence that the saying of the author of al-Nusrah does not. In sum, the human being is not the counterpart of this world alone, but the counterpart of both worlds together — gathering within himself all the matters that preceded him in existence and resembling, in what is within him, them both. The matter concerning him stands on this — not on what he said.

I said that the human being — humankind — is the counterpart of both worlds, because he gathered the multiplicity existing between origination and the second emanation, then became one; and so he came to stand, in himself, parallel to the world of origination and emanation, and by his body — the gatherer of all its parts — parallel to the corporeal world. In this way he became both worlds at once.

Consider his resemblance to this world. The world is entirely body: of it is the subtle, which is what is high, and of it is the dense, which is what is low. The human body is likewise, the subtle being what is high and the dense being what is low.

In the world are stars and encompassing spheres. In the human body likewise, the skin encompasses the whole body and the other coverings wrap the bone; and what runs the course of the stars are the two ears, the two eyes, the nose, and the mouth. In the human being likewise there is a mover that moves, which is the soul.

In the world are the four elements; in the human being likewise, the four natures.

In the world are rivers, valleys, and waters of differing taste, and mountains and minerals. In the human being likewise — as the author of al-Islah set forth — there are the bones and cartilage, the flesh and blood, and all that flows and issues from him.

In the world is that which grows and increases without sensing. In the human being likewise, hair and nails grow and increase without sensing; and the body itself grows and increases from its smallness to its full greatness.

In the world is the sensing animal; in the human being likewise, the sensing faculties.

The whole world is compositions and combinations, and by what exists of these within the human being, the resemblance to the world is completed. All that I have adduced here in summary is set out in detail in my book known as Rahat al-Aql.

As for his resemblance to the world of origination and the first emanation: that world is the world of intellect, of glorification, praise, and sanctification, of oneness and devotion, of virtues, of generosity, and of bounty. Humankind — the human being — is likewise the gatherer of these very virtues: in intellect, glorification, oneness, devotion, bounty, and generosity toward others and in himself. That world has stamped him, and by his body he has come to resemble this lower world; and being so constituted, he is undistinguished from what preceded him in existence except by rank alone. May Allah make us among those who follow the traces of His friends, and may the intercession of His chosen ones not pass us by, through His grace and His bounties.

The Seventh Section of the Seventh Chapter

The author of al-Islah said:

That humankind is preferred over the world by a noble essence that is not of this world — though I do not thereby mean that the essence he denied to this world therefore belongs to that world, the noble upper world, as was revealed to him. Rather, since it is neither of that world alone nor of this world alone, I say that it is of both worlds together. For its condition in existing within the two worlds is the condition of a child between his two parents: it is not permitted to ascribe it to one of them apart from the other, since it is by both together, and from both, that it came to be.

What may rightly be ascribed from it to this world is that within it which runs the course of matter — the receptive. What may be ascribed to that world is that within it which runs the course of form — the received. This is as with the child, who receives from his two parents the higher and nobler form on one side, and on the other — lower than that — the receptive matter.

The proofs of this are many, and they are found in my book known as Rahat al-Aql. And Allah the Exalted grants us success to the abode of His religion, and provides us the intercession of Muhammad His prophet, and the imams of his pure household, and his progeny (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

By this the human being comes to resemble that higher world in his soul, and his body comes to resemble this lower world; and being so constituted, he is no longer set apart from that whose existence preceded him except in rank alone. May Allah make us among those who follow the traces of His friends and do not overstep them, and grant us the intercession of His chosen ones, through His grace and His bounties.

The seventh section of the seventh chapter.

And the statement of the author of al-Islah:

> ان البشر يفضل على العالم بالجوهر الشريف الذي ليس هو من هذا العالم

That the human being surpasses the world by the noble substance which is not of this world.

Now I do not hold that the substance which he denied could belong to this world is therefore from that world — I mean the higher and noble world above — as was disclosed to him. Rather, because the human being is neither from that world alone nor from this world alone, I say that he is from both worlds together. For his condition in existing within the two worlds is the condition of a child in coming to be between his two parents: it is not permitted to trace him to one of them apart from the other, since it is by both together, and from both, that he was brought into being.

What may rightly be traced from him to this world is that in him which runs the course of matter, and this is the receptive. What is traced to that world is that in him which runs the course of form, and this is the received. This is as it stands with the child, who receives from his two parents the higher and nobler form on one side, and on the other — lower than that — the receptive matter.

The proofs of this are many, and they are found in my book known as Rahat al-Aql.

> والله تعالى يوفقنا الى موطن دينه ، وَيَرْزُقَنَا شَفَاعَةَ مُحَمَّدٍ نَبِيِّهِ ، وَالْأَئِمَّةُ مِنْ آلِهِ الطَّاهِرِينَ ، وَعِتْرَتِهِ صَلَوَاتُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِمْ

And Allah the Exalted grants us success to the abode of His religion, and provides us the intercession of Muhammad His prophet, and the imams of his pure household, and his progeny (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

## 9. Decree and Destiny

**Section One**

The author of al-Islah said:

The claim that decree is prior and destiny posterior is wrong. Destiny comes before decree; the prior comes before the posterior. It is not permissible for decree — which comes after destiny — to be the very thing that stands before the posterior. Decree and destiny are two words whose meaning is known from ordinary speech: destiny is estimation, and decree is elaboration, and there is no elaboration except after estimation. The first stands before the second by the farthest measure of priority. The evidence from the Book of God is His saying:

> قضي الامر الذي به تستفتيان
>
> "The matter concerning which you two sought a verdict has been decreed,"

that is, separated out and finished; and His saying:

> فَإِذَا قُضِيَتِ ٱلصَّلَوٰةُ
>
> "When you have finished the prayer,"

that is, when you have completed it. The likeness of decree is that of a garment which a tailor first estimates. He estimates it before he cuts it, and while it remains in estimation it can be increased, decreased, widened, or narrowed; but once it has been cut, it is finished and complete, and no further increase is possible.

The upshot of his saying is this: decree cannot be predicated of the prior, for it comes after destiny; and destiny cannot be predicated of the posterior, for it comes before decree. Destiny is estimation and decree is elaboration. So long as a thing remains within estimation, it can be increased, decreased, and widened; but once it has been elaborated and decreed, neither increase nor decrease is possible.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Decree is not elaboration. The state of the elaboration within the decree is no less than the state of the thing separated out; the separated thing is fixed in its form only by a decree that requires it, just as the separated thing is itself established only by what has been decreed for it. As for destiny, it is estimation, as he said; yet there is no estimation except through a decree that requires it. As for the evidence he cited from the Book of God the Exalted — that decree is a completing — this is not to be rejected. The sages have said that when God the Exalted originated the Intellect He completed the two worlds, since the Intellect is the sum of the images of the two worlds and nothing of those images is absent from it; it is pure completion. Yet the author of al-Islah erred in likening destiny and decree to the garment a tailor estimates. He made the tailor's estimation correspond to destiny and his elaboration correspond to decree, and this likeness is unsound. He ought rather to have made the image of the sewing that lies within the tailor's own soul correspond to decree, because it is spiritual; and the estimation by which the tailor estimates that sewing correspond to destiny, for it too is a spiritual thing that has not yet emerged to the limit of intellect — and this is the meaning of the image of the two origins. The elaboration then carried out after the estimation, upon the corporeal boundaries that appeared from the first and the second, corresponds to what is made manifest. If the matter is as we have described, then decree precedes destiny, just as the sewing precedes the estimation, and by that sewing the tailor is made ready for the estimation by which he estimates. So it is established that decree is prior and destiny posterior. The upshot of his saying is that decree comes before destiny, that there is no estimation except by decree, and that decree is the prior and destiny the posterior.

We say:

The interpretations of decree and destiny offered by the two sages — each according to what he has invoked — lead, despite the meaning of the two words making any such interpretation impossible (as we have shown), to the same conclusion in every case: that decree and destiny are the prior and the posterior. The author of al-Islah, by saying that destiny is estimation and decree is elaboration, and by the likeness he drew from the garment and the image of the sewing, bound himself to holding that destiny is what stands in potency — what could yet be — and that decree is what has been brought into act, having emerged from the door of possibility, which is potency, into actuality. But if this is the meaning of destiny and decree, then it is impossible to speak of them as the prior and the posterior at all, in any respect; for neither stands in potency, nor was either in potency and then emerged into act, as the proofs we have already set down make clear.

**Section Two**

The author of al-Nusrah bound himself — by his saying that there is no estimation except through a decree that requires it, and by his representing decree with the craft of sewing from which the estimation comes — to placing destiny, which he made posterior, as an existence standing in potency, granting that destiny's meaning is, as the author of al-Islah established it, precisely such an existence. Yet this cannot be said of the posterior, since it has already been established from what we said above that the posterior stands in act; and if it could be said, then destiny would not be posterior. And if destiny is not posterior, then decree is the more fitting to be the prior — and it is still more proper that there never be a state in which decree was in potency and then emerged into act, so that what its word conveys would match what actually holds of the prior. It was equally impossible, then, for decree and destiny to be spoken of as the prior and the posterior. And since this is impossible, we say that their saying is correct in the way we shall explain, not according to what they intended; for the word "decree" shares its meaning among several things, and all those things belong to the world of body, not to the world of origination; and likewise destiny.

**Section Three**

As for decree, it points, first, to the Speaker who stood in potency and then emerged into act by the support of God the Exalted, and to those who stand in his place (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), who have reached in their attainment the rank parallel to the First Emanation. All of these (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), in their standing in act — in command, prohibition, and decree within the summons that stands in act, after they had been in potency — correspond to the meaning the word "decree" indicates. This is what the author of al-Nusrah intends, and upon this reading his saying, and his placing of decree first, is correct.

**Section Four**

Decree points, second, to what is brought out by interpretation from the potency of the Revelation and the Law: the sciences not set forth plainly in them but present within them in potency, drawn out into exposition and act, by which souls are taken from the limit of potency to the limit of act. This is what the author of al-Islah intends by his interpretation of the word "decree."

**Section Five**

Decree points, third, to what emerges into act — the latency of events and the transformations destined to arise in the world through the motion of things that move and the stillness of things that are still, which admit no reversal. It is for this reason that, when some matter comes to pass and is realized in act, it is said, "There is no reversing the decree of God." These are the things of which decree is spoken.

**Section Six**

As for the الْقَائِم, he is, first, the promised الْقَائِم, foretold from the time of Adam (ع) and by his summons, whose guarding and upholding is carried out by the Imams and the Messengers (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) through their summoners in the period of concealment, extending the force by his being destined to emerge from estimation into act and existence. His standing summons, from the side of the boundaries of the cycle of the Prophet (ع) and his household in the concealment, runs the course of the qaim-in-potency, not the manifest course that runs as the qaim-in-act. For this reason the interpretation of Laylat al-Qadr (ع) refers to him, and to him point the allusions of the shaykhs in their saying:

> كوني (قَدَر)
>
> "Be al-Qadr."

**Section Seven**

Destiny points, second, to what the Prophet (ع) and his household brought of the Revelation, the Law, the rulings, and the parables, which hold within their potency the sciences and knowledge that the soul needs in its passage to maturity and act, by being cast in a form that gathers all of that together. For this reason not every science is found in the outer face of these things except through their interpretation. The saying of the author of al-Islah tends toward this meaning of destiny.

**Section Eight**

Destiny points, third, to the world with its parts, which holds within its power to emerge into being and act across the passage of days, months, years, events, and existents — existence that comes from it through the perpetual motion of the movers over what is below them, this being necessary because they are destined within it. For this reason it is said, "What God has destined will be," for the destined comes to be by the necessity of its existence, and though its term be long, it is as the thing already in act. Hence God the Exalted said:

> إِنَّهُمْ يَرَوْنَهُۥ بَعِيدًۭا ونراه قريباً
>
> "They see it as far, and We see it as near."

So whatever was within estimation, that it should come to be, is decree when it comes to be — since the word "decree" indicates what was in potency and then emerged into act. These are the meanings of decree and destiny.

If one considers the words of the author of al-Islah, and his saying that destiny precedes decree, its meaning has no sound sense except in what the Prophet (ع) and his household brought of the Revelation and the Law, which precedes the decree that is the interpretation — as he interpreted, God's mercy be upon him. And the saying of the author of al-Nusrah, that decree precedes destiny, when pondered, has no sound sense except in the Speaker (ع), the one standing in act, who is the decree that precedes what he brought of the Revelation and the Law, which is destiny.

**Section Nine**

The author of al-Nusrah said, reporting from the wise scholars: when God the Exalted originated the Intellect, He completed the two worlds, for it is the assembly of the images of the worlds.

We say:

The Intellect that is the assembly of the images of the worlds is not the First originated Intellect, nor the Second Intellect — which is the Second Emanated. Rather it is the Intellect that ascends from the world of nature, by acquisition in deed and in knowledge, to a parallel with the First Emanation, so that it is emanated in a second emanation and thereby becomes an assembly of the images of the worlds despite their multitude. Upon its essence are imprinted the forms of all existents, as is the case with the Speakers, the Executors, and the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). The First Intellect is too exalted for this kind of multiplicity, since its existence is by origination, and the multiplicity of originated existence is only according to what it intellects of its own essence: that it is a cause and an effect, and that its existence is from what it received therein from what is outside it alone. As for the rest of the existents — I mean the intellects emanated in a first emanation — their multiplicity is according to what I have said of their essences and of what precedes them in existence, back to the First Intellect, while their existence is nonetheless from that in which they found their godward reach, from what is outside themselves. So the later in existence is multiple, but not with the multiplicity of the prior in existence; and its need to be multiple — in order to intellect all existents — is a necessary need, from which the first in existence is free in its completeness. If this is so, then it has become clear that the one that is the assembly of the images of the two worlds is not the First Intellect, but the intellects emanated from the abode of nature in a second emanation.

Then we say:

The First Intellect is not bound to intellect what is below it, nor to represent it — not on account of its standing in eternity, completeness, subsistence, unity, and immunity, but on account of that from which it came to exist. And since its being is eternal, complete, subsisting, one, single, and immune from transformation — on account of that from which it came to exist, exalted be He — it is impossible for it to intellect what is below it, for it has no need of that.

And likewise we say:

The one that is later in existence, from the second emanation, comes to be bound to intellect what precedes it in existence, so that it becomes an assembly of the images — because the completeness in which its subsistence lies it acquires by intellecting that whose existence precedes its own. And since it stands in need, for its completeness, of intellecting what precedes its existence, from among the boundaries upon whom the interpretive summons was established, which is the second worship, for the making clear of their ranks, it is bound to teach them and to intellect them. When it teaches them and intellects them, its essence becomes multiple by virtue of the multitude of its intellected objects, and becomes one from the other side, which is its essence — so that it resembles thereby what precedes it in existence. Our book known as Rahat al-Aql contains, of the proofs of the Law's evidence for this and of the religious depictions, what suffices and heals the seeker for his return. May God make us among those who follow His good pleasure in what grieves and what gladdens, and who follow His friends (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) in that regard.

**Section Ten**

The author of al-Islah said:

The error of those who made decree the prior and destiny the posterior arose from this alone: they found the scholars had called the posterior by the name of destiny, which is indeed its true name — for all the entities were emanated from the prior as things destined, and that emanation was destiny itself, so the sages named it destiny. And "decree and destiny" ran together in the speech of the people, and they found them placing decree before destiny in speech, saying "this is decree and destiny." The meaning of decree and destiny was hidden from them, so they assigned decree to the first and destiny to the second, and error arose from this. This is as we have said: all the entities were emanated from the prior as things destined, and that emanation was destiny itself — which is the assembly of the entities. The confirmation of this is the saying of God the Exalted:

> إِنَّا كُلَّ شَىْءٍ خَلَقْنَهُ بِقَدَرٍۢ
>
> "Indeed, We have created everything according to a measure."

Just as all the entities were emanated from the First — the command in its destined potency — and were destiny itself, so from the Second there appeared, in act, out of matter and form; and that act was decree itself. Destiny is what admits of change and alteration; it is the book of which God the Exalted said:

> يَمْحُوا۟ ٱللَّهُ مَا يَشَآءُ وَيُثْبِتُ
>
> "God erases what He wills and confirms."

Destiny is the essence of the posterior, as we have said, and decree is its act — for the posterior is what the prior destines. The substance of his saying in this section, then, is that destiny names the posterior, because all the entities were emanated from the prior as things destined, as God the Exalted said:

> إِنَّا كُلَّ شَىْءٍ خَلَقْنَهُ بِقَدَرٍۢ
>
> "Indeed, We have created everything according to a measure,"

and that decree is its act.

The author of al-Islah said:

As for what he put forward concerning decree and destiny — namely, the saying of God the Exalted:

> إِنَّا كُلَّ شَىْءٍ خَلَقْنَهُ بِقَدَرٍۢ
>
> "Indeed, We have created everything according to a measure,"

— he has erred in his interpretation of this verse. Under the interpretation we set out and maintain, the verse is entirely free of his reading, for by it He intends only pure ipseity.

We say:

By "We" He intends a subject untouched by attribute or by no-attribute; by "every thing" He intends the intellect, the totality of things; by "We have created it" an image is intended, one within which both worlds are contained; and by "a measure" He intends the posterior — that upon which the matter is made firm. All the spiritual and physical images appeared within these in the world of witness, which is the pen, the tablet, the writing, and the writer. The writer stands in the place of "We" — pure ipseity. The pen stands in the place of the intellect, and it is the pen of pens. "We have created it" corresponds to the tablet. The pen answers to the letters that appear upon the tablet, and "a measure" answers to the tablet itself, which receives all the forms of the letters. Just as all things physical and spiritual — the simple and the dense — fall beneath these intelligible letters that appear from the writer, by the pen, upon the tablet, so too all the images above and below were brought forth by God the Exalted through the intellect and the soul; and nothing among them was withheld, just as nothing is withheld from the ordered letters that appear in act from the writer upon the tablet. This is the position of the author of al-Nusra in his refutation.

We say:

The two authors have set down in this chapter what cannot stand — neither the one in al-Islah nor the other in the refutation. The author of al-Islah maintained that destiny names the posterior, and argued for this from the saying of God the Exalted, "Indeed, We have created everything according to a measure." Yet the discussion already given on destiny and decree shows that destiny cannot be predicated of the posterior. The author of al-Nusra took the rebuttal of al-Islah to be its interpretation of the verse, but that is neither balanced nor congruent with the matter. For al-Islah said, by way of interpretation:

God the Exalted, by "We," intends only pure ipseity, which no attribute taints and no no-attribute taints; and the word أنا falls only at the limit from which the Prophet draws by taking his station in the world, not upon pure ipseity. How could "We" apply to pure ipseity, when between Him and it stands the intermediary of negation? It falls rather upon the near intermediary — the Messenger of God (ع) in the emanation upon him — and the meaning is the Prophet, for he is the one who conveys from Him.

Section Eleven of Chapter Eight

His claim that "every thing" signifies the intellect, the totality of things, does not hold; "every thing" in this passage is not the intellect. We have already shown that the first intellect is not multiple in the way that a thing is multiple whose existence encompasses "every thing." What is meant here is rather what the community needs from the two worships that gather the sciences — outwardly by deed and inwardly by the perception that leads to deliverance from the torment of the worlds.

Section Twelve of Chapter Eight

His reading of "We have created it" does not hold either, for "We have created it" means "We have measured it." That is: We made the form of the revelation and the Law a form encompassing every science and religious work that the community needs to ward pain from the soul in this world and the next. It is as though He said, glorified and exalted be He: "We have created everything according to a measure" — meaning the matter of the two worships in knowledge and deed that the community needs in its religion and its worldly life. "We have measured it" means: We brought it forth in measured form in what We sent down of the revelation and the Law — that is, We placed it within her power, so that it might emerge in interpretation to the one who merits it.

Section Thirteen of Chapter Eight

The author of al-Islah said:

Decree is the posterior act. Here he struck true from one side: what comes forth into act from the world of nature comes forth by the outpouring of the posterior, is completed by it, and from its side attains its completion. This is why decree came to be predicated of the speaker — because his coming forth into act and his attainment of perfection are by the outpouring and support of the posterior.

Section Fourteen of Chapter Eight

His claim that "all the essences were emanated from the prior as things measured" contains a contradiction, for the posterior is an essence emanated from the prior and is not itself measured. What that saying properly denotes is only the emanation from which the physical world arose — measured so that things might come forth from it, as we explained in the chapter on destiny — and that is not the posterior.

Section Fifteen of Chapter Eight

The author of al-Nusra said:

In the passage whose opening is, "We have created it, by which He intends the image of the two worlds among them, and a measure, by which He intends the posterior upon which the matter is made firm, and all the spiritual and physical images appeared among them," the existence of things is not bodily and not bodily as he set it down — and this is on account of what we showed regarding the corruption of the foundation upon which he built his words. Were it not that the discussion of the order in which existing things follow upon the first lies outside the scope of this writing, we would bring it forth; and it shall not fail to be mentioned, God the Exalted willing.

Section Sixteen of Chapter Eight

The author of al-Islah said:

As for the interpretation of the saying of the Messenger of God (ع) — "I flee from the decree of God to His destiny":

> أَفِرُّ مِنْ قَضَاءِ اللَّهِ إِلَى قَدَرِهِ
>
> "I flee from the decree of God to His destiny,"

— he intended only to flee from the subtle support of the prior to the plain declaration of the posterior, as a man flees from fire, which is the sign of support, to water, which is the sign of declaration. He wished to move away from what was already sealed and decreed — what admits of no repelling — toward the destiny that has not yet been decreed. This is the saying, and it is not well ordered.

The Messenger of God (ع) fled from the decree of God to His destiny for a specific reason: the leaning wall whose fall he feared. He quickened his walking and spoke these words, wishing to set before them a parable. The leaning wall is a structure that has inclined toward collapse, and it represents one who stands at a certain limit — one who has resolved upon treachery and hypocrisy and turned from obedience toward corruption; for if he turns treacherous, he falls from his station. As God the Exalted said:

> فَوَجَدَا فِيهَا جِدَارًۭا يُرِيدُ أَن يَنقَضَّ فَأَقَامَهُۥ
>
> "And they found in it a wall about to collapse, so he set it upright."

I mean, one of the limits had resolved upon hypocrisy and rupture, so he set it upright until it held firm and neither broke nor fell. Such was the leaning wall past which the Messenger of God (ع) quickened his step: he had discerned in one of the limits that it had sensed hypocrisy and was inclining toward rupture. He addressed it outwardly, through his speed of walking — for the posterior, in walking, signifies declaration, proof, haste, and swiftness of pace in the outward. So he (ع) would address the one who had resolved upon hypocrisy with admonition and warning in the outward, without the declaration of the inward. Destiny is upon the outward, as we have explained, and decree is upon the inward. His flight from the decree of God to His destiny was for the sake of this wall — out of caution against its fall, and caution over himself — and not a flight from the honor with which God the Exalted had honored him, or from the degree to which God had raised him.

The author of al-Nusra said:

And he is the more eager for increase.

The interpretation of "I flee from the decree of God to His destiny" is not as the author of al-Islah imagined — that the Prophet was fleeing from the rank of the posterior to the rank of the prior on the ground that, by his account, the prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) forever seek elevation, and the seeking of loftiness is not the seeking of lowliness. We say concerning it, by the help of God the Exalted and His blessing, that the meaning here unfolds across three senses, each of which establishes that decree falls upon the prior and destiny upon the posterior.

The first — and the greatest, most fitting, and most exact in meaning — is this: from the prior comes support, from the posterior comes composition, from the speaker comes the authorship of the Law, and from the foundation comes the interpretation of the whole. The interpretation, which is the end, is more like support, and authorship is more like composition. So the speaker (ع), in saying this, made plain that he resembles the posterior — in his authorship of the Laws as the posterior composes the world — and does not resemble the prior. He fled from the decree of God, which is the prior, and did not liken himself to it from the aspect of originating support; he turned instead toward his destiny, which is interpretation, and to this he likened himself.

The second meaning is his disclosure to his community that he draws from the first by means of the second — so that in everything he wished to apprehend of the first, he returned to it by means of the second. It is as though he said: I return, in all I wish to grasp, to the one who is the link between me and the intellect, which is the second. This is the second meaning.

The third meaning — and the most noble — is what the author of the book (ع) mentioned: his flight from the intensity of the light of the prior's support to the support of the posterior. Even the Grandfather (اَلْجَدّْ), for all his spiritual power, could not bear that light until his very identity nearly dissolved. This is his saying in the story of Moses (ع). This is what the two of them said.

We say:

As for the author of al-Islah, his interpretation of the verse moves in a direction conformable to the true meaning of decree and destiny, and this is well. It would have been better still had he said this: since the Prophet (ع) was one informed of hidden things among beings and events, and of what lies in men's hearts, by the revelation with which God the Exalted had singled him out — he wished to make plain how what was cast to him was opened by the اَلْجَدّْ. So he said, "Return," which is his saying, "I flee." That is: I cross, by means of the اَلْجَدّْ, from the side of the limits of God the Exalted, through the whole of what is about to occur — the events and beings that are the decree of God going forth inevitably into act — toward His destiny, which is God's creation: the souls and the horizons and their motions, which point to what He has measured within them. By this there is opened to us what we made plain in the chapter of revelation. For this, God the Exalted said:

> سَنُرِيهِمْ آيَتِنَا فِى ٱلأٓفَاقِ وَفِىٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَهُمْ أَنَّهُ ٱلْحَقُّ
>
> "We shall show them Our signs upon the horizons and within themselves, until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth."

And those who reflect upon the creation of the heavens and the earth say:

> رَبَّنَا مَا خَلَقْتَ هَذَا بَطِلًۭا
>
> "Our Lord, You did not create this in vain."

This does not merely point to what has preceded and what follows in the existence of beings; it signifies and articulates it. It is as though he said: I return, and the whole of what the اَلْجَدّْ casts to me is carried over to the destiny, so that it is shown and opened through its representation and conception in me. That is his saying, "I flee from the decree of God to His destiny," and this is a kind of revelation. The book رَاحَةُ الْعَقْلِ contains an explanation of that.

Section Seventeen of Chapter Eight

As for the author of al-Nusra, the manner of his answer itself proves his submission to the author of al-Islah. He brought forward nothing that overturns what al-Islah built his argument upon and made plain — only the meanings he put forward, supposing them to constitute its interpretation, and claiming that each establishes decree upon the prior and destiny upon the posterior. That claim would only oblige one to establish decree upon the prior and destiny upon the posterior if there were a correspondence between decree and destiny and the meanings he expounded — and there is none at all. Had the meaning of decree and destiny corresponded to the state of the first and the second, and been fitting to them from some aspect, it would have been permissible to say that decree and destiny apply to them from that aspect. But decree means what has already been discussed, and the same is true of destiny; neither deserves to be predicated of the first and the second, for those two are free of what these terms require. Whoever predicates them of these has spoken as one who says of honey, whose nature is sweetness, that it is aloes, whose nature is bitterness.

His likening of the speaker to the posterior in the second meaning does not conform to the law of the guiding call. The speaker, in his standing within the world of the body and the world of religion, holds the station of the prior; the guardian's station in these two worlds is the station of the posterior in the world of origination. The speaker and the foundation are thus two likenesses of the prior and the posterior, and two likenesses of the sun and the moon.

Section Nineteen of Chapter Eight

The third meaning — which he set down as what compels the speaker's flight, from the intensity of the light of the prior's support toward the support of the posterior — runs contrary to what is due. The interpretation he cited as witness regarding the case of the Grandfather calls for full inquiry and thorough treatment, and the discussion of it will come in its proper place. In sum, the saying of the author of al-Islah in this section is the stronger.

Section Twenty of Chapter Eight

The author of al-Islah said, interpreting the verse in the story of Moses (ع):

> رَبِّ أَرِنِىٓ أَنظُرْ إِلَيْكَ قَالَ لَن تَرَىٰنِى وَلَكِنِ ٱنظُرْ إِلَى ٱلْجَبَلِ فَإِنِ ٱسْتَقَرَّ مَكَانَهُۥ فَسَوْفَ تَرَىٰنِى فَلَمَّا تَجَلَّىٰ رَبُّهُۥ لِلْجَبَلِ
>
> "My Lord, show me, that I may look upon You. He said: You shall not see Me, but look at the mountain; if it holds firm in its place, then you shall see Me. But when his Lord manifested Himself to the mountain..."

This one sought to have disclosed to him more than what had been measured out for him of the limit of the prior. He was told, "You shall not see Me, but look at the mountain," making known to him that he would not attain that, and commanding him to keep within what runs to him by the اَلْجَدّْ; and He made known that the very thing he asked — the اَلْجَدّْ, for all its subtlety — has no power over its like, so how should he attain what he cannot see?

And He says, "if it holds firm in its place, then you shall see Me":

> فَلَمَّا تَجَلَّىٰ رَبُّهُۥ لِلْجَبَلِ جَعَلَهُۥ دَكًّۭا وَخَرَّ مُوسَىٰ صَعِقًۭا
>
> "But when his Lord manifested Himself to the mountain, He made it crumble to dust, and Moses fell down senseless."

When he knew that he had erred in seeking what he could not attain, he turned in humble supplication.

When he recovered, he said:

> سُبْحَنَكَ تُبْتُ إِلَيْكَ

"Glory be to You, I repent to You." This is the saying of the author of al-Islah.

The author of al-Nusra said:

> رَبِّ أَرِنِىٓ أَنظُرْ إِلَيْكَ قَالَ لَن تَرَىٰنِى وَلَكِنِ ٱنظُرْ إِلَى ٱلْجَبَلِ

"My Lord, show me, that I may look upon You. He said, You will not see Me, but look at the mountain." By "You will not see Me" is meant: you have not the strength for that rank, nor can you bear patiently to stand upon it. The mountain is the Grandfather (اَلْجَدّْ), with his nearness to me. If he settles upon the rank I have given him and bears what I did not disclose to him, then you too shall bear it. But the Grandfather did not bear it when his Lord was manifested to him, and his very identity all but dissolved away. This is his saying.

We say:

The interpretation to which each of these two men turned demeans Moses (ع). They place him at the station of the prophets and the highest rank — one who converses with the upper limits — and then cast him out of the company of the learned and count him among the ignorant, alleging that he asked for what lies beyond his reach among the limits above him. How could this be, and so plain a matter escape him? The interpretation of the verse is other than what they supposed.

Moses (ع) was not addressing the upper limits at all. His address was to the lower limits, at a time when he had not yet attained the rank of the Speaker. He asked of the Master of the Command what its hour had not yet come, for he was living in a time of interruption, and the people of truth were under affliction, veiling, and concealment from their adversaries and opponents.

Moses (ع) saw in himself a power by which he reckoned that, in that time of his, he might command the lifting of that ordeal from the people of truth, take vengeance upon the enemies of God, and govern the community by the governance of truth — just as the Foundation (ع) reckoned, by the power he found in himself, what he reckoned, and then repented of it when he knew he had erred, whereupon the sun was returned to him, as we have set forth by interpretation in our epistles. So Moses (ع) asked this of the Master of the Command, who is Shuayb (ع), when he came to him. Shuayb was his Lord, after his charge had been made plain to him regarding the strength and capacity for governance he found within himself. That is his saying:

> رَبِّ أَرِنِىٓ أَنظُرْ إِلَيْكَ

"My Lord, show me, that I may look upon You," meaning: give me the command, that I may set this affair in order and lift the grief. He said:

> لن تراني

"You will not see Me," meaning: you will not see Me do any of that, nor will I invest you with any command at this time, for everything has its hour and its season. This season is not the season for turning these people — that is, the community — away from following their own desires, nor is it the time for restoring affairs to the laws of governance; what we say to you shall not be brought to completion.

Shuayb (ع) wished to alert him to his error in the best of ways, and to teach him that what he sought was difficult and would not be brought to completion in that time, even at the hands of one possessing power, might, and equipment. How much less could it be so at the hands of a man of great station in the summons, of great worldly presidency, possessed of might, wealth, men, and dominion? He said to Moses (ع): I know that this affair will not be brought to completion in this time — it is hard and cannot be measured out — and my withholding is not from any grudging toward you, nor from any wish to delay you from your station. But look at the mountain, meaning this man who has worldly power, equipment, and capacity.

> فَإِنِ ٱسْتَقَرَّ مَكَانَهُۥ فَسَوْفَ تَرَىٰنِى

"If it settles in its place, then you shall see Me," meaning: if he holds firm in what he is commanded, and is able, with the power and might he has, to establish the laying-out of the ordinances of the governance of truth, and it is brought to completion for him, then you shall see Me do what you asked of these people.

> فَلَمَّا تَجَلَّىٰ رَبُّهُۥ لِلْجَبَلِ جَعَلَهُۥ دَكًّۭا

"And when his Lord was manifested to the mountain, He made it crumble to dust," meaning: when he commanded him to raise the beacon of truth, the affair pressed hard upon the people and they did not accept the command; they broke the staff, the affair was confounded and thrown into disorder, and his presidency came to the brink of ruin as the people rose against him. Then Moses (ع) knew that what he had reckoned would be completed at his hand in that hour was an impossible reckoning, and that the Master of the Command — who is his Lord — knew the matter better than he. He awoke from the confusion that warning had cast over him and said:

> سُبْحَنَكَ تُبْتُ إِلَيْكَ

"Glory be to You, I repent to You." He retracted his word, felt remorse, and held to patience under what the Master of the Support had commanded him, until the term for which he had hired himself was spent, and the hour came, and there came about from him what came about, and his word was verified: "then you shall see Me" — the affair being surrendered to you, and you doing as you will. This is the interpretation of the verse, not what is measured out by the one who lowers and raises; it does not correspond.

The twenty-first section of the eighth chapter.

The author of al-Nusra said:

As for his citing the words:

> وَخَلَقَ كُلَّ شَىْءٍۢ فَقَدَّرَهُۥ تَقْدِيرًۭا

"And He created everything and measured it out with due measure," this is the clearest proof that the measuring-out belongs to the Follower, and that the decree, which is the creating, belongs to the Preceder, since the meaning of "and He created everything" is the Preceder, because the Intellect is all things and all things are the Intellect.

We say:

The discourse on the decree and the measuring-out has already gone before, and it was made plain there that neither decree nor measuring-out may rightly be predicated of the Preceder and the Follower, and that what is meant by His words, "and He created everything and measured it out with due measure," is not what the author of al-Nusra supposed; we have already shown the corruption of that, and that it does not befit either the Preceder or the Follower that decree and measuring-out be predicated of them. Rather, what is meant by "and He created everything and measured it out with due measure" is that He placed everything pertaining to the affair of religion and to the interests — whether outward or inward — within the power of the revelation and the sacred law, and measured it out according to what the revelation and the sacred law comprise of all the sciences bearing upon religion and the interests, which lie within them in potency and which interpretation brings forth from them.

The twenty-second section of the eighth chapter.

As for his saying that the Intellect is all things and all things are the Intellect, this is a proposition that is not sound as it stands, for the corporeal world, with all it gathers, is not intellect; it is a thing. The best sense that may be attributed to his words — "the Intellect is all things and all things are the Intellect" — is that the Intellect, which is the very Command of God, is the cause of the existence of all things, corporeal and non-corporeal alike, and that the existence of all things is from the Intellect. This is his intended meaning.

The twenty-third section of the eighth chapter.

The author of al-Nusra said:

God has granted the prophets license in the inner meaning of the sacred laws and in leaving off their outward use, in His words:

> لَيْسَ عَلَى ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ جُنَاحٌۭ فِيمَا طَعِمُوٓا۟ إِذَا مَا ٱتَّقَوا۟ وَّآمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ ثم اتقوا وآمنوا واحسنوا ان الله يحب المحسنين
>
> "There is no blame upon those who believe and do righteous deeds for what they have eaten, so long as they fear God and believe and do righteous deeds; then fear Him and believe; then fear Him and do good, and God loves the doers of good."

We say:

The verse contains, neither outwardly nor inwardly, any mention of a license to the prophets to leave off the use of the sacred laws. I do not know how this escaped him, that he should let fall such a saying without pursuing it to the end. The exposition and the interpretation of this verse run contrary to what he supposed.

God, knowing that souls do not attain happiness, nor reach the degree of perfection, nor pass from the bound of potency to the bound of act, save through work and knowledge, charged the whole of mankind with the upholding of the two worships — in knowledge and in deed — so that by them they might attain the two felicities in the two abodes. Since souls do not attain their perfection and happiness save through the two worships, any soul that abandons both of them, or one of them, is far from attaining perfection. So it stands with the soul so long as it remains in the bond of this life: it is held between His hands until the hour of its passing, either to be in the Garden, or — should it abandon them or one of them — to be in the Fire, until God forgives it. We have made plain in our books, and in what has preceded of this discourse, that souls are upheld only through work and through the reforming of character, which is the greatest jihad (اَلْجِهَادُ الْأَكْبَرُ), as the Messenger of God (ع) said, and through their acquisition of the virtues by way of spiritual discipline and knowledge, in relation to what precedes them in existence, according to what the interpretive summons requires.

If the states of the souls in worship stand in this likeness, how can it be permitted that license be granted — to the souls, or to the Speakers, or to those beneath them among the limits — to leave off work and the full reforming of souls, and what is deficient in them?
