No; the verse carries an interpretation other than the one he gave it, bearing in particular upon those who uphold the summons among the preachers who call to the التَّوْحِيد of God and to the knowledge of His limits by way of the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ).

The Speaker (ع), knowing that his community would differ after him, and that each faction of it would set apart for itself a doctrine it fashioned by its own opinions and its own deficient minds, and would prefer over the community of guidance — the guides, upon whom be the blessings of God — one for whom God has made no light; and knowing that the preachers would suffer from the divided community every hardship in their standing forth against them, in their being injured and in the assailing of their doctrine and of their masters, the pure Imams, upon whom be the peace of God, granted to the preachers who uphold the summons a license to look into the sciences that are not of their own belief, upon conditions he laid down, that these might be for them as equipment in their disputations. He said:

> لَيْسَ عَلَى ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ جُنَاحٌۭ فِيمَا طَعِمُوٓا۟ إِذَا مَا ٱتَّقَوا۟ وَّآمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ

"There is no blame upon those who believe and do righteous deeds for what they have eaten, so long as they fear God and believe and do righteous deeds," meaning: there is no blame — that is, no constraint — upon those who uphold the summons to the التَّوْحِيد of God and to the knowledge of His limits by way of the deputies (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), those who make the respondents believe when they take hold of the rope of God and cleave to their guardianship, delivering them from doubts and confusions in the religion of God and bringing them to the degree of perfection and attainment; there is no blame upon them in what they have eaten — that is, in what they read of the sciences that are not of their belief — so that these become for them a weapon with which they do battle against the adversaries in the establishing of the truth, so long as they fear and believe and do righteous deeds. This is a condition from Him.

The first condition is that their path be kept straight in obedience to God Almighty, in following His Messenger (ع) and believing in His limits and upholding the two worships together according to what He brought. The second condition, in "then fear Him and believe," is that they obey the Messenger in his legatee and in the one who upholds the affair in his stead, and that they raise the summons to him, outwardly and inwardly. The third condition, in "then fear Him and do good," is that they obey the Imam in the one he sets up and invests with the affair, and that they do well in the summons to the التَّوْحِيد of God under his imamate. When they do this and stand by these conditions, there is no harm upon them in looking into those doctrines and sciences that belong to other peoples, and in pondering them, and there is no constraint upon their religion in that; for it is a help to them in what they need for withstanding the enemies and whoever seeks to assail the limits.

This is the interpretation of the verse, and there is in it no license to leave off the use of the sacred law; it is only a license to look into the sciences upon the conditions mentioned. The sum of the matter is that every interpretation leading to the permitting of what God has forbidden, or the forbidding of what He has permitted, or the altering of the ranks of the limits of God Almighty — and which is a slighting of the sacred law and of the rites of the upright religion, and a summons to leave off working by it and to acquire happiness by refraining from it — is a corrupt interpretation. Our masters, the pure Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), are innocent before God Almighty of what he interpreted, and of every interpretation that contradicts what the Prophet (ع) brought.

The twenty-fourth section of the eighth chapter.

The author of al-Nusra said:

The decree (الْقَضَاء) is the beginning, the measuring-out (اَلتَّقْدِير) is the middle, and the completion (اَلتَّمَام) is the end, because the measuring-out comes about only through the decree.

Then he said:

If the decree were the end, it would not be right that it be named a decree, for the decree is only before everything, and things come after the decree, and the decree is for some thing; but the completion is not for some thing — rather, the completion is for the very thing that has become complete.

We say:

In what has gone before of the discourse, the answer concerning the decree and the measuring-out is already folded up, and there is in it sufficiency, without our taking in the whole of the discourse upon it and drawing it out at length in a way that might be ascribed to us as other than what we intended.

## 10. Adam, Noah, and the Origin of the Law

He spoke about it in the chapter of the Law of Adam and the اَلْوِلَايَة of Noah (ع).

## The First Section

The author of *al-Islah* said:

Whoever claims that the first of the speaker-prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) had no Shariah is mistaken. He is mistaken again in counting him among the possessors of determination — as we have explained — because determination arises only through the abrogation of a Shariah or the renewal of another. The first Speaker had no prior Shariah to abrogate, so that the name of determination could fall upon him, as we have already argued. Rather, he was the first to draw the lines of religion in command and prohibition.

Whoever studies the laws set down by the rest of the speaker-prophets in their apparent Shariahs — from which are inferred the subtleties of what those laws recommend, the meanings of the parables called interpretation, which is the manifest wording of the wise — knows that every law set down by the speaker-prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), every command, prohibition, and act of servitude, is a Shariah, whether small or great, whether little or much. Each Speaker composes only as much as he is commanded, and no more. Some composed much; others were confined to a little, restricted to drawing statutes and setting down their wording. We see, for example, that fasting in the Shariah of Moses (ع) is less than it is in the Shariah of Jesus (ع), and in the Shariah of Jesus there is more than in the Shariah of Muhammad (ع). Likewise all the other rulings of religion increase and diminish according to the difference of their cycles and the measure of what each was commanded.

No one may hold that this great cycle — the longest of them all — passed without work, that its people went on without profit, without apparent statutes whose rulings they observed. For if there were no work, no profit, and no apparent statutes, then there is no interpretation, no inner meaning, and no true summons. The summons is raised upon the Shariah; interpretation requires revelation; the inner requires the outer; the meaning requires the parable. If there is no outer, there is no inner, no summons, and no Shariah — and creation is left neglected, its people ignorant. Knowledge goes together with work. This is the origin of wisdom among the wise, as they have said:

> "اَلْحِكْمَةُ هِيَ عَمَلٌ مَعَ عِلْمٍ"
>
> "Wisdom is action with knowledge."

It is impossible to say that the people of that cycle lived on such a path.

Rather, we say:

The first Speaker earned the name of speaking-prophecy by composing a Shariah. Without it, he would not have been counted among the six speaker-prophets, even if it were incumbent upon him to establish a foundation that calls to the التَّوْحِيد of Allah Almighty. Had he no Shariah and no summons, those who belonged to him in his cycle would have had no statutes by which to stand, and could not have prepared those who came after to receive the laws of the remaining speaker-prophets. This is a diseased belief. It is not permissible to say that there was no revelation in that cycle and no Shariah, because command and prohibition come only by inspiration from Allah, and the manifesting of Allah's command is revelation. When command, prohibition, and revelation are established, the Shariah is established in the manifestations of its statutes. This indicates that the first Speaker had a Shariah, over and above what the necessary proof already indicates, according to what the wise have said. Allah Almighty said:

> "واتل عليهم نبأ ابن آدم بالحق اذ قرّبا قرباناً فتقبل من احدهما ولم يتقبل من الآخر"

"And recite to them the tidings of the son of Adam in truth, when the two offered an offering, and it was accepted from one of them and not accepted from the other" (the verse).

The offering is one of the laws — among the most firmly established causes of the Shariah. Allah has commanded it in every Shariah and made it the root of devotion and purity. For this reason the offering is named a rite. Allah Almighty said:

> "وَلِكُلِّ أُمَّةٍۢ جَعَلْنَا مَنسَكًۭا لِّيَذْكُرُوا۟ ٱسْمَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَىٰ مَا رَزَقَهُم مِّنۢ بَهِيمَةِ ٱلْأَنْعَمِ"
>
> "And for every nation We have appointed a rite, that they may mention the name of Allah over what He has provided them of the beasts of cattle."

And He said:

> "ولكل امة جعلنا منسكا هم ناسكوه فلا ينازعنك"

"And for every nation We have appointed a rite which they perform, so let them not dispute with you" (the verse).

The rite is the place in which the offering is brought near. And in the hadith:

> "العرجاء تجر رجلها الى المنسك"
>
> "The lame ewe drags her leg to the rite."

The rite itself is the offering. A man is called a rite-keeper when he is known for religion, scrupulousness, and purity. The offering is called devotion because it is the utmost of the statutes of religion — the utmost of drawing near to Allah Almighty in every Shariah and among all nations — and it is the most firmly established of the causes of the Shariah. It is called an offering because by it they draw near to Allah Almighty, and through that nearness arise the first causes of obedience and disobedience, of disbelief and faith, of command and prohibition. It is the first of the statutes of the Shariah.

The author of *al-Nusrah* said:

The author of *al-Islah* argued for establishing the Shariah for Adam (ع) from the saying of Allah Almighty, "واتل عليهم نبأ ابن آدم بالحق اذ قرّبا قرباناً," and he claimed that the offering is one of the statutes of the laws — though before making this argument he had drawn out at length the distinction between determination and Shariah and multiplied his witnesses from the poetry of the Arabs, with no benefit beneath them and no proof that verifies what he wished to establish. But the matter in the interpretation of this verse is not as he supposed. If the offering in this place were what occurs in the apparent laws — the slaughter of sheep and camels — then it would be as he said. But the offering in this place is what does not occur in the apparent laws: the establishing of proofs from beneath their hands, and of those who stand in their place when they depart from the world. The interpretation of this story is apparent to the wise.

Had he read this story to its end, it would have been plain to him that Adam (ع) had no Shariah. One of his two sons did not bury his brother's corpse, and did not know how, until Allah sent a raven to scratch in the earth and show him how to bury it. The burying and interring of the dead, in whatever manner, is one of the laws. Had Adam had a Shariah, the practice of it would not have been hidden from him. This is his statement.

And we say:

The Shariah of Adam (ع) is established on two grounds: because what the author of *al-Islah* argued does establish it, and because what the author of *al-Nusrah* brought forward does not overturn it. The author of *al-Islah* argued for establishing the Shariah — one of the statutes the speaker-prophets set for the worship of Allah Almighty and the road to knowledge of the causes — from the apparent sense of the Book: from the verse in which the offering is mentioned, from the universally affirmative verse in Allah's saying, "وَلِكُلِّ أُمَّةٍۢ جَعَلْنَا مَنسَكًۭا لِّيَذْكُرُوا۟ ٱسْمَ ٱللَّهِ" (the verse), and from the other verse, "ولكل امة جعلنا منسكاً هم ناسكوه." The Shariah is thus established. The words of the author of *al-Nusrah* — "the matter in the interpretation of this verse is not as he supposed" — do not contradict the author of *al-Islah*, since the author of *al-Islah* did not argue from the interpretation of this verse but from its apparent sense. When the words of the author of *al-Islah* establish the Shariah of Adam (ع), and the reply of the author of *al-Nusrah* does not overturn it, the Shariah stands proven.

## The Second Section of the Ninth Discourse

And the words of the author of *al-Islah*:

That if the offering were what occurs only in the apparent laws — the slaughter of sheep and camels — then it would be as he mentioned, but the offering in this place is what does not occur in the laws, according to his interpretation: even this does not contradict the words of the author of *al-Islah*. Rather, it necessitates what he made necessary. His saying "only what is in the apparent laws" is itself an affirmation of the offering as one of the statutes of religion and of the apparent Shariah, even though he pushed aside the claim that what is meant by it is the kind of offering that occurs in the apparent laws. And his saying that the offering in this place does not occur in the apparent sense of the Shariah scarcely overturns the words of the author of *al-Islah*. What the author of *al-Nusrah* said follows the course of a summons established only by proof. It is entirely permissible that the apparent sense stand present and real alongside the inner sense — all the more so since this verse does not belong to the kind of parable whose interpretation is sought while its apparent sense is set aside. Since that is so, and the apparent sense of the verse fixes the work as established, the statutes of the Shariah of Adam (ع) are fixed.

## The Third Section of the Ninth Discourse

As for the words of the author of *al-Nusrah* — that the offering is the establishing of proofs from among those who stand in the place of the prophets — this itself requires that Adam have something to which those proofs call. The proofs, and those set up among the ranks, are established only to call to what the Speaker proclaims and sets down: that in which lies the welfare of those summoned, in this world and in religion, in the Hereafter and the first life. Adam (ع) cannot be other than a father who laid down the Shariah and enacted the statutes — the firmly binding matters. Had he laid down nothing, had he manifested no statute of the laws of worship, great or small, he would have had no need to establish proofs and send forth callers to stand in his place, for he would have left behind nothing that needs to be preserved and by which the community is governed. By the affirmation of the author of *al-Nusrah* — through his own interpretation — that Adam did establish proofs, the Shariah and the statutes are made necessary. The Shariah, then, belongs to Adam (ع).

## The Fourth Section of the Ninth Discourse

And after this we say:

The prophets (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) were needed to teach the community what would make it happy in the Hereafter and in this world, and to guide it to its interests in this world and in religion. Adam (ع) was a prophet; it necessarily follows that he was a teacher and a guide. If he must be a teacher and a guide, then what he teaches his community — that by which it is made happy and guided to its interests — must exist. And that can be nothing other than the laws of worship, which are the Shariah. The Shariah of Adam (ع) is therefore fixed. This is verified by the saying of Allah Almighty:

> "كَانَ ٱلنَّاسُ أُمَّةًۭ وَحِدَةًۭ"

"Mankind was one nation" — meaning in ignorance —

then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and as warners,

> "وَأَنزَلَ مَعَهُمُ ٱلْكِتَبَ بِٱلْحَقِّ لِيَحْكُمَ بَيْنَ ٱلنَّاسِ فِيمَا ٱخْتَلَفُوا۟ فِيهِ"

"and He sent down with them the Book in truth, that it might judge between the people in that wherein they differed" (the verse).

Adam (ع) is among the prophets. If he was a prophet, Allah did not send him except that he might bear glad tidings, warn, judge, and decree. Glad tidings, warning, judgment, and decree occur only through statutes and enactments. The statutes — which are the Shariah — are then fixed.

## The Fifth Section of the Ninth Discourse

And the author of *al-Nusrah* said:

Had he read this story to its end, it would have been plain to him that Adam had no Shariah. One of his two sons did not bury his brother's corpse, and did not know how, until Allah sent a raven to scratch in the earth and show him how to bury it. The burying and interring of the dead, in whatever manner, is one of the laws. Had Adam (ع) had a Shariah, the practice of it would not have been hidden from his two sons. This is a statement issued without inquiry and without examination.

And we say:

Had the author of *al-Islah* leaned on sophistry — as the author of *al-Nusrah* leaned on it against him in the matter of the verse on the offering, when he said, "the matter in the interpretation of the verse is not as he supposed" — then the author of *al-Nusrah*, in defending his position, could not escape one of two courses. Either he assigns to the verse he cited, in his effort to push away the Shariah of Adam (ع), an interpretation in which he stands alone and by which he argues — and then his argument for pushing away that Shariah is voided, since he first cited the verse by its apparent sense. Or he assigns it no interpretation at all and lets it run upon what its apparent sense conveys — and then it does not indicate anything of what he intended. For the verse does not indicate that the son of Adam, when the raven scratched in the earth, saw it and buried his brother and interred him, so that the argument from it should be established for him. The verse is:

> "فبعث الله غراباً ليبحث في الارض ليريه كيف يواري سوأة اخيه قال يا ويلتا أعجزت ان اكون مثل الغراب فأواري سوأة أخي فأصبح من النادمين"
>
> "So Allah sent a raven to scratch in the earth, to show him how to bury the corpse of his brother. He said: Woe to me, am I unable to be like this raven, that I might bury the corpse of my brother? And he became one of the regretful."

It does not mention that he buried it, nor does it indicate that he interred him.

**The Sixth Section of the Ninth Discourse**

Neither does the verse's interpretation nor its inner sense point to any of that. Both the opponent and the friend held a belief about the worship of Allah Almighty — each had something he offered, by which he drew near to Allah. The opponent held that worship consists in outward works alone, that deliverance rests on nothing else, and that whoever stands in the place of the Messenger (ع) is established by this belief and by sincerity in it. The friend held that the worship of Allah Almighty is the outward — which is the work — joined to the inward — which is knowledge — and that deliverance rests on both together, and that the one who stands in the place of the Messenger (ع) is the man the Messenger (ع) himself put forward and approved. The two of them disputed over this one day, arguing before the Prophet, and each made plain the belief by which he worshiped Allah.

This is the meaning of His saying, "إِذْ قَرَّبَا قُرْبَانًۭا" — each brought forward what he believed he drew near to Allah Almighty and to His Messenger by. "And it was accepted from one of them": this is the legatee — the Speaker who stands in the place of Allah approved his belief concerning Allah and His worship. "And it was not accepted from the other": this is the opponent — the Speaker did not approve his belief, for it was not upon its proper face. The Speaker (ع) had approved the friend's belief without declaring himself openly, out of tenderness, lest the opponent accuse him of inclining toward the friend who stood beneath him. And so he said:

> "إِنَّ أَكْرَمَكُمْ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ أَتْقَىٰكُمْ"
>
> "Indeed the most noble of you in the sight of Allah is the most God-fearing of you."

The opponent had heard him (ع) say, "اتقاكم ابو بكر" — "the most God-fearing of you is Abu Bakr." Hearing that from the Messenger, he concluded that the verse had been recited only to approve that man's belief and way, and that he was the one who would stand after him. He rose — and what sat in the opponent's heart was the conviction that the friend had prevailed. The opponent said to him:

"لَأَقْتُلَنَّك"

"I will surely kill you,"

meaning: I will drive you from this belief that was not approved from you, and from the standing you reckon is yours. The friend said to him:

> "إِنَّمَا يَتَقَبَّلُ ٱللَّهُ مِنَ ٱلْمُتَّقِينَ"
>
> "Allah accepts only from the God-fearing,"

meaning that the one who stands in the place of Allah — who is the Messenger — does not appoint anyone in his place, and does not approve a belief from anyone, save from those who are God-fearing: those who join knowledge to work and draw near to Allah Almighty by raising the landmarks of both together. The opponent then turned to reviling and disparaging the friend, claiming he knew nothing. When the two of them brought their belief before the Prophet (ع), he approved one over the other. The friend said:

> "لَىِٕنۢ بَسَطتَ إِلَىَّ يَدَكَ لِتَقْتُلَنِى مَآ أَنَا۠ بِبَاسِطٍۢ يَدِىَ إِلَيْكَ لِأَقْتُلَكَ إِنِّىٓ أَخَافُ ٱللَّهَ رَبَّ ٱلْعَلَمِينَ"
>
> "If you stretch out your hand against me to kill me, I will not stretch out my hand against you to kill you. Indeed I fear Allah, the Lord of the worlds,"

meaning: if you make war on me, loose your tongue against me, lower my standing, speak beyond what is due, and are not content with what I believe concerning the worship of Allah, the Lord of the worlds — I will not do the same to you, nor will I revile you. I stand under the command and the obedience of the Speaker who holds the place of Allah, who is the master of the cycle and the lord of the imams. I fear him, and I do not put myself forward.

**Adam, Noah, and the Origin of the Law**

This word of the guardian worked upon the opponent, so that he slackened from what he had set himself upon — and yet his own soul made the killing of his brother easy for him. His soul rose against the second opponent, and he ran on with the first opponent, the one with whom alone he would resolve nothing, bind nothing, and take no counsel. That is: when the first opponent recalled what had befallen the second, he said to him, "There is no belief in Allah and in His worship except what I have said" — meaning that the guardian is astray. He made the affair seem good to the man: lowering the guardian, barring him from his rank, charging him with knowing nothing. He thrust him from his station, overcame him, and said, "Worship is nothing but this outward observance, and not what this man says to you." And so the opponent became one of the losers, worshiping Allah by the obedience of the Speaker without the obedience of the Foundation, and by the outward without the inward.

Then Allah sent a raven scratching in the earth:

> فَبَعَثَ ٱللَّهُ غُرَابًۭا يَبْحَثُ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ

That is: when the term had lengthened and the end of the opponent drew near, the guardian wished to complete the proof against him, so that he might leave the world only after a reminder. He sent to him — and this is the meaning of "And Allah sent" — the one who stands in the place of the Speaker, who himself stands in the place of Allah, sent a man from among his companions. That man was to make plain to the opponent his affair and his station, to reveal what Allah Almighty holds in the secret of His silence over the seeking of his right, to open a way out from what he had brought upon his own soul, and to move him to return the right to its people. This is the meaning of the raven's scratching in the earth: to show him how to free himself from what he had brought upon himself, and to guide him to his duty. The man the guardian of Allah sent for this was Muhammad ibn Abi Bakr (مُحَمَّدٌ بْنُ أَبِي بَكْرٍ) — bound to the guardian from the side of religion, and to the opponent from the side of lineage. Muhammad did what he was sent for: the opponent was stirred to awareness of his crime, his soul softened, and he inclined toward binding himself to the guardian's mandate. But the second opponent prevailed over his judgment and barred him from what he had resolved upon — to acknowledge the guardian's right. He grieved, and said within himself when he was prevented:

> يَا وَيْلَتَا أَعَجَزْتُ أَنْ أَكُونَ مِثْلَ هَٰذَا الْغُرَابِ

"O woe to me! Was I unable to be like this raven?" That is: "Would that I were in the standing of this man" — meaning Muhammad his son, who is a stranger to the guardian and yet is his like in guarding the covenant of Allah, His religion, and the secrets of the guardian of Allah. He became one who regretted what he had missed in following the guardian from the first, and regretted the deed that would remove him at the last to the abode of the Hereafter. We seek refuge in Allah from breaking the covenant while arguing by it.

If this is so, then the author of al-Nusrah has nothing in his argument by way of this verse, except the saying of the author of al-Islah concerning what he held obligatory — the ordinances of the Shariah — for Adam (ع).

**The Seventh Section of the Ninth Chapter**

It has been established that the laws, dispensations, ordinances, and observances may increase and decrease according to the time in which the Speaker arises and according to what the setting-right of the community requires. Narrations have come down that the washing of the dead, the embalming, the shrouding, and the burial are from the law of Adam (ع). If those reports have come in unbroken succession, as has been mentioned, then this verse — in what it relates of the raven and the scratching in the earth at the killing of the son of Adam — runs only in the course of the parables whose interpretation is to be sought and whose outward sense is not to be taken literally, as we have done and interpreted. The Shariah of Adam (ع) is thus established beyond dispute.

**The Eighth Section of the Ninth Chapter**

We say further: the argument from this verse against the ordinances of the Shariah of Adam (ع) is itself void. Burial of the dead is not among those matters — purity, prayer, zakat, and the rest of the acts — whose ordinances, if lifted, would bring down all the other ordinances of the laws that are the foundations of worship. Burial is one kind among the kinds of what is done with the dead: burning with fire, casting into water, carrying to a cave or a wilderness, burying in the earth, or feeding to the beasts. Since it is one kind among the kinds of a Shariah ordinance — one for which another kind may be substituted — the lifting of the ordinance of burial cannot void the ordinances of the whole law. And since that is impossible, the Shariah is established for Adam (ع).

**The Ninth Section of the Ninth Chapter**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Adam (ع) was not the possessor of a law, for had he been the possessor of a law, the command of Allah Almighty to the angels to prostrate to him would have been by his mediation.

We say:

By this very saying, the author of al-Nusrah has himself made it binding that Adam (ع) is the possessor of a law. For the command of Allah Almighty to the angels to prostrate — though outwardly it does not come from the side of Adam (ع) — was only by his mediation. The proof is this: the command to prostrate to him would be absurd if he were not chosen; it is not absurd; therefore he is chosen. That he is chosen is established by the saying of Allah Almighty:

> إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ ٱصْطَفَىٰٓ آدَمَ وَنُوحًۭا

"Indeed Allah chose Adam and Noah," the verse. Since he is chosen, it is absurd that Allah should command those below the chosen except by the mediation of the chosen. The command of Allah to the angels to prostrate to Adam was therefore by his mediation; and since it was by his mediation, the law is established for him by his own word.

**The Tenth Section of the Ninth Chapter**

Furthermore, the command of Allah to prostrate to Adam (ع) — in His saying,

> ٱسْجُدُوا۟ لِآدَمَ

"Prostrate to Adam" — is like His command to the Children of Israel in His saying:

> يا بني اسرائيل اذكروا نعمتي التي انعمت عليكم وأوفوا بعهدي اوف بعهدكم وإياي فارهبون وآمنوا بما انزلت مصدقاً لما معكم ولا تكونوا اول كافر ولا تشتروا بآياتي ثمناً قليلاً وإياي فاتقون ولا تلبسوا الحق بالباطل وتكتموا الحق وانتم تعلمون وأقيموا الصلاة وآتوا الزكاة واركعوا مع الراكعين
>
> "O Children of Israel, remember My favor which I bestowed upon you, and fulfill My covenant, and I shall fulfill your covenant, and Me alone hold in awe. And believe in what I have sent down, confirming what is with you, and be not the first to disbelieve in it, and sell not My signs for a small price, and Me alone be mindful of. And confound not the truth with falsehood, nor conceal the truth while you know. And establish the prayer, and give the zakat, and bow with those who bow."

This command, though outwardly in a form that does not point to him being a mediator, reached the Children of Israel by the mediation of Muhammad. Were it permissible to say that Muhammad has no law — on the ground that the verse contains no mention of Allah's command to the Children of Israel being by his mediation — and were the two verses placed together in the class of things that do not point to a single, well-rooted mediator, this would be absurd. For the command of Allah Almighty to the Children of Israel, though outwardly bare of any mention of a mediator, was only by the mediation of Muhammad. And since that is absurd, Adam is the possessor of the law, and the law is established for him.

**The Eleventh Section of the Ninth Chapter**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

The angels are not charged with obligation, nor is the practice of the law incumbent upon them, for they disobey Allah not for the blink of an eye; rather they glorify the praise of their Lord, and do what they are commanded.

We say:

The angels who were charged with prostration — had they not been of those upon whom obligation is laid, Allah Almighty would not have charged them with prostrating to Adam, nor commanded them by His saying, "Prostrate to Adam." As for the angels who bear no obligation, they are those who were never in the abode of body, nor ever standing in potency, but came forth into act; these are the primal ones in the abode of eternity. What is meant here are the charged angels — the assembly who were in the abode of nature and possessed the excellences by which they resemble those in the abode of eternity: the angels of origination and of emanation, as we have explained before, since none advances save one who is in the abode of origination and emanation. As for prostration and the like — from among the ordinances of worship and works — these are found only in the world of nature. Those who are charged are charged only with establishing the two worships, so that by them they may attain, of increase and of the blessings of felicity, what raises their standing before Allah, and attain the completeness that gains them enduring and abiding. For this reason the angels were charged and called to the work.

**The Twelfth Section of the Ninth Chapter**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Had the prostration of the angels to Adam been a law for them, Adam would have said, "Allah commands you to prostrate and to obey me," and he would have striven against whoever opposed him in Allah's command, as all the Speakers did.

We say:

The author of al-Islah may reasonably reply: it does not follow, from Adam's silence on the matter — his not having said, "Allah commands you to prostrate to me and to obey me" — that prostration must therefore fall outside the ordinances of the law. The Prophet (ع) did not say to the Children of Israel, "Allah commands you to establish the prayer and to give the zakat to me," and that was no proof that prayer, zakat, and everything else he commanded them were not among the ordinances of the law. Moreover, if a law required its bearer to be one who strives, then Jesus (ع) would have had no law appointed for him, since he did not strive — and that is plainly untenable.

**The Thirteenth Section of the Ninth Chapter**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Furthermore, Allah Almighty did not mention in the Quran that Adam (ع) had a people and followers, nor did He mention his being sent as He mentioned others when He said,

> إِنَّآ أَرْسَلْنَا نُوحًا

"Indeed We sent Noah"; and He did not number him among the bearers of the law in His saying,

> شَرَعَ لَكُم مِّنَ ٱلدِّينِ مَا وَصَّىٰ بِهِۦ نُوحًۭا وَٱلَّذِىٓ أَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَيْكَ وَمَا وَصَّيْنَا بِهِۦٓ إِبْرَهِيمَ وَمُوسَىٰ وَعِيسَىٰٓ أَنْ أَقِيمُوا۟ ٱلدِّينَ وَلَا تَتَفَرَّقُوا۟

"He has laid down for you of the religion that which He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to you, and that which We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: that you establish the religion and be not divided therein," the verse.

We say: the author of al-Nusrah has been neither fair in his argument nor attentive to what the author of al-Islah actually says in his book. Adam is indeed mentioned in the Quran, and his people and followers are the angels commanded to prostrate to him — those who learned from him the names, as Allah Almighty commanded in His saying,

> يَا آدَمُ أَنْبِئْهُمْ بِأَسْمَائِهِمْ

"O Adam, inform them of their names." As for the mention of his being sent, no sending is greater than his. The sending of messengers was made obligatory only so that they might serve as the vicegerents of Allah in His earth, since Allah Almighty is beyond direct apprehension; and Allah Almighty has stated plainly in His Book that He made Adam His vicegerent in the earth, to whom its people would turn for what they needed in the affairs of their religion. As for his absence from the verse that names the bearers of the law, the author of al-Islah has explained this: Adam was not among the resolute — not one of firm resolve — and so he was not numbered with them. And if it were permissible to exclude Adam from the company of the possessors of the laws simply because his name does not appear alongside the five (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), then by the same reasoning — because he is not mentioned among those to whom revelation was sent in the saying of Allah Almighty,

> إِنَّآ أَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَيْكَ كَمَآ أَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَىٰ نُوحٍۢ وَٱلنَّبِيِّۦنَ مِنۢ بَعْدِهِۦ وَأَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَىٰٓ إِبْرَهِيمَ وَإِسْمَعِيلَ وَإِسْحَقَ وَيَعْقُوبَ وَٱلْأَسْبَاطِ وَعِيسَىٰ وَأَيُّوبَ وَيُونُسَ وَهَرُونَ وَسُلَيْمَنَ

"Indeed We have revealed to you as We revealed to Noah and the prophets after him; and We revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, the tribes, Jesus, Job, Jonah, Aaron, and Solomon," the verse — it would be equally permissible to conclude that he falls outside their company there too. That is absurd. The saying, then, is the saying of the author of al-Islah, and no one else's.

**The Fourteenth Section of the Ninth Chapter**

The author of al-Nusrah said:

We must also consider what cause makes a law necessary, so that we may properly conclude that Adam (ع) had none.

We say:

In his view, what makes a law obligatory is the neglect of limits and their suspension, creation's following of their opposites, and its claiming for those opposites what Allah did not place in them. This condition was not present, nor did it appear, in the days of Adam — for those who came before him and those who were his people were keeping to the limits, and not one of them had violated a single one, as they declared of themselves when they said,

> أَتَجْعَلُ فِيهَا مَن يُفْسِدُ فِيهَا وَيَسْفِكُ ٱلدِّمَآءَ وَنَحْنُ نُسَبِّحُ بِحَمْدِكَ وَنُقَدِّسُ لَكَ

"Will You place therein one who works corruption in it and sheds blood, while we glorify You with praise and sanctify You?" the verse. Therefore, he argues, there was no obligation upon Adam to establish a law.

We say: the reason for the necessity of a law is something other than what the author of al-Nusrah has supposed — that it lies in the neglect of limits, their suspension, and creation's following of their opposites. That does not make a law obligatory, nor does it bring one into being. But let us grant his terms and speak from within them. As for the true reason for the necessity of the law — the sum of the ordinances of deeds — we have set it out in our book known as al-Masabih fi al-Imamah. The most immediate ground is the need of human beings, first of all and according to their ranks, in their very survival, for what guards them against destruction: the ordinances by which blood is preserved, and by which honor, life, and wealth are safeguarded, and by which they are made whole in the necessary matters that arise among them. Without such ordinances, their affairs would come to a standstill and they would destroy one another; the strong would press upon the one weaker than him, out of desire for what that person possesses, and the fires of discord would be kindled among them. Since it is impossible, in the wisdom of the Maker Almighty, that He should deny His creatures what guards them against destruction, the ordinances that are the law are of necessity present.

Then we say: the existence of these laws — the drafting of them — is itself a necessary existence, because the souls of human beings do not hold themselves back from one another in the acts that belong to each. From those acts there arises what makes human beings unable to do without ordinances that steady the community, so that by holding fast to those ordinances each person is made secure from the other. And once the existence of these ordinances is established as necessary, we add: some are necessary in every age and season, and some are not. Those that are not may be present at one time and absent at another, and may increase or decrease.

What is of necessary existence includes: drawing near to Allah; the ordinance of restraint and retribution for offenses against life and person; the ordinance of marriage; the division of wealth, such as inheritance and its like; the ordinance for the burial of the dead; and the ordinance for the recovery of rights and their return to their owners. The reason lies in the nature of human beings, for they possess three souls.

The first is the rational soul, which possesses discrimination and thought. It knows that it has a Creator, submits to Him by nature, and through that submission draws near to Him; and in drawing near it requires ordinances by which to fulfill what it seeks, whether those ordinances are many or few.

The second is the irascible soul, by which a person is moved to anger, and which loves mastery, domination, revenge, oppression, killing, and injustice. This soul makes ordinances necessary — ordinances by which one person's violence is turned back from another, and by which the right of the wronged is extracted from the wrongdoer who has transgressed: in the case of life, by force; in the case of the body, by retribution; and otherwise by what stands in place of blood-money.

The third is the appetitive soul, which seeks pleasures and is driven of necessity to seek marriage, so that it too makes ordinances necessary — ordinances by which holding fast to them removes the calamity of jealousy that would otherwise drive men to killing.

This appetitive soul also seeks, of necessity, to gather all wealth to itself by whatever means it can, so that human beings require ordinances by which they are made secure against the cheating, cunning, and deceit that would lead them to contention and combat. These souls, in their eagerness, stir in the pursuit of livelihood, making dealings between people a necessity; and since treachery and cunning are set in human nature, people require ordinances by which claimant and denier are brought to their proper course in recovering the truth for its rightful owner. Death, too, is necessary among them, and human beings cannot do without some means of removing the body of the dead from their midst — whether by burial, by burning, by casting it into the valleys, or by some other way — for otherwise harm would fall back upon them, and so each person is driven to adopt one of these acts. These are six gates that run in the manner of genera, and beneath them fall the particular kinds whose existence is necessary among human beings without exception.

What is not of necessary existence — what is greater at one time and less at another — is the ordinances of worship and of drawing near to Allah through the knowledge of His limits, by which completeness is attained; this falls under the genus of drawing near to Allah. All these ordinances belong to the law, and there must first be one who lays them down and establishes them among human beings at the beginning of their existence. Since the existence of these ordinances comes with the existence of human beings, and since Adam (ع) was himself among human beings — he and those who were with him — the law is established for him.

As for whoever was before Adam, his case is the case of one who will come at the end of the seventh cycle — like for like. He was not free of the ordinances whose existence is necessary, those we have named; he was free only of the ordinances that fall under the genus of worship in drawing near to Allah, those which, as we have said, at times increase and at times diminish, and by which the second completeness is gained. So Allah sent Adam (ع) to teach them and others what they had not known, and to bring them to completeness where they were deficient. Though they were distinguished above others by their excellence, they fell short of the degree of completeness through their leaving off the work of worship. He therefore laid down the law, believed, and called to the two acts of worship in knowledge and in deed. Some followed and obeyed; others refused and turned away, following the pattern of the six who had come before at the beginning of the cycles. Such was the case with those people.

The author of al-Nusrah said: It became obligatory upon Noah (ع) to establish the law because, before his cycle, there had passed cycles that violated the limits and followed their opposites — such as the people of Wadd (اَلْوَدّ), Suwa (*al-Suwaa* (اَلسُّوَاع)), Yaghuth (*Yaghooth* (يَغُوث)), and others. It was therefore obligatory upon Noah (ع) to establish it, so that whoever perished might perish upon clear evidence, and whoever lived might live upon clear evidence.

We say: this argument of his does not establish that Adam had no law; on the contrary, it requires that Adam too had ordinances and a law. For he has granted that a cycle turned upon the violation of limits and the following of their opposites — and violation belongs only to a follower with respect to the one followed, in his abandoning that one's command and ordinance. By granting the existence of violation in his own words, he grants the existence of the ordinances that are the law — the very ordinances against which the violation occurred — and so the law is established for Adam (ع).

The author of al-Islah said: It has been narrated that Allah, the Mighty and Majestic, commanded Adam (ع) to pitch a tent that He sent down to him from the Garden, making it his qiblah and the qiblah of his children. Seth then built, after him, the place of the law — made it firm, gathered within it the revelation and its interpretation, established its branches and its roots, and set in order the sound arrangement of its obligations.

We say: the saying of the author of al-Islah is sound, for Adam has a law and ordinances, as do all the other Speakers.

The author of al-Nusrah said: In the composition of the world and its ordering, as proof of divine oneness, there is already that which makes the setting down of works and appointed rites unnecessary. Moreover, the setting down of works and signs has turned many people away from divine oneness and led them into likening Allah to His creatures — something utterly impossible. The composition of the world, with the ordering and wonders of its wisdom — what lies in the joining of bodies, the fitting together of frames, and the pairing of opposite natures in distinct bodies and measured forms — all of this, as proof of divine oneness, renders one independent of the setting down of outward works: the like-actions and differing arrangements that point to agreed meanings, the parables set up for a subsisting original, and the proofs established for a plain object of proof. What is already present in these proofs, in these outwardly contradictory deeds, and in the parables struck to establish known limits is itself proof of the One, since the firm, precise, and wondrous acts of the world and of souls make the additional testimony of works and signs unnecessary. Indeed, many of these signs, works, and outward expressions have led most people away from divine oneness and driven them into a likening that would be unfitting even for the least of His creatures. In sum, his argument is this: the composition and ordering of the world already furnish sufficient proof of divine oneness, and the setting down of works and rites has in fact turned much of creation away from that oneness and cast them into likening Allah to His creatures.

We say: this argument of his renders the sending of the messengers a needless excess. For if the world's firm and precise order points to divine oneness, and if the messengers (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) were sent to call people to that oneness and direct them toward it, then sending them when a proof already stands — when something already accomplishes the very work for which they were sent — is a needless excess. I do not know how this escaped him, since it is plain that the world's firmness and precision is not, by itself, sufficient proof of divine oneness, unless someone is present who knows that proof and makes it plain to others. One profits from knowledge of the world's composition, its ordering, and the arrangement of its existents only through one whose rank in knowledge has been raised by the limits, who has been taught the manner of the works and signs that revelation and law bring together, so that he may reflect, deduce, and draw from them what he seeks of the knowledge of Allah's limits and of those signs whose knowledge is made obligatory upon human beings. Allah says:

> [ حَتَّىٰٓ إِذَا جَآءُو قَالَ أَكَذَّبْتُم بِـَٔايَتِى وَلَمْ تُحِيطُوا۟ بِهَا عِلْمًا ]

Until, when they come, He will say: Did you deny My signs when you did not encompass them in knowledge? And if you do know, then the foremost in existence are the noble substances that are the angels brought near. The same holds when there is need of works, signs, arousing, and teaching, since not everyone who has known, worked, and been aroused is able to draw a true inference from the existents. How much less, then, one who does not know at all? In sum, the argument that leads to the nullifying of the ranks of the limits is not one we hold, nor one that belongs to the doctrine of our masters (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ). Perhaps this argument was only planted in the book by adversaries, so that it might be ascribed to the book's author. And Allah knows best.

Furthermore, the works are not set down for divine oneness alone. They are set down for the straightening of the soul in worship, for the likening of it to the highest assembly, and for its acquiring, through discipline, the noble traits and generous habits by which it is made ready to unite with what is above it — and for its being drawn out from the baseness of potentiality, so that the honor of the intellect is preserved through its applying itself to the obligations and keeping to their marks. Were it not for the works and rites within the community, and the completeness of souls that comes through perseverance in them and taking them up, the souls would perish. For this reason Allah did not concede to the angels when they reckoned that the worship they were already engaged in was sufficient in the matter of nearness to Him and of their completeness, and that they had no need, in what they were upon, of a vicegerent to teach them anything or command them to work. He said:

> [ إِنِّىٓ أَعْلَمُ مَا لَا تَعْلَمُونَ ]

I know what you do not know. The Almighty said this only because the states of being, by His decree and His wisdom, are set to waver between potency and act: subsisting in potency at one time, and in act at another. A thing therefore, when it reaches the utmost of what it may reach in potency and act, turns backward: if it was in potency, it moves toward act; if it was in act, it moves toward potency. Consider the hot thing: when it reaches the limit of its heat, the heat is spent and coldness appears, as in what melts among bodies and the like. Consider the cold thing: when it reaches the limit of its coldness, the coldness is spent and heat appears, as with snow. Consider the compacted mass whose parts are gathered from the mountains: when it reaches the utmost of that for which it was compacted, which is dryness, dissolution appears in its parts, so that it scatters and returns to sand. The same is true of many other things among the existents in the abode of nature.

So too were knowledge and ignorance in the world of coming-to-be and passing-away: at one time ignorance subsisting in act and knowledge in potency — meaning that ignorance was general through the abundance of its people, and knowledge was lost through the fewness of its own. That was the time of ignorance's dominion and its reaching its utmost in act, and of knowledge's being hidden and reaching the utmost of its end in potency, through the effacing of the traces of the teachers and the disruption of the marks of the laws and works as the age drew out and times passed. By the laws here I mean what is not of necessary existence among the ordinances and rites. Those distinguished by the name of angels were in themselves the best — their most excellent were the bearers of knowledge in that age, those who worshiped Allah upon an edge — and it was held that what they were upon was sufficient in the matter of worship and of completeness.

Yet the matter was other than what they reckoned, for they were in need, for their completeness, of the knowledge of the limits foremost in existence, those in the abode of origination and the first arising, and of the knowledge of those later in existence, like the second arising, and of the work by which they might gain completeness. Since they did not know that their time was the time of the completion of the cycle of ignorance, and that they were not in the cycle of knowledge nor in the cycle of works, the Almighty knew that to leave them upon what they were upon — while ignorance had reached its utmost and the time was the time of knowledge's beginning to appear — would be at odds with wisdom. So the Mighty and Majestic said, in effect: I know of the arrival of ignorance in the world at its utmost in act, and the completion of its cycle and its manifestation; and of the arrival of knowledge in the world at its utmost in potency, and of the return of the turning back to it in manifestation, and of the rising need, as it appears in act, for the setting up of teachers and guides — what you do not know.

So Allah sent Adam (ع), making him His vicegerent, that he might bring knowledge forth, gain them completeness in act, guide them to the knowledge of the limits, and teach them what they had not known. This was the beginning of the matter, the coming forth of knowledge from potency into act, through the presence of its people from his days (ع); and its end extends to the الْقَائِم. Likewise it is said that when the الْقَائِم appears, knowledge becomes in act — meaning that it becomes general and its people multiply — and ignorance is hidden, meaning that its people grow few and its root is cut off. What is said in the narration, that the treasures of the earth appear in his days and it casts them up to its surface, is a sign of the الْقَائِم pointing to the standing of knowledge in act, so that all that was laid up in the bellies of the revealed books and the knotted symbols appears.

And since the angels — with the presence of the heavens, the beauty of their ordering, and the precision of the composition of the world's existents — still did not know, and had no completeness, and drew no inference from them, except by what Adam (ع) made general among them and called them to of work, then what the author of al-Nusrah mentioned is not thoroughly examined, and the works, the signs, and the parables are not to be dispensed with in the matter of inference.

If that is so, then Adam (ع) is the author of a Sharia and of customs to which he called, and by which he pointed to the limits of Allah and to His unity. For what was indispensable to them concerning monotheism, politics, the correction of the soul, and its discipline lay in coming to know the limits and in bringing that knowledge out into act.

The twenty-first section of the ninth chapter.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Does the author of this article claim that the creation, in the great cycle, did not know, since there was no manifest, similar law among them? Or does he claim that the الْقَائِم, in raising the represented laws, nullifies the knowledge of monotheism and drives people out into the assertion that all obligation is suspended? For if the creation was not ignorant of monotheism, and is likewise not ignorant of it in the cycle of the الْقَائِم even when the laws are raised, then it would have been possible for Adam (ع) to have called people to monotheism without any references or striking of parables, and the like of this he says.

We say:

Our earlier discussion of knowledge and ignorance — of how each of them is at one time in potency and at another in act — already unfolds the explanation of the state in which the creation stood in the great cycle. If the creation was at the very end of ignorance, as the author of al-Nusrah's own argument requires, then Adam's call (ع) and his being a teacher to his followers in the landmarks of monotheism would be a mere superfluity that was not needed, since he has made it necessary that the people already knew monotheism. And if they already knew monotheism, then Adam's calling them to a thing they already knew would be nothing but a needless superfluity, and that is impossible. For the people were in fact in need of that by which their own selves are perfected. It was for this that Allah sent Adam (ع) to them, made him His caliph among them, chose him out from among them, and made obedience to him and return to him in what they did not know obligatory upon them. As for the saying of the assembly named the angels,

> ((وَنَحْنُ نُسَبِّحُ بِحَمْدِكَ وَنُقَدِّسُ لَكَ))

"And we glorify You with Your praise and sanctify You," this was their own estimation that they had reached the utmost in glorification, sanctification, and monotheism, and that what they were already in was sufficient in the matter of worship and in the knowledge of the limits. But Allah, exalted, knew of their affair that which His mercy made obligatory: the choosing of Adam from among them and the sending of him to them, so as to make him a caliph in their instruction, that he might call them to that by which their perfection comes, of work and knowledge together, which leads to the knowledge of the limits within which monotheism is sound. For this He said:

> ((إِنِّىٓ أَعْلَمُ مَا لَا تَعْلَمُونَ))

"I know what you do not know," because He, exalted, knew that they were deficient, that ignorance had reached its end in act, that work had come to the point of beginning to appear, and that they did not know this. So wisdom made it obligatory to establish the office of instruction, which is the cause of the spreading of knowledge and the increase of its people. Allah chose Adam, and he legislated and taught, as Allah, exalted, said:

> ((وعلم آدم الأسماء كلها ثم عرضهم على الملائكة فقال انبئوني بأسماء هؤلاء ان كنتم صادقين قالوا سبحانك لا علم لنا إلا ما علمتنا انك انت العليم الحكيم قال يا آدم انبئهم باسمائهم فلما انبأهم باسمائهم قال الم اقل لكم اني اعلم غيب السماوات والارض))

"And He taught Adam the names, all of them, then He set them before the angels and said: Inform Me of the names of these, if you are truthful. They said: Glory be to You, we have no knowledge except what You have taught us; You are the Knowing, the Wise. He said: O Adam, inform them of their names. And when he had informed them of their names, He said: Did I not tell you that I know the unseen of the heavens and the earth" — the verse. The people, then, were not knowing, and because they were so, Adam came to them as a teacher for them.

The twenty-second section of the ninth chapter.

We say:

As for his saying and his affirmation that the creation was not, at first, ignorant of monotheism in the cycle of the الْقَائِم: had they not been ignorant, they could only have derived it from the side of the confirming aids, in which lies the perfecting of their essences and their forms, and Adam would not have been chosen from among them to be the caliph of Allah, exalted, in teaching them what holds their welfare, sooner and later. It is impossible to hold both that conception of them and, at the same time, the granting of a rank that would belong to them. As for his saying, "nor in the cycle of the الْقَائِم are they ignorant": if what he means is the aspect the دَعْوَة takes — that the cycle of the الْقَائِم, before his appearance and before his coming to be out of the world of nature, is preserved by his caliphs and his summoners after the completion of the cycle of Muhammad al-Mustafa (ع) and his household, up to his own time and the appearance of his signs in the world — then that is true, for at that time knowledge has already raised high the beacon of its appearance through the multitude of the learned. This is the time of which Allah, exalted, said:

> ((يَوْمًۭا يَجْعَلُ ٱلْوِلْدَنَ شِيبًا))

"A day that will turn the children gray." But if what he means by his saying, "in the cycle of the الْقَائِم they are not ignorant," is that they are not ignorant after the الْقَائِم — who is the master of the seventh cycle and of the decisive word, at which the people of the Garden are set apart from the people of the Fire, the door of instruction is closed, and repentance is no longer valid — then it is not sound, for they do not remain knowing, nor are they of the people of knowledge; for that age is the age when the waters overflow and sink inward, and the age when dryness and barrenness prevail and come forth. The words of the author of al-Nusrah in this are not those of one who has examined the matter thoroughly.

In sum, the author of al-Nusrah brought forward these sayings and citations only in support of the author of al-Mahsul and his rejection of the Sharia of Adam. Yet Adam, as a summoner of his people, a prophet, a teacher, and a guide, cannot but have done one of three things: he called them by holding out the enticement of the Garden and nearness to the Lord of the Worlds; or he called them by warning them of the Fire and of being kept far from the mercy of the Best of Creators; or he called them by both together.

If he called them by enticement, then enticement — when the thing held out is not perceptible to the senses — can be conveyed only through the striking of parables and through describing it by way of what the senses already know: the kinds of delights, blessings, and goodly places, such as dwellings, gardens, rivers, and trees, so that its image may form within the listener. That is a form of speech that calls for interpretation. If he called them by warning, then likewise his speech can only proceed through the striking of parables drawn from the sensory world, so that they may picture what is to be feared; and the interpretation of that runs in the same channel as all that has gone before. If he called them by both together, then his speech was not devoid of a manifest sense that is acted upon, and so it too calls for interpretation.

In every case, all three possibilities make necessary a fixed, manifest sense belonging to Adam, upon which interpretation then follows. Adam, then, is the possessor of a Sharia and of manifest customs.

The twenty-fourth section of the ninth chapter.

Then we say:

Adam (ع) cannot but have called the people of his time to obedience to Allah, exalted, and to his own obedience, either with wisdom or without wisdom. He did not act without wisdom, for that is impossible, and to hold it is to depart from religion. Since, then, he called them with wisdom — and wisdom requires both knowledge and work together — Adam is the possessor of a Sharia that gathers knowledge and work into one. Those two are the worship of Allah; by them perfection is attained and the ascent to the stations of honor is made.

The twenty-fifth section of the ninth chapter.

As for the الْقَائِم and the saying that he raises the laws — we say: that position is unsound. The estimation of his coming to be out of the world of nature does not proceed by way of the second emanation, for he abolishes nothing of what pertains to the worship of Allah, exalted. Rather, he is the sealing of cycles that have passed and the perfecting of a matter completed in its own place. He removes the limits from their ranks by the occurrence of the cessation of instruction concerning them, and the completing of the matter in the new creation, of which Allah, exalted, said:

> ((بَلْ هُمْ لَيْسَ مِنْ خَلْقٍ جَدِيدٍ))

after He has established a judgment concerning the souls from which the course of the foundations of the masters of the past cycles is derived — those whose cycles are emptied out. And Allah, exalted, said, making plain the master of the Resurrection:

> ((واذا السماء انفطرت واذا الكواكب انتشرت))

"And when the heaven is cleft asunder, and when the planets are scattered." And He said:

> ((إذا الشمس كورت واذا النجوم انكدرت واذا الجبال سيرت واذا العشار عطلت))

"When the sun is folded up, and when the stars fall, and when the mountains are set moving, and when the pregnant camels are abandoned." All these parables are struck upon the limits and the summonses in their passing away from their ranks — not upon the laws and the ordinances of the works, whose landmarks wear away with the passing of time through the absence of teachers, save what is of necessary existence, as we have mentioned before. That age — in which this act appears through the closing of the door of instruction and the ceasing of the summonses on the part of the master of the Greater Resurrection — is the beginning of the return of knowledge to potency, so that it sinks inward, and the beginning of the increase of ignorance and its rising into act. The appearance of the signs in this manner suffices the one who is sufficient and has come of age. May Allah make us, and the company of the believers, of the people of the divine goods, by following His friends, and gather us with Muhammad and Ali and the pure Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), the lights of His purity, by His grace and His mercy.

The twenty-sixth section of the ninth chapter.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Everyone who says that monotheism can be grasped only through references and works has thereby ruled that the First Intellect, the Soul, the angels, the اَلْجَدّْ, the الْفَتْح, and the Khayal did not know monotheism, since there were no references or works among them or between them.

We say:

The one who says this means neither the First Intellect nor the high limits, but rather the intellects that are in the world of the body — which are the second emanation — since their attaining of perfection was through instruction, obligation, cognition, references, works, and a standing to acquisition. The First Intellect and the transcendent limits that are separate from bodies did not attain their perfection through any of that; rather, they gained their perfections from the very beginning of their existence, by way of origination and emanation. It is not allowable that the judgment of this saying be carried over onto those. As for what the author of al-Nusrah brought forward concerning the meaning of the seven stars, their fixity, their return, and their setting straight — and the six days in which Allah created the heavens and the earth and what is between them, and what he mentioned of the resolve — the author of al-Islah left nothing of the interpretation of that and its meanings unbrought, and in it is sufficiency enough to dispense with a rendering into Arabic in an interpretation that scarcely balances and does not match.

The twenty-seventh section of the ninth chapter.

The author of al-Islah said:

The single line does not make the crosswise mark obligatory except by the appearance of the second line, because the line points to the Speaker and the crosswise mark points to the Sharia, and the form of a thing ought not to appear except after its completion. Just as the crosswise mark appeared after the second line, likewise the Sharia of the first was not completed except by the appearance of the second Speaker; and when the second Speaker appeared, the Sharia of the first was completed and its form appeared in perfection. Likewise the six crosswise marks are completed by the seven lines, for there is no Sharia before the first of the Speakers, and no Sharia at the appearance of the seventh; the six laws belong rather to the Speakers, the seventh of whom is the الْقَائِم. If you draw seven lines, six crosswise marks appear among them upon these likenesses: one, two, three, four, five, six. Were the reckoning based on the saying that the first of the Speakers has no Sharia, then five crosswise marks would have to appear between seven lines. Since six crosswise marks appear from among seven lines, the seven lines stand for the masters of the seven cycles and the six crosswise marks for the six laws, and the Sharia was made obligatory upon Adam.

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Concerning the matter of the six crosswise marks between the seven lines and his inferring from the lines to the Prophets and from the crosswise marks to the six laws — the matter is not as he said. Rather, the seven lines stand for the higher letters, of which each Prophet wrote one single letter; and the six crosswise marks stand for the cycles of the Imams: between Adam and Noah, who are the seven Imams; and between Noah and Abraham in the second cycle; and between Abraham and Moses in the third cycle; and so on up to Muhammad (ع). No further cycle remains for the two after the الْقَائِم save the cycle of his followers and his caliphs who succeed him in the world. So six crosswise marks of five lines fell, in that there occurred between the cycles of the Imams five Speakers who are the masters of the laws, and the likeness of that is this:

Adam — Noah — Abraham — Moses — Jesus — Muhammad — al-Qaim.
the Imams — the Imams — the Imams — the Imams — the Imams — the Imams — the Imams.

Just as, after the الْقَائِم, no Imams occur, so the establishing of the Sharia was not made obligatory upon him. Likewise, since before Adam (ع) no Imams occurred, the establishing of the Sharia was not made obligatory upon him. For if the Sharia, in its establishment, must have Imams before it, it must also have them after it. The foundation-Imams of Noah (ع) preceded the cycle of the foundation-Imams of Adam (ع) and so came after the Imams of his own cycle, and for this reason the establishing of the Sharia was made obligatory upon him; and so too with Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and Muhammad (ع). But the الْقَائِم was preceded by the Imams of the cycle of Muhammad (ع), and no Imams came after him, and for this reason the establishing of the Sharia was not made obligatory upon him. Likewise, Adam had his foundation-Imams come after him, and no other Imams preceded him, and for this reason the establishing of the Sharia was not obligatory upon him. This is what the two of them say.

We say:

The saying of the author of al-Islah agrees with what he has pointed to, save for what he said in the course of his statement: "Just as the crosswise mark appeared after the second line, likewise the Sharia of the first was not completed except by the appearance of the second Speaker." This is an unsound and crooked saying, for the Sharia of Adam is itself its own foundation, and he established its foundation for it. If it were allowable to say that the Sharia of Adam was completed only by the coming of the Speaker who is the master of the second cycle, then it would be equally allowable to say that the Sharia of Muhammad (ع) is not complete and is completed only by the coming of the الْقَائِم, the master of the seventh cycle (ع) — and that is among the impossibilities of speech, for the Sharia is a stature that needs neither increase upon it nor decrease from it. Had the author of al-Islah said that the first cycle, which is Adam's (ع), was not completed except by the appearance of the master of the second cycle, who is Noah (ع), and that likewise no cycle is completed except by the appearance of what follows it — setting in the place of his saying about the Sharia the first cycle itself — it would have been consistent and balanced. And Allah forgives us and him, by His mercy.

As for the author of al-Nusrah — in the matter of the line and the crosswise mark and his interpretation of the six crosswise marks as standing for the Imams — it is a good interpretation, provided the lines stand for the Speakers and not for the higher letters, and the crosswise marks for the span of their affair in the cycle and not for the Imams. Even so, this does not require that Adam have no Sharia; rather, it makes the Sharia obligatory through the existence of the Imams after him, and the existence of the Imams is for the sake of preserving the station of the Speaker, his Sharia, and his ordinances among his community. Were it not for that, obedience to an Imam after a Speaker would not be obligatory. As for the five lines and the six crosswise marks, it is open to the author of al-Islah to interpret them by setting the lines upon the five possessors of firm resolve and the crosswise marks upon the cycles of the masters of the six laws.

The ground for this is that, in interpretation, the soundest reading is the one that comes out identical and balanced when stated, for a reading of that kind holds a nobler place in souls.

In sum, we say: the cycle is of two kinds, a major cycle and a minor cycle. The major cycle belongs to the Speakers, whose place their Imams preserve within the community after them; and the minor cycle belongs to the faithful foundations, who seal the weeks. If the lines are interpreted as the masters of the major cycles — who are the Speakers — then the 'ardas can only concern the span of their governance of the community through the Imams who come after them in a cycle sealed by the coming of another like the first, and the measure of that is known. And if they are interpreted as the masters of the seven minor cycles — who are the Imams that bring each cycle to completion — then the ardas can only concern the span of the Imams' command in each week, together with those who complete them, whose cycle is sealed by their seventh; and their number is known, as is the number of Imams in each cycle, and the number of the appendices according to the existing higher bodies already described. We shall set this out in a book other than this one, if Allah wills.

Chapter Twenty-Nine of Title IX

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Take, for example, the words of Allah, may He be exalted:

> «كَمَا بَدَأَكُمْ تَعُودُونَ»
>
> "As He began you, so you shall return."

That is, as the matter was at its beginning — which was not by way of the realities — so shall the matter be at its end.

We say:

Even granting that this is an interpretation, the fitting way to state it, so that it corresponds properly, is this: that these words of Allah point to what comes about after the الْقَائِم, namely the return of knowledge to potentiality after it has gone out into actuality, and of its abundance to scarcity, so that after him all people become as they were in the time of Adam; and the return of ignorance, after it had been in potentiality and in fewness, to actuality and abundance, by reason of the wisdom decreed within it. And blessed be Allah, the best of creators.

Chapter Thirty of Title IX

The author of al-Nusrah said:

Furthermore, had the author of al-Islah looked at the significance contained in his name — I mean Adam — it would have become plain to him that Adam had no law of his own. For his name is of three letters, alif (أَلِف), dal (دَالّ), and mim (مِيْم), and they are not compounded or joined together; rather each letter of them stands apart from the other, a clear indication that he was not the author of a composed Sharia. As for the five Prophets who came after him, their names are joined and compounded, such as

> نُوحٌ وَإِبْرَاهِيمُ وَمُوسَى وَعِيسَى وَمُحَمَّدٌ

Noah, Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and Muhammad (ع) — which is proof that each of them possessed a Sharia, and the revelation of it was his.

We say:

If the separateness of the letters of Adam's name (ع) entails that he had no law, then the fact that those very letters are themselves not separate entails that he did have one. For the alif of the name is itself composed of alif (أَلِف), lam (لَام), and fa (فَاء); the mim is composed of mim (مِيْم), ya (يَاء), and mim (مِيْم); and the dal (دَالّ) likewise. If separateness does not oblige a law for him, then non-separateness does. In truth, reasoning of this kind is impossible and cannot stand. Were it sound, it would follow necessarily that the author of a Sharia must be the author of composition — and that his name, according to what the Prophet (ع) reported, is Muhammad, which is composed of four letters. And since it is impossible for the الْقَائِم to be the master of a Sharia while his name is compounded, it is void that the separateness of the letters of Adam's name should indicate that he was not an author of a composed Sharia — just as it is void that the joining and compounding of the letters of the name of the الْقَائِم, which is Muhammad, should indicate that he is the author of a Sharia. Once that is void, no weight attaches to reasoning of this kind. The Sharia of Adam is established. And praise be to Allah, Lord of the Worlds.

Chapter Thirty-One of Title IX

The author of al-Islah said:

As for the statement that Noah (ع) was the first to establish his foundation, and the likening of him to the ship, enough has already been said on that in the chapter on the foundations.

We say:

Rather, Noah (ع) was singled out by the ark because he was the first of the possessors of firm resolve — the first to abrogate the earlier laws and compose a new one. His law stood in the place of the ship, and that with which he abrogated the old law stood in the place of the flood; for by it he destroyed the people of the earth, save those who answered his call to his law and boarded his ship, that is, those who established obedience to his foundation. He laid down a law during his lifetime, and when he had perfected it, he committed the matter to his foundation. He commanded him to raise the call upon his law, and commanded the appendices to give ear to him, so that they attained the flowing grace only through him. Whoever obeyed his foundation was inside the ship; whoever opposed him and held fast to the first law was drowned.

Do you not see that he described as drowned by the flood those who worshipped

> وَدًّا، وَسُوَاعًا، وَيَغُوثَ، وَيَعُوقَ، وَنَسْرًا

Wadd, Suwa, Yaghuth, Yauq, and Nasr (نَسْراً)? He said:

> «من خطيئاتهم اغرقوا فادخلوا النار»
>
> "Because of their sins they were drowned and made to enter the Fire."

The author of al-Nusrah said:

If the rank of the ship is like the rank of the apparent law, then whoever holds fast to it among the people of the apparent has been saved, and the true hidden call is nullified. Moreover, what appears from the Book of Allah, may He be exalted, is other than what the author of al-Islah maintained. Allah described the beginning of Noah's affair (ع) — His sending him to his people, his calling them to his law — and then commanded him to make the ark. This account is set forth in Surat Hud. Had the ship been the apparent law, Allah would have commanded him to make the ark first. But the account rests upon Noah having first composed the apparent law and called creation to act upon it, and only then having been commanded to make the ark — that is, to appoint his guardian. The proof of that is the saying of Allah, may He be exalted:

> «وَٱصْنَعِ ٱلْفُلْكَ»
>
> "And make the ark,"

down to His saying:

> «وَكُلَّمَا مَرَّ عَلَيْهِ مَلَأٌۭ مِّن قَوْمِهِۦ سَخِرُوا۟ مِنْهُ»

"And whenever a company of the chiefs of his people passed by him, they mocked him" —

meaning that whenever Noah (ع) set up his guardian, the notables of his people mocked him and marveled at him, because he was the first to institute a guardian; and so they mocked him. This is what he said.

We say:

As for the statement of the author of al-Islah, with his interpreting the ark as the face of the new Sharia: this is because whoever came under its rulings was like one inside a ship, delivered from humiliation, abasement, and every affliction, just as one inside a ship is delivered from drowning. This meaning is admissible. And as for the author of al-Nusrah, with his interpreting the ark as the call and the guardian: this is because whoever entered the guiding call and bound himself to obedience to its foundation was kept safe from drowning in error, in the oneness of Allah, may He be exalted, and in the knowledge of His limits — the guardian standing for him in the rank of a guide. Both interpretations are sound in that respect: one concerns the affairs of this world and the other the affairs of religion, and neither dispenses with the other, for each stands by itself.

Chapter Thirty-Two of Title IX

As for the statement of the author of al-Nusrah, that Noah was the first to set up a guardian by the interpretation that came to him, it is not sound. The prophethood of Adam (ع) and his Sharia are established, and it is proven that Seth (ع) was his guardian. Precedence belongs to the Sharia and its laying down; the guardian and his appointment proceed from that. What reason also indicates is that every one of the Speakers must of necessity have a guardian who takes up deriving benefit from him. This follows because every mover necessarily has something moved: the mover is one, even if many moved things proceed from it. The Speaker, in his age, is the mover of all souls through his call to seek subsistence by establishing the two acts of worship. It necessarily follows that he must have something moved, standing to him in the position of the learner from him — even if many learners learn from him — and the one truly learned from is what we call the guardian. Adam, being a Speaker, must therefore have had a guardian, and likewise every Speaker. Since Adam did indeed appoint a guardian for himself, no belief contrary to that may be held.

Chapter Thirty-Three of Title IX

The author of al-Nusrah said:
